Anderson Hamiltonians with singular potentials
Toyomu Matsuda, Willem van Zuijlen

Abstract
We construct random Schrödinger operators, called Anderson Hamiltonians, with Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions for a fairly general class of singular random potentials on bounded domains. Furthermore, we construct the integrated density of states of these Anderson Hamiltonians, and we relate the Lifschitz tails (the asymptotics of the left tails of the integrated density of states) to the left tails of the principal eigenvalues. Keywords and phrases. Anderson Hamiltonian, regularity structures, integrated density of states. Acknowledgements. We would like to thank Nicolas Perkowski for his support since the beginning of the work. We are also grateful for Kazuhiro Kuwae for a useful discussion. Moreover, we are grateful to the referees for their valuable feedback. TM was supported by the German Science Foundation (DFG) via the IRTG 2544. MSC 2020 . Primary. 60H17, 60H25, 60L40, 82B44. Secondary. 35J10, 35P15.



1
Introduction

In this paper, we consider random Schrödinger operators of the form

-\Delta-\xi,

(1)

where
\Delta=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\partial_{i}^{2}
is the Laplacian on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\xi
is a random potential.
Such operators are also called
Anderson Hamiltonians
.
This name is due to the influential work by Anderson
[
3
]
.
We consider the construction of such operators for irregular potentials
\xi
(also called singular potentials) that do not need to be functions, hence there is –a priori– no obvious interpretation of (
1
).

After constructing the Anderson Hamiltonian it is natural to investigate its spectral properties.
One of the most studied objects in the theory of random Schrödinger operators is the
integrated density of states
(IDS), see for example
[
16
, Chapter VI]
and
[
44
]
for overviews.
The IDS is a nonrandom, increasing and right-continuous function on
\mathbb{R}
and is often characterized as the vague limit
of the normalized eigenvalue counting functions.
The left tail asymptotics of the IDS are called
Lifschitz tails
, which capture disorder effect in the operator (
1
).
Relating the Lifschitz tails to the tail
asymptotics of the principal eigenvalue is a classical result, see for example Kirsch and Martinelli
[
43
]
and
Simon
[
65
]
.

The rest of our introduction is split as follows.
In Section
1.1
we discuss the previous works on the construction of Anderson Hamiltonians with singular potentials and how our construction relates to these works regarding the assumptions and techniques.
In Section
1.2
we discuss the study of the spectral properties of the Anderson Hamiltonians.
In Section
1.3
we discuss the techniques that we use to derive our results.
In Section
1.4
the main results are presented.
In Section
1.5
we give the ideas behind the construction of the operator and how we derive the stochastic terms.
In Section
1.6
we describe the outline of the rest of the paper and in Section
1.7
we give an overview of some notation that is used throughout the paper.

1.1
Construction of Anderson Hamiltonians with singular potentials

The mathematical study of Anderson Hamiltonians with singular potentials dates back to the work

[
27
]
by Fukushima and Nakao.
They constructed the Anderson Hamiltonian with a white noise potential and with Dirichlet boundary conditions
on the one dimensional domain
(-L,L)
,
as the self-adjoint operator associated to the closed symmetric form on
H^{1}_{0}((-L,L))
, (formally) given by

(u,v)\mapsto\int_{(-L,L)}\nabla u\cdot\nabla v-\int_{(-L,L)}\xi uv.

For
\xi
being the white noise one has to make sense of the term
\int_{(-L,L)}\xi uv
. To do so, Fukushima and Nakao replaced it by

\int_{(-L,L)}(uv^{\prime}+vu^{\prime})B,

where
B
is the Brownian motion on
(-L,L)
(as
\xi
is the derivative of
B
, this is an integration by parts identity).
In general,
for a bounded open set
U
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a potential
V
of regularity greater than
-1
,
it is possible to make sense of

\int_{U}Vuv

for
u,v\in H^{1}_{0}(U)
(we show this in Theorem
4.3

(a)
).
Therefore, in that case, one
can construct the Anderson Hamiltonian by considering the associated symmetric form.

However, this approach fails to work if the regularity of
\xi
is below
-1
.
The treatment of such singular
\xi
became possible only after
the advent of the theory on
singular stochastic partial differential equations

(singular SPDEs), most notably the theory of

regularity structures

by Hairer
[
33
]

and the theory of

paracontrolled distributions
by Gubinelli, Imkeller and Perkowski
[
32
]
.

Motivated by the theory of paracontrolled distributions, Allez and Chouk
[
2
]
constructed the Anderson Hamiltonian with white noise on the 2D torus as the limit of

-\Delta-\xi_{\varepsilon}+c_{\varepsilon},

where
\xi_{\varepsilon}
is a regularized potential and
c_{\varepsilon}
is a suitably chosen number such that
c_{\varepsilon}\uparrow\infty
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.
They obtained an explicit domain of the operator and its action.
Subsequently, Gubinelli, Ugurcan and Zachhuber
[
30
]
constructed
the Anderson Hamiltonian with white noise on the 2D and 3D torus
and studied SPDEs whose linear part is given by the Anderson Hamiltonian (
1
).
Chouk and van Zuijlen
[
18
]
constructed the Anderson Hamiltonian with white noise and with either Dirichlet or Neumann boundary conditions on
2D boxes.
Mouzard
[
57
]
constructed the Anderson Hamiltonian with white noise on 2D compact manifolds, using the theory of higher order paracontrolled distributions
[
8
]
.
This paper can also be viewed as a generalisation of
[
2
]
.
Additionally, he proved a Weyl law for the Anderson Hamiltonian. We also prove such a Weyl law in Proposition
5.17
.
Ugurcan
[
72
]
constructed the Anderson Hamiltonian on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
using the methods of paracontrolled distributions.

The works
[
18
,
30
,
57
,
72
]

mentioned above use the techniques of the theory of paracontrolled distributions
[
32
]
.
Labbé
[
48
]
used the theory of
regularity structures to construct the Anderson Hamiltonian with white noise
on a
d
-dimensional box (
d\leq 3
) with Dirichlet or periodic boundary conditions. Instead of directly constructing the operator itself,
he constructed the resolvent operators
G_{a}=(a-\Delta-\xi)^{-1}
with Dirichlet boundary conditions for large
a>0
and defined the Anderson Hamiltonian
by
G_{a}^{-1}-a
.
Although this approach is robust,
the construction is abstract and the domain of the operator is implicit.

In this work we consider a fairly general class of irregular potentials under the minimal assumption on the regularity of the potential
\xi
, which means that we assume that the regularity of
\xi
is
-2+\delta
for some
\delta>0
.
Typical examples of potentials that are within this regularity regime include the
white noise
, namely the centered Gaussian field with delta correlation,
in
d
-dimensions with
d\in\{1,2,3\}
. However we can go beyound white noise, as we can treat a Gaussian noise
\xi
whose covariance is formally given by

\mathbb{E}[\xi(x)\xi(y)]=c\lvert x-y\rvert^{-\alpha},\quad c\in(0,\infty),\,\,\alpha\in(0,\min\{d,4\}).

Moreover, instead of working on a box, we consider a bounded domain
U
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and construct the Anderson Hamiltonian on
U
with both Dirichlet as well as Neumann boundary conditions. For the latter, besides that the domain needs also to be Lipschitz, we have to impose more restrictive assumptions on the potential.
For example, these assumptions do not allow us to construct the Anderson Hamiltonian with Neumann boundary conditions for a white noise potential on a three dimensional domain. In order to construct this operator one expects – due to the work of Hairer and Gerencsér
[
28
]
for the parabolic Anderson model – the need to perform an additional renormalisation, but then only on the boundary.

1.2
Spectral properties of Anderson Hamiltonians

Fukushima and Nakao
[
27
]
studied the integrated density of states (IDS) for the Anderson Hamiltonian with white noise potential in one dimension
and derived the explicit formula that was predicted by physicists.
The IDS for the Anderson Hamiltonian with white noise potential on two dimensional boxes was constructed by Matsuda in
[
52
]
.

Besides the study of the IDS, quite related are the studies of the asymptotics of the eigenvalues.
Chouk and van Zuijlen
[
18
]
showed the asymptotics of the eigenvalues in two dimensions for a white noise potential and Labbé and Hsu
[
39
]
extended this to three dimensions.
The asymptotics of the principal eigenvalues plays an important role in the mass asymptotics of the parabolic Anderson model

[
45
,
46
,
29
]
.
Most recently, Bailleul, Dang and Mouzard
[
5
]

studied different properties of the Anderson Hamiltonian and its spectrum, for example the corresponding heat kernel and heat kernel estimates are studied, estimates of the norms of the eigenfunctions in terms of the size of their corresponding eigenvalues are given and a lower estimate on the spectral gap is given.

We remark that in one dimension with white noise, beyond the asymptotics of the eigenvalues and the study of the IDS, more is known about the spectrum properties.

Namely, McKean
[
53
]
showed that appropriately shifted and rescaled principal eigenvalues converge,
as the segment size grows to infinity, to the Gumbel distribution in law.
Cambronero and McKean
[
13
]
and Cambronero, Ramírez and Rider
[
14
]
derived
precise tail asymptotics of the principal eigenvalue on the fixed torus.
Dumaz and Labbé investigated the detailed statistics of the eigenvalues and the eigenfunctions in a series of works

[
24
,
25
,
23
,
22
]
.
No analogous results are known for singular potentials other than the white noise in one dimension (see the conjectures in the introduction of
[
39
]
).

In this work we construct
the IDS of the Anderson Hamiltonian with a singular potential
and we relate its left tail to those of the principal eigenvalues. In particular, by applying the
work
[
39
]
by Hsu and Labbé, we derive the precise tail behaviour of the IDS for the white noise in

d
dimensions, for
d\in\{2,3\}
.

1.3
Techniques

The techniques to construct the operators.

Instead of directly constructing the operators themselves, we construct the corresponding symmetric forms.
In fact, we are inspired by Gubinelli, Ugurcan and Zachhuber
[
30
]
, where
they figured out that the form domain of the Anderson Hamiltonian for a white noise potential on the 2D or 3D torus is quite simple.
The work
[
47
]
by Kuwae and Shioya is important for us as it provides a correct notion of convergence of symmetric forms that are bounded from below.

To construct the symmetric forms, we combine an exponential transformation with an integration by parts formulae, see Section
1.5
for a heuristic description.
The exponential transformation is now a well-known technique in singular SPDEs. The most notable one is the Cole-Hopf transform of the
KPZ equation as used by Bertini and Giacomin
[
9
]
. Hairer and Labbé
[
34
]
used
the exponential transformation to simplify the 2D parabolic Anderson model.
Gubinelli, Ugurcan and Zachhuber
[
30
]

used it to construct the Anderson Hamiltonian with
3D white noise.
Recently, Jagannath and Perkowski
[
41
]

applied it to simplify the construction of the dynamical
\Phi^{4}_{3}
model
and Zachhuber
[
73
]
applied it to prove global well-posedness of multiplicative stochastic wave equations.
It is interesting to note that, unlike previous works, we can apply the trick of exponential transformation
for the entire subcritical regime.
A major drawback of the exponential transformation is the lack of robustness. For instance, it does not work
if we replace the Laplacian with a fractional Laplacian.

The techniques to treat the IDS.

There are two standard approaches to construct the IDS:
the path integral approach
[
16
, Section VI.1.2]
and the functional analytic approach

[
16
, Section VI.1.3]
. In our framework, we cannot use the path integral approach.
Indeed, it was shown in
[
52
]
that the 2D white noise is critical for this approach in that
the Laplace transform of the IDS is finite only for small parameters. Therefore, if the regularity of the potential

\xi
is lower than that of the 2D white noise, we expect the blow-up of the Laplace transform of the IDS for any parameter.
Hence, in this paper we adopt the functional analytic approach.
This approach, introduced by Kirsch and Martinelli
[
43
]
, is based on the
super-(sub-)additivity of the Dirichlet (Neumann) eigenvalue counting functions and the ergodic theorem
by Akcoglu and Krengel
[
1
]
.

In order to apply this approach only under the assumptions that guarantee the existence of the Anderson Hamiltonian with Dirichlet boundary conditions, i.e., without assuming the additional Neumann assumption
3.2

(II)
needed for the Anderson Hamiltonian with Neumann boundary conditions, we are introducing an –what we call– artificial Neumann Anderson Hamiltonian (see Definition
5.9
).
For the construction of this artificial operator, we rely on a rather explicit representation of the symmetric form associated to the Anderson Hamiltonian.

Many technical estimates here are inspired by
Doi, Iwatsuka and Mine
[
21
]
.

1.4
Main results

In this section we state our three main results of the paper. These results do not require the introduction of technical notions, however their assumptions do.
We therefore briefly give an idea about what kind of assumptions we assume below in Remark
1.3
and postpone the detailed description of the assumptions to Section
3
, in which we also discuss in which cases these conditions are fulfilled.
In Section
2
we introduce the necessary notation and recall a few definitions.
In Section
1.5
we discuss the heuristic idea behind the construction of the operator and the idea on how to proof that these assumptions are fulfilled in general.

Assumption 1.1
.

We fix the dimension parameter
d\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{1\}
.
We let
\Omega\vcentcolon=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\mathbb{P}
a probability measure on the Borel-
\sigma
-algebra on
\Omega
.
We define the random variable
\xi
with values in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by
\xi(\omega)\vcentcolon=\omega
for
\omega\in\Omega
.
There exists a
\delta\in(0,1)
such that for all
\sigma\in(0,\infty)
one has
\mathbb{P}(\xi\in\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))=1
, where
\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a weighted Besov-Hölder space, see Definition
2.2
.
A smooth, symmetric function
\rho\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\int\rho=1
is given and we set

\rho_{\varepsilon}(x)\vcentcolon=\varepsilon^{-d}\rho(\varepsilon^{-1}x)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\xi_{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=\rho_{\varepsilon}\ast\xi,\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\varepsilon\in(0,\infty).

(2)

Remark 1.2
.

We do not allow
d
to be equal to one, since we will work with Green’s functions and for
d=1
the Green function is not singular and one would need a different treatment.

However, the interesting potentials for
d=1
are given by the derivative of fractional Brownian motions, whose regularity is
greater than
-1
. Hence, they can be easily treated in the classical framework of symmetric forms, see Theorem
4.3

(a)
.

Remark 1.3

(The main assumptions)
.

Beside the above Assumption
1.1
, we introduce three other assumptions in Assumptions
3.2
, namely what we call the

•

Construction assumption
, Assumption
3.2

(I)
. This imposes the existence of renormalisation constants
(c_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
in
\mathbb{R}
under which (renormalised) stochastic objects converge.

•

Neumann assumption
, Assumption
3.2

(II)
. This imposes certain convergence of the stochastic terms considering the boundary of the domain.

•

Ergodic assumption
, Assumption
3.2

(III)
. This imposes ergodicity on the noise.

Now we state our three main results of the paper.
By “domain” we mean a nonempty open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(remember that we assume
d\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{1\}
).

Definition 1.4
.

We impose the construction assumption
3.2

(I)
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
.

(a)

For a bounded domain
U
we define
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}
to be the self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(U)
,

\displaystyle-\Delta-\xi_{\varepsilon}+c_{\varepsilon}

(3)

with Dirichlet boundary conditions.

(b)

For a bounded Lipschitz domain
U
we define
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}
to be the self-adjoint operator (
3
) on
L^{2}(U)
with Neumann boundary conditions.

Remark 1.5
.

Actually, in Section
5
we first define the operators
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}
as those that correspond to symmetric forms given in terms of the stochastic terms that we describe in Section
3
.
Then we show that these equal (
3
).

Definition 1.6
.

[
60
, Definition p. 284]

Let
A,A_{1},A_{2},\dots
be self-adjoint operators on a Banach space
\mathfrak{X}
. We say that the sequence
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges in

norm resolvent sense
and write

\displaystyle A_{n}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{NR}}_{n\rightarrow\infty}A

if

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lVert(\mathrm{i}+A_{n})^{-1}-(\mathrm{i}+A)^{-1}\rVert_{\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{X}}=0.

A sequence converges in norm resolvent sense if and only if the above convergence holds with “
\mathrm{i}
” replaced by “
\lambda
” for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
, see
[
60
, Theorem VIII.19]
.

The first result concerns the construction of Anderson Hamiltonians
on bounded domains, with Dirichlet boundary conditions.

Theorem 1.7

(Theorem
5.3
)
.

Assume the construction assumption
3.2

(I)
.
Let
U
be a bounded domain.
There exists a self-adjoint operator
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}
on
L^{2}(U)
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{NR}}_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}\mbox{ in probability}.

Furthermore, each of the operators has a countable spectrum of eigenvalues and the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}
converge in probability to those of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}
.
Moreover, there exist choices of eigenfunctions of
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}
such that one also has convergence of these eigenfunctions in probability.

The limit
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}
is independent of the mollifier
\rho
.

The second main result concerns Anderson Hamiltonians on bounded Lipschitz domains with Neumann boundary conditions.

Theorem 1.8

(Theorem
5.8
)
.

We impose the construction and Neumann assumption
3.2

(I)
and
(II)
.
Let
U
be a bounded Lipschitz domain. There exists a self-adjoint operator
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}
on
L^{2}(U)
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{NR}}_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}\mbox{ in probability}.

Furthermore, each of the operators has a countable spectrum of eigenvalues and the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}
converge in probability to those of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}
.
Moreover, there exist choices of eigenfunctions of
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}
such that one also has convergence of these eigenfunctions in probability.

The limit
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}
is independent of the mollifier
\rho
.

Remark 1.9
.

The statement of Theorem
5.3
is actually slightly more general than that of Theorem
1.7
. Convergence in probability implies that there exists a subsequence and a set
\Omega_{1}\subseteq\Omega
of probability one such that the subsequence converges everywhere on
\Omega_{1}
. For the convergence of the Dirichlet operators, this set
\Omega_{1}
can be chosen independently from the choice of bounded domain
U
.

The last main result concerns the integrated density of states (IDS) of Anderson Hamiltonians. For example, we show that the notion of the IDS for Anderson Hamiltonians with smooth potentials can be extended to irregular potentials.

For a bounded domain
U
and
L\in[1,\infty)
we write
|U|
for the Lebesgue measure of
U
and

\displaystyle U_{L}\vcentcolon=LU=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}L^{-1}x\in U\}.

We recall that for the Anderson Hamiltonian with a smooth ergodic potential
V
the integrated density of states
\bm{N}_{V}
is given by the right-continuous and increasing function
\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow-\infty}\bm{N}_{V}(\lambda)=0
for which, with
(\lambda_{k}(U,V))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
being the eigenvalues of
-\Delta-V
with Dirichlet boundary conditions on
U
(counting multiplicities), for any bounded domain
U
and continuity point
\lambda
of
\bm{N}_{V}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{1}{\lvert U_{L}\rvert}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\lambda_{k}(U_{L},V)\leq\lambda\}}=\bm{N}_{V}(\lambda).

Theorem 1.10

(Theorem
5.26
, Theorem
5.29
and Theorem
5.30
)
.

We impose the construction and ergodic assumption
3.2

(I)
and
(III)
.
There exists a (deterministic) right-continuous and increasing function
\bm{N}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
with

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\to-\infty}\bm{N}(\lambda)=0,

such that the following holds:

(a)

For
(\lambda_{k}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}(U))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
being the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}
as in Theorem
1.7
(counting multiplicities), almost surely, one has for every bounded domain
U
and every continuity point
\lambda
of
\bm{N}

\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{1}{\lvert U_{L}\rvert}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\lambda_{k}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}(U_{L})\leq\lambda\}}=\bm{N}(\lambda).

(
\bm{N}
is called the integrated density of states of the Anderson Hamiltonian with potential
\xi
.)

(b)

Let
\bm{N}_{\varepsilon}
be the integrated density of states of the Anderson Hamiltonian with potential
\xi_{\varepsilon}-c_{\varepsilon}
, where
c_{\varepsilon}
is
the renormalization constant from Assumption
3.2

(I)
.

Then,
\bm{N}_{\varepsilon}
converges vaguely to
\bm{N}
(see Definition
5.21
).

(c)

One has
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda^{-\frac{d}{2}}\bm{N}(\lambda)=\frac{\lvert B(0,1)\rvert}{(2\pi)^{d}}
.

(d)

For any bounded domain
U
and

\alpha\in(0,\infty)
, the following identities hold in
[-\infty,0]
:

\displaystyle\limsup_{\lambda\to-\infty}(-\lambda)^{-\alpha}\log\bm{N}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\limsup_{\lambda\to-\infty}(-\lambda)^{-\alpha}\log\mathbb{P}(\lambda_{1}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}(U)\leq\lambda),

\displaystyle\liminf_{\lambda\to-\infty}(-\lambda)^{-\alpha}\log\bm{N}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\liminf_{\lambda\to-\infty}(-\lambda)^{-\alpha}\log\mathbb{P}(\lambda_{1}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}(U)\leq\lambda).

(e)

Impose furthermore the Neumann assumption, Assumption
3.2

(II)
. For
(\lambda_{k}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}(U))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
being the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}
as in Theorem
1.8
,
for every bounded Lipschitz domain
U
and every continuity point
\lambda
of
\bm{N}
,

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\to\infty\\
L\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lvert U_{L}\rvert}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\lambda_{k}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}(U_{L})\leq\lambda\}}=\bm{N}(\lambda),\quad\text{almost surely}.

With the above theorem in combination with the tail asymptotics of the principal eigenvalue proven in
[
39
, Theorem 2]
we obtain the precise tail behaviour of the IDS for the Anderson Hamiltonian with white noise potential in
d
dimensions.

Corollary 1.11
.

Let
d\in\{2,3\}
and
\xi
be the
d
-dimensional white noise. Then,

\lim_{\lambda\to-\infty}(-\lambda)^{-\frac{4-d}{2}}\log\bm{N}(\lambda)=-\frac{8}{d^{d/2}(4-d)^{2-d/2}}\kappa_{d}^{-4},

where
\kappa_{d}
is the best constant of the Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality

\lVert f\rVert_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{d/4}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{1-d/4}.

Proof.

This follows from
Theorem
1.10
(d) (see Theorem
5.30
) and
[
39
, Theorem 2]
.
∎

Remark 1.12
.

The case
d=1
is of course known, see
[
27
]
.
The case
d=2
was proved in
[
52
]
.
In the physics literature, these tail behaviours have been already expected (e.g.,
[
15
,
11
]
).

1.5
Ideas behind the construction and deriving the stochastic terms

In this section we give the general ideas behind the construction of the Anderson Hamiltonians as in Theorem
1.7
and Theorem
1.8
.
As is common practice when dealing with singular SPDEs, we transform and decompose terms and give a meaning to the appearing stochastic terms by means of a renormalization procedure.
The heuristics behind how to derive these stochastic terms is described in Remark
1.16
.

As mentioned before, we construct the Anderson Hamiltonian by constructing the corresponding symmetric form (for a definition see Definition
4.2
). For a smooth potential
\zeta
, the Anderson Hamiltonian
-\Delta+\zeta
on a bounded domain
U
with Dirichlet boundary conditions corresponds to the symmetric form

\displaystyle(u,v)\mapsto\int_{U}\nabla u\cdot\nabla v+\int_{U}\zeta uv,

(4)

on the Sobolev space
H_{0}^{1}(U)
, i.e.,
u,v\in H_{0}^{1}(U)
.
Whereas, if we consider instead Neumann boundary conditions the operator corresponds to (
4
) on the Sobolev space
H^{1}(U)
.
For a discussion on this, we refer to
[
19
, Section 6 and 7]
.

Now we want to give a meaning to the symmetric form (
4
), basically with “
\zeta
” replaced by “
\xi
”. By the bilinearity of symmetric forms, it suffices to give a meaning on the diagonal, i.e.,
we may consider
u=v
in the above formula (see also the comment below Definition
4.2
).
Now we will transform the above formula, in a type of “partial Cole–Hopf” transform, by imposing that
u
is the product of
e^{w}
and another function, for some well-chosen function
w
. We present this in Lemma
1.15
after introducing some convenient notation in Definition
1.13
and recalling Green’s formula in Lemma
1.14
. For the definition of a Lipschitz domain
U
and
C^{\infty}(\overline{U})
, see Definition
2.3
.

Definition 1.13
.

Let
U
be a bounded Lipschitz domain.
Let
\nu:\partial U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
be such that
\nu
is the outer unit normal on
\partial U
almost everywhere.
Let
S
the
(d-1)
-dimensional Lebesgue measure on
\partial U
.
Let
g\in C^{1}(\overline{U})
.
For
x\in\partial U
we define
\nabla_{\nu}g(x)\vcentcolon=\nabla g(x)\cdot\nu(x)
.

Let
f
be a measurable function on
\partial U
such that
f\nabla_{\nu}g
is integrable on
\partial U
.
We write

\int_{\partial U}f\nabla g\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\bm{S}\vcentcolon=\int_{\partial U}f\nabla_{\nu}g\,\mathrm{d}S.

Lemma 1.14

(
[
26
, Theorem 4.6]
)
.

•

Let
U
be a bounded domain. Then, for every

f\in H^{1}(U)

and
g\in C^{2}_{c}(U)
,

\int_{U}\nabla f\cdot\nabla g=-\int_{U}f\Delta g.

•

Let
U
be a bounded Lipschitz domain. Then, for every

f\in H^{1}(U)
and
g\in C^{2}(\overline{U})
,

\int_{U}\nabla f\cdot\nabla g=-\int_{U}f\Delta g+\int_{\partial U}\mathcal{T}(f)\nabla g\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\bm{S},

(5)

where
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{H^{1}(U)}
is the trace operator (see Lemma
2.8

(b)
).

Lemma 1.15
.

Let
U
be a bounded Lipschitz domain,
\zeta,w\in C^{\infty}(\overline{U})
and
u\in H^{1}(\overline{U})
.
Set
v\vcentcolon=e^{-w}u
. Then, one has

\int_{U}(\lvert\nabla u\rvert^{2}-\zeta u^{2})=\int_{U}e^{2w}\lvert\nabla v\rvert^{2}-\int_{U}e^{2w}(\zeta+\lvert\nabla w\rvert^{2}+\Delta w)v^{2}+\int_{\partial U}\mathcal{T}(v^{2})e^{2w}\nabla w\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\bm{S}.

Proof.

One has

\int_{U}e^{2w}\lvert\nabla v\rvert^{2}=\int_{U}e^{2w}\lvert\nabla(e^{-w}u)\rvert^{2}=\int_{U}\lvert\nabla w\rvert^{2}u^{2}+\int_{U}\lvert\nabla u\rvert^{2}-\int_{U}\nabla w\cdot\nabla(u^{2})

and by integration by parts (Lemma
1.14
) one has

\int_{U}\nabla w\cdot\nabla(u^{2})=\int_{\partial U}\mathcal{T}(u^{2})\nabla w\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\bm{S}-\int_{U}(\Delta w)u^{2}.\qed

By taking
\zeta=\xi_{\varepsilon}-c_{\varepsilon}
for some
c_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{R}
and a smooth enough
w^{\varepsilon}

and by writing

\displaystyle y^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=-e^{2w^{\varepsilon}}(\xi_{\varepsilon}-c_{\varepsilon}+|\nabla w^{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\Delta w^{\varepsilon}),

(6)

\displaystyle u^{\flat}

\displaystyle=e^{-w^{\varepsilon}}u,

the Anderson Hamiltonian with potential
\xi_{\varepsilon}-c_{\varepsilon}
, namely,

\displaystyle-\Delta-\xi_{\varepsilon}+c_{\varepsilon},

(7)

on
U
with Dirichlet boundary conditions, corresponds to
the symmetric form on
H_{0}^{1}(U)
given by,

\displaystyle\int_{U}|\nabla u|^{2}-(\xi_{\varepsilon}-c_{\varepsilon})u^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{U}e^{2w^{\varepsilon}}|\nabla u^{\flat}|^{2}+\int_{U}y^{\varepsilon}(u^{\flat})^{2}.

Similarly, if we instead consider Neumann boundary conditions, it corresponds to the symmetric form on
H^{1}(U)
given by,

\displaystyle\int_{U}|\nabla u|^{2}-(\xi_{\varepsilon}-c_{\varepsilon})u^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{U}e^{2w^{\varepsilon}}|\nabla u^{\flat}|^{2}+\int_{U}y^{\varepsilon}(u^{\flat})^{2}+\int_{\partial U}\mathcal{T}((u^{\flat})^{2})e^{2w^{\varepsilon}}\nabla w^{\varepsilon}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{S}.

As
w^{\varepsilon}
will be chosen smooth enough such that
H_{0}^{1}(U)=e^{w^{\varepsilon}}H_{0}^{1}(U)
and
H^{1}(U)=e^{w^{\varepsilon}}H^{1}(U)
, this means that we have to give a meaning to the limit of two, in the case of Dirichlet boundary conditions, and three, in the case of Neumann boundary conditions, symmetric forms, namely

\displaystyle v\mapsto\int_{U}e^{2w^{\varepsilon}}|\nabla v|^{2},\qquad v\mapsto\int_{U}y^{\varepsilon}v^{2},\qquad v\mapsto\int_{\partial U}\mathcal{T}(v^{2})e^{2w^{\varepsilon}}\nabla w^{\varepsilon}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{S}.

(8)

As we will show in more detail in Section
4
, if there are
w
and
y
such that
w^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow w
in
\mathcal{C}^{\delta}
and
y^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow y
in
\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta}
, then the first two symmetric forms in (
8
) converge to the corresponding symmetric forms with
w
and
y
in place of
w^{\varepsilon}
and
y^{\varepsilon}
, respectively.
This basically reflects the first assumption that we impose and call the
construction assumption
: Assumption
3.2

(I)
.
As
\mathcal{T}(v^{2})
is in
L^{1}(\partial U)
for
v\in H^{1}(U)
, one would expect the third symmetric form to converge only if
\delta>1
, as then
\nabla w^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\nabla w
in
\mathcal{C}^{\delta-1}
.
However, with an additional assumption on a stochastic term, which we call the
Neumann assumption
namely Assumption
3.2

(II)
, it suffices to assume that
\delta>\frac{1}{2}
.
For this we use a further decomposition of the third symmetric form in (
8
).
We discuss this in more detail in Section
3.3
.

So as mentioned, the construction and Neumann assumptions impose conditions on the stochastic terms.
Given a noise
\xi
there is a way on how to find these stochastic terms. We describe the idea in the following remark.

Remark 1.16

(Idea behind the derivation of the stochastic term
w^{\varepsilon}
)
.

Let us present the heuristic idea on how to choose these
w^{\varepsilon}
, by forgetting for the moment about the regularization parameter “
\varepsilon
” and the renormalisation constants “
c_{\varepsilon}
”.
Namely,
we are going to construct a
w
such that

\displaystyle\xi+\lvert\nabla w\rvert^{2}+\Delta w

is sufficiently regular.
For convenience of conversation, let us introduce a formal notion “
\deg(\sigma)
” which more or less reflects the regularity of an object “
\sigma
”, namely, we set (
\deg
coincides with
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{+}
as in Definition
B.6
)

\displaystyle\deg(\xi)=-2+\delta,

\displaystyle\deg(\partial_{i}\sigma)=\deg(\sigma)-1,

\displaystyle\deg((-\Delta)^{-1}\sigma)=\deg(\sigma)+2,

\displaystyle\deg(\sigma_{1}\cdot\sigma_{2})=\deg(\sigma_{1})+\deg(\sigma_{2}).

Now we expect to be able to choose
w
with positive regularity, so that the term
|\nabla w|^{2}
has a larger regularity than
\Delta w
, i.e.,
\deg(|\nabla w|^{2})>\deg(\Delta w)
).
Hence, by ignoring
|\nabla w|^{2}
we try to find a
w
such that
\Delta w
compensates the irregularity of
\xi
.
The most natural choice for this is

w=(-\Delta)^{-1}\xi
. In this case,

\xi+\lvert\nabla w\rvert^{2}+\Delta w=\lvert\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\xi\rvert^{2}=:\tau_{1}.

Observe that
\deg(\tau_{1})=-2+2\delta
, which is greater than
\deg(\xi)=-2+\delta
.
If the degree
-2+2\delta
is too small to our taste, then we instead set

w\vcentcolon=(-\Delta)^{-1}(\xi+\tau_{1}).

For this
w
we obtain

\xi+\lvert\nabla w\rvert^{2}+\Delta w=\underbrace{2\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\xi\cdot\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\tau_{1}}_{\tau_{2}}+\underbrace{\lvert\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\tau_{1}\rvert^{2}}_{\tau_{3}},

where
\deg(\tau_{2})=-2+3\delta
and
\deg(\tau_{3})=-2+4\delta
are both greater than
-2+2\delta
.
One can repeat this argument until
one obtains a sum of terms for
\xi+\lvert\nabla w\rvert^{2}+\Delta w
such that
each term has sufficiently large degree.
(As Theorem
4.3

(a)
shows, “sufficiently large” means that the degree is greater than
-1
.)

The above arguments are not yet mathematically rigorous, as for instance, the term
\lvert\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\xi\rvert^{2}
, that is the inner product of
\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\xi
with itself, a priori does not make sense since
\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\xi
is not a function in general.
Moreover, it turns out that

\lvert\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\xi_{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}
itself does not converge as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
,
but if we take a “renormalization” of it, namely

\lvert\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\xi_{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\lvert\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\xi_{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}(0)],

then it does converge in probability.
Then, we take the limit of it as our definition of
\tau_{1}
(instead of the nonrigorous definition
\lvert\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\xi\rvert^{2}
above).

We will discuss the derivation of the stochastic terms in more detail in Section
3
.
On the one hand we consider the concrete examples of two and three dimensional white noise, in which case it suffices to take the first and second choice of
w
as described above, respectively. On the other hand, we mention how to deal with general noises of regularity greater than
-2
with the use of the theory of regularity structures.

1.6
Outline

In Section
2
, we introduce some notation related to the function spaces that we use.
Technical estimates related to the objects introduced in Section
2
are postponed to Appendix
A
.
In Section
3
, we describe the main assumptions and give examples for which these assumptions are satisfied.
In Section
4
, we cover some theory on (deterministic) symmetric forms that will be relevant to our problems.
In Section
5
, we give the definition of the Anderson Hamiltonians and prove the main theorems on the construction and the IDS.
In Appendix
B
we review some necessary terminalogies from regularity structures in order to prove in Appendix
C
that under general conditions the construction assumptions are satisfied for subcritical noises.

1.7
Notation

We set
\mathbb{N}\vcentcolon=\{1,2,3,\ldots\}
and
\mathbb{N}_{0}\vcentcolon=\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
.
We call a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
a
domain
if it is
an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We denote by
\overline{U}
the closure of a subset
U
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Given a subset
U
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
L\in(0,\infty)
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we set

\displaystyle U_{L}\vcentcolon=LU=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}L^{-1}y\in U\},

d(x,U)\vcentcolon=\inf\{\lvert x-y\rvert\ \ \nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}y\in U\}
,

B(U,R)\vcentcolon=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}d(y,U)\leq R\}
and
B(x,R)\vcentcolon=B(\{x\},R)
.
We denote by
\lvert U\rvert
the Lebesgue measure of a measurable set
U
.

We denote by
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the space of Schwartz functions equipped with the locally convex topology generated by the Schwartz seminorms, and, by
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the space of tempered distributions,
that is, the dual space of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
We denote by
\operatorname{supp}(f)
the support of a distribution or a continuous function
f
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. For a domain
U
, we write
C^{k}(U)
for the
k
times continuously differentiable functions on
U
and
C_{c}^{k}(U)
for those functions in
C^{k}(U)
with compact support. For a closed set
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
(we will consider
\overline{U}
and
\partial U
for domains
U
), we define

\displaystyle C^{k}(V)\vcentcolon=\{f|_{V}:f\in C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\}.

For a subset
U
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, either open or closed, we define

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{C^{k}(U)}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\sup_{x\in U}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}:\lvert l\rvert\leq k}\lvert\partial^{k}f(x)\rvert\quad\text{if }k<\infty

We denote by
L^{p}(U)
,
p\in[1,\infty]
, the usual Lebesgue
L^{p}
-space on
U
.
We denote by
\langle F,f\rangle
the dual pairing of
F\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and the dual pairing of
Besov spaces
[
63
, Theorem 2.17]
.
We denote by
f\ast g
the convolution of
f
and
g
.
By duality, the convolution
f\ast g
for
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
g\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined and represents a smooth function.

Let
A,X
be sets and
f,g:A\times X\rightarrow[0,\infty]
.
We write
f(a,x)\lesssim_{a}g(a,x)
if there exists a constant
C\in(0,\infty]
(possibly) depending on
a
–for which we also write either
C=C(a)
or
C=C_{a}
– such that
f(a,x)\leq Cg(a,x)
for all
x
.
We will not explicitly write
the dependence on the dimension
d
, i.e., we write “
\lesssim_{a}
” instead of “
\lesssim_{d,a}
”.

2
Function spaces and Green’s functions

2.1
Besov spaces on
\mathbb{R}^{d}

Here we describe definitions and important properties of Besov spaces on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Technical estimates related to Besov spaces will be given in Section
A.1
.

Definition 2.1
.

The
Fourier transform
of a function
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined by

\mathcal{F}f(y)\vcentcolon=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)e^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}x\cdot y}\,\mathrm{d}x.

We define
\mathcal{F}f
for
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by duality:

\langle\mathcal{F}f,g\rangle\vcentcolon=\langle f,\mathcal{F}g\rangle
for
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\check{\chi}
,
\chi
be
smooth radial functions with values in
[0,1]
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the following properties:

•

\operatorname{supp}(\check{\chi})\subseteq B(0,\frac{4}{3})
,
\operatorname{supp}(\chi)\subseteq\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\frac{3}{4}\leq\lvert x\rvert\leq\frac{8}{3}\}
.

•

\check{\chi}(x)+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\chi(2^{-j}x)=1
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

and
\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}\chi(2^{-j}x)=1
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
.

The existence of such
\check{\chi}
and
\chi
is guaranteed by
[
4
, Proposition 2.10]
.
For
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we set

\displaystyle\Delta_{-1}f=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\check{\chi}\mathcal{F}f),\qquad\Delta_{j}f=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\chi(2^{-j}\cdot)\mathcal{F}f),\qquad j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Let
p,q\in[1,\infty]
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
.
For
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, we set

\displaystyle w_{\sigma}(x)\vcentcolon=(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2})^{-\frac{\sigma}{2}}.

The weighted nonhomogeneous Besov space
B_{p,q}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
consists of those distributions
f
in

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\lVert f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty
, where

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\vcentcolon=\Big\|\Big(2^{-rj}\lVert w_{\sigma}\Delta_{j}f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big)_{j=-1}^{\infty}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}}

Let us mention that the norm actually depends on the choice of
\check{\chi}
and
\chi
, though the space does not. See for example
[
4
, Corollary 2.70]
.
[
4
, Lemma 2.69]
implies that different choices of
\check{\chi}
and
\chi
as above give equivalent norms.

We set
\mathcal{C}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\vcentcolon=B^{r,\sigma}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and write
\mathcal{C}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\vcentcolon=\mathcal{C}^{r,0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
B_{p,q}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=B_{p,q}^{r,0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

2.2
Sobolev–Slobodeckij spaces on bounded domains

Definition 2.3
.

We say that a bounded domain
U
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is called a
bounded Lipschitz domain
if its boundary can be
locally approximated by Lipschitz functions in the following sense:
For each
y\in\partial U
, there exist
r>0
, a Lipschitz function
\gamma:\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and an bijection (a relabelling)
\sigma:\{1,\dots,d\}\rightarrow\{1,\dots,d\}
such that

\displaystyle U\cap B(y,r)=\{x\in B(y,r):x_{\sigma{(d)}}>\gamma(x_{\sigma(1)},\dots,x_{\sigma(d)})\}.

(9)

A function
f:\partial U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is called smooth if for each
y\in\partial U
, additional to the
r
,
\gamma
and
\sigma
as above such that (
9
) holds, there exists a smooth function
g:\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
f(x)=g(x_{\sigma(1)},\dots,x_{\sigma(d-1)})
for
x\in\partial U\cap B(y,r)
.

C^{\infty}(\partial U)
is the space of all smooth functions on
\partial U
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
U
be a domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
p\in[1,\infty]
and
r\geq 0
.

(a)

The space
W^{r}_{p}(U)
is the completion of
\{f|_{U}\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}f\in C^{\infty}(\overline{U}),\lVert f|_{U}\rVert_{W^{r}_{p}(U)}<\infty\}
with respect to the norm

\lVert f\rVert_{W^{r}_{p}(U)}\vcentcolon=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d},\lvert\alpha\rvert\leq r}\lVert\partial^{\alpha}f\rVert_{L^{p}(U)}+\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d},\lvert\alpha\rvert=\lfloor r\rfloor}[\partial^{\alpha}f]_{W^{r-\lfloor r\rfloor}_{p}(U)},

where
[g]_{W^{0}_{p}(U)}\vcentcolon=0
and for
s\in(0,1)
,

[g]_{W^{s}_{p}(U)}\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\Big(\int_{U}\int_{U}\frac{\lvert g(x)-g(y)\rvert^{p}}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d+ps}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}&p<\infty,\\
\sup_{x,y\in U,\lvert x-y\rvert\leq 1}\frac{\lvert g(x)-g(y)\rvert}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{s}},&p=\infty.\end{cases}.

We set
H^{r}(U)\vcentcolon=W_{2}^{r}(U)
.
We denote by
W^{r}_{p,0}(U)
the completion of
C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
with respect to the norm

\lVert\cdot\rVert_{W^{r}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
(not
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{W^{r}_{p}(U)}
) and we set
H^{r}_{0}(U)\vcentcolon=W^{r}_{2,0}(U)
.

(b)

Let
U
be a bounded Lipschitz domain and
r\in(0,1)
.
The space
W^{r}_{p}(\partial U)
is the completion
of
C^{\infty}(\partial U)
with respect to the norm

\lVert g|_{\partial U}\rVert_{W^{r}_{p}(\partial U)}\vcentcolon=\lVert g\rVert_{L^{p}(\partial U)}+[g]_{W^{r}_{p}(\partial U)}

where

[g]_{W^{r}_{p}(\partial U)}\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\Big(\int_{\partial U}\int_{\partial U}\frac{\lvert g(x)-g(y)\rvert^{p}}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d-1+pr}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}&p<\infty,\\
\sup_{x,y\in\partial U,\lvert x-y\rvert\leq 1}\frac{\lvert g(x)-g(y)\rvert}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{r}}&p=\infty.\end{cases}

Remark 2.5

(Equivalent definitions)
.

For a bounded domain
U
, let
\tilde{W}^{r}_{p}(U)
be the space of
f\in L^{p}(U)
such that the distributional derivatives
\partial^{\alpha}f
for
|\alpha|\leq r
are in
L^{p}(U)
and
\lVert f\rVert_{W^{r}_{p}(U)}<\infty
.

Then
W^{r}_{p,0}(U)
is the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
in
\tilde{W}^{r}_{p}(U)
and if
U
is a bounded Lipschitz domain, then
W^{r}_{p}(U)=\tilde{W}^{r}_{p}(U)
, see for example
[
58
, Theorem 1.2]
and
[
55
]
.

Definition 2.6
.

For
U
a domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
r\geq 0
we also write
C^{r}(U)=W^{r}_{\infty}(U)
and
\lVert f\rVert_{C^{r}(U)}=\lVert f\rVert_{W^{r}_{\infty}(U)}
.

The following lemma relates the Sobolev–Slobodeckij spaces
W^{r}_{p}
(and
C^{r}
) for
U=\mathbb{R}^{d}
to the Besov spaces.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
s\in(0,\infty)\setminus\mathbb{N}
and
p\in[1,\infty]
.
Then
W^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=B_{p,p}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and the norms
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{W^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{B_{p,p}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
are equivalent (hence
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
are equivalent).

Proof.

This follows by
[
70
, p.90]
:
For
p\in[1,\infty)
one has
W^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=B_{p,p}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with equivalent norms, see
[
70
, p. 90 and p.113]
(
W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is written instead of
W^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and it is shown that
W^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\Lambda_{p,p}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=B_{p,p}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
), for
C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=B_{\infty,\infty}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with equivalent norms, see
[
70
, p.90 (9), (6) and p.113]
(actually, in
[
70
]

\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined differently but shown to be the same as
B^{s}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
).
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
U
be a bounded Lipschitz domain.

(a)

Set
\mathcal{D}\vcentcolon=\cup_{p\in[1,\infty],r\in[0,\infty)}W_{p}^{r}(U)
.
There exists an extension operator
\iota:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

•

\iota(f)=f
as distributions on
U
for
f\in\mathcal{D}
,

•

\lVert\iota(f)\rVert_{W^{r}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{U,p,r}\lVert f\rVert_{W^{r}_{p}(U)}

for every
p\in[1,\infty]
,
r\in[0,\infty)
and
f\in\mathcal{D}
,

•

\iota(f)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
f\in C^{\infty}(\overline{U})
.

(b)

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
and
r\in(\frac{1}{p},1+\frac{1}{p})
.
Then, the map
C^{\infty}(\overline{U})\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\partial U),f\mapsto f|_{\partial U}
extends uniquely to a bounded linear
operator
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{W^{r}_{p}(U)}:W^{r}_{p}(U)\to W^{r-\frac{1}{p}}_{p}(\partial U)
.
Furthermore,
there exists a bounded linear operator that is the right inverse of
\mathcal{T}
.

Proof.

For
(a)
see
[
67
, Chapter 6]
in combination with
[
69
, Section 4]
, or, for
r\in[0,1)
,
[
20
, Theorem 5.4]
.
For
(b)
, see
[
50
, Theorem 3]
.
∎

Definition 2.9
.

An extension operator
\iota
as in Lemma
2.8

(a)
is called a
universal extension operator
from
U
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The operator
\mathcal{T}
as in Lemma
2.8

(b)
is called the
trace operator
.

2.3
Green’s functions

Let
G
be the Green’s function of
-\Delta
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(remember that
d\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
), which means that
-\Delta G*f=f
for
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
That is,
G
is the distribution which is represented by the function defined for
x\neq 0
by

G(x)=\begin{cases}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\lvert x\rvert&d=2,\\
\frac{1}{d(d-2)\omega_{d}}\lvert x\rvert^{-(d-2)}&d\geq 3,\end{cases}

where
\omega_{d}
is the volume of the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The identity
-\Delta G*f=f
for
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
implies the formal identity
G=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(|2\pi\cdot|^{-2})
.
As
\lvert\cdot\rvert^{-2}
is singular at the origin, it is convenient to introduce the following variants of
G
.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
\check{\chi}
be the function introduced in Definition
2.2
.
For
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we set

G_{N}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{F}^{-1}((1-\check{\chi}(2^{-N}\cdot))\lvert 2\pi\cdot\rvert^{-2}).

Even though in general one cannot take the convolution of any two tempered distributions, for a tempered distribution
g\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we write

\displaystyle G_{N}*g:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}((1-\check{\chi}(2^{-N}\cdot))|2\pi\cdot|^{-2}\mathcal{F}g).

Observe that the latter is indeed a tempered distribution as the product of the smooth function
(1-\check{\chi}(2^{-N}\cdot))|2\pi\cdot|^{-2}
, which itself and all its derivatives are of at most polynomial growth, and the tempered distribution
\mathcal{F}g
, is again a tempered distribution.

Moreover, observe that for any
g\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
there exists a
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\displaystyle-\Delta G_{N}*g=g+f.

(10)

The parameter
N
is introduced to control the norm of
G_{N}\ast f
by letting
N
large:

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
U
be a bounded domain,
\sigma\in(0,\infty)

and
\delta_{-}\in(0,\delta)
.
Then for
L\geq 1
and
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

\displaystyle\lVert G_{N}*g\rVert_{C^{\delta_{-}}(U_{L})}\lesssim_{U,\delta_{-},\delta,\sigma}L^{\sigma}2^{-(\delta-\delta_{-})N}\lVert g\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Consequently, for
g\in\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}G_{N}*g=0
in
\mathcal{C}^{\delta_{-}}(U)
.

Proof.

This follows by Lemma
A.5
and Corollary
A.10
.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

For
M,N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we have
G_{N}-G_{M}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Furthermore, for
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we set

[\Delta(G_{N}-G)]\ast f\vcentcolon=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\check{\chi}(2^{-N}\cdot)]\ast f,

(11)

which is a smooth function.
By considering their Fourier transforms, one observes

\Delta(G_{N}\ast f)=-f+[\Delta(G_{N}-G)]\ast f.

(12)

3
Assumptions on the stochastic terms

We motivate and state the main assumptions in Section
3.1
,
namely, those that are already mentioned in Remark
1.3
: the construction assumption
3.2

(I)
, the Neumann assumption
3.2

(II)
and the ergodic assumption
3.2

(III)
.
In Section
3.2
we show that the construction assumption is satisfied for the white noise
\xi
in two and three dimensions and mentioned that it is satisfied for a very general class of subcritical noises within the framework of regularity structures (the arguments are postponed to the Appendix
C
, since it requires the full-fledged theory of regularity structures, see also Appendix
B
).
In Section
3.3
we consider sufficient conditions and examples for which the Neumann assumption
3.2

(II)
is satisfied.

3.1
Assumptions

In addition to Assumption
1.1
, we will introduce three more assumptions.
In this section we go into more depth on the idea behind the construction as explained in Section
1.5
and motivate the main assumptions, which we formulate in
3.2
.

Remark 3.1

(Towards the construction assumptions)
.

Recall from Assumption
1.1
that
\xi_{\varepsilon}
is a mollification of the white noise
\xi
.
As already motivated in Section
1.5
, for the convergence of the smooth Anderson Hamiltonians, it basically suffices to find functions
w^{\varepsilon}
and scalars
c_{\varepsilon}
such that

\displaystyle y^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=-e^{2w^{\varepsilon}}(\xi_{\varepsilon}-c_{\varepsilon}+|\nabla w^{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\Delta w^{\varepsilon}),

(13)

converges in
\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta}
.
Moreover, in Remark
1.16
we have described the heuristics of finding such
w^{\varepsilon}
, or actually their limit,
w
, where (formally)

\displaystyle w=(-\Delta)^{-1}X,\quad\mbox{ and,}\quad X=\xi+\tau_{1}+\cdots+\tau_{k}.

(14)

For the above mentioned convergence of
y^{\varepsilon}
, we have not been precise on what
\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta}
means.
In fact, for the noise and the stochastic terms, we will not consider them as elements on function spaces on
U
, but as elements of weighted Besov spaces on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
That is, we consider
\xi
as an element of
\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The
\sigma
determines the “amount” of polynomial growth that it allows for. As
\xi
is of at most polynomial growth, so is the
w
as in (
14
).
But then
e^{2w}
could be of exponential growth, so that we cannot guarantee a priori that (
13
) is an element of a space that allows only polynomial growth, which we want in order to show the existence of the integrated density of states.
For this reason we replace
e^{2w^{\varepsilon}}
by
F(w^{\varepsilon})
in the definition of
y^{\varepsilon}
, where
F
is a bounded smooth function which equals
t\mapsto-e^{2t}
around the origin.

Now “
(-\Delta)^{-1}
” as in (
14
) is a formal notation, which one could replace by “
G*
”, where
G
is the Green’s function as in Section
2.3
.
However, due to the singularity of
G
, we will not exactly work with the Green’s function itself, but rather a version of it in which “the Fourier terms around zero” are cut off, i.e., we will work with
G_{N}
instead (Definition
2.10
).
The extra parameter
N
will be tuned, i.e., it will be replaced by a random variable
M
that also depends on the domain
U
so that instead of (
14
), we consider

\displaystyle w=G_{M}*X,

and
M
is set in such a way that
F(w)=-e^{2w}
.

By the above remark we are now ready to put the assumptions on the stochastic terms for the construction of the Anderson Hamiltonian with Dirichlet boundary conditions, see Assumptions
3.2

(I)
.
In these assumptions we will also give a rigorous meaning to the object
X
that appeared in (
14
).

In Remark
3.5
we comment on the Neumann assumption
3.2

(II)
, as it is easier to discuss this given that the reader has read the construction assumption
3.2

(I)
first.

In Assumptions
3.2
we display not only both the construction and the Neumann assumptions, but also the ergodic assumption, which is a rather standard assumption that is made to guarantee the existence of the integrated density of states.

Recall that
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha,\sigma}
and
B_{p,q}^{\alpha}
are defined in Definition
2.2
and
G_{N}
is defined in Definition
2.10
.

Assumptions 3.2
.

We abbreviate for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\alpha}=\bigcap_{\sigma\in(0,\infty)}\bigcap_{p\in[1,\infty)}L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{\alpha,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).

(I)

(Construction assumption)
There exist
(c_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
in
\mathbb{R}
;
X
and
(X^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
in
\mathbb{L}^{-2+\delta}

such that
X-\xi\in\mathbb{L}^{-2+2\delta}

and for all
\sigma\in(0,\infty)
and
p\in[1,\infty)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\lVert X^{\varepsilon}-X\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\lVert X^{\varepsilon}-\xi_{\varepsilon}-(X-\xi)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-2+2\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}

\displaystyle=0.

Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a compactly supported smooth function such that

F(x)=-e^{2x}
if
\lvert x\rvert\leq 2
.
For
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we set
W_{N}^{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=G_{N}\ast X^{\varepsilon}
and

Y^{\varepsilon}_{N}\vcentcolon=F(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})\big(\xi_{\varepsilon}-c_{\varepsilon}+\lvert\nabla W^{\varepsilon}_{N}\rvert^{2}+\Delta W_{N}^{\varepsilon}\big).

(15)

There exists a
Y_{N}\in\mathbb{L}^{-1+\delta}
such that for all
\sigma\in(0,\infty)
and
p\in[1,\infty)
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\lVert Y^{\varepsilon}_{N}-Y_{N}\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}=0.

Furthermore,
there exists an integer
\bm{\mathfrak{b}}=\bm{\mathfrak{b}}(\delta)\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
\sigma\in(0,\infty)
and
p\in[1,\infty)
we have

\bm{\mathfrak{a}}\vcentcolon=\bm{\mathfrak{a}}(\delta,\sigma)\vcentcolon=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-\bm{\mathfrak{b}}N}\lVert Y_{N}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{P}).

(16)

Both
X
and
Y_{N}
are independent of the mollifier
\rho
.

(II)

(Neumann assumption)
We assume that
\delta\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
.
For a bounded Lipschitz domain
U
, let
\tilde{Y}^{U}_{\varepsilon}
be the tempered distribution given by (see Definition
1.13
for the notation
\int_{\partial U}f\nabla g\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{S}
)

\varphi\mapsto\int_{\partial U}\varphi\nabla(G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\bm{S}.

Then there exists a random variable
\tilde{Y}^{U}
with values in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, independent of the mollifier function
\rho
, such that

\displaystyle\lVert\tilde{Y}^{U}_{\varepsilon}-\tilde{Y}^{U}\rVert_{B^{-2+\delta}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}0\quad\mbox{in probability for all $p\in(2,\infty)$.}

(17)

In addition, for every
p\in(8d,\infty)
and
q\in[1,\infty)
we have

\sup_{L\in\mathbb{N}}L^{-\frac{1}{4}}\lVert\tilde{Y}^{U_{L}}\rVert_{B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\in L^{q}(\mathbb{P}).

(18)

(III)

(Ergodic assumption)
Recall that our probability space
\Omega
is the space
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of tempered distributions.
Then, we have maps
T_{x}:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega
(
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
) of translations
\omega\to\omega(\cdot-x)
.
We assume the probability measure
\mathbb{P}
to be translation invariant (
\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}\circ T_{x}
for all
x
) and ergodic with respect to
(T_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
.

Remark 3.3
.

It is well-known (e.g.
[
61
, Proposition 6.1]
) that Assumption
3.2

(III)
is satisfied for the Gaussian noise
\xi
with
covariance
\mathbb{E}[\xi(x)\xi(y)]=\gamma(x-y)
such that

\lim_{\lvert x\rvert\to\infty}\gamma(x)=0.

In particular, this assumption is satisfied for the white noise.

Remark 3.4
.

The technical estimates (
16
) and (
18
) are necessary only for the construction of the IDS (Section
5.3
, in particular Remark
5.13
).

Remark 3.5

(About the Neumann assumptions)
.

From the discussion in Remark
3.1
we know that the
w^{\varepsilon}
and the
y^{\varepsilon}
as in Section
1.5
are equal to
W^{\varepsilon}_{M}
and
Y^{\varepsilon}_{M}
for a well-chosen random variable
M
(for its definition, see Definition
5.2
).

From the convergences of the stochastic terms
w^{\varepsilon}
and
y^{\varepsilon}
that we obtain from the construction assumption, the two first symmetric forms in (
8
) converge, as forms on
H^{1}(U)
.
We need an additional assumption in order to deal with the third symmetric form in (
8
), which is,

\displaystyle v\mapsto\int_{\partial U}\mathcal{T}(v^{2})e^{2W_{M}^{\varepsilon}}\nabla W_{M}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{S}.

Set

\displaystyle\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon}_{M}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=G_{M}\ast(X^{\varepsilon}-\xi_{\varepsilon})+(G_{M}-G_{0})\ast\xi_{\varepsilon}.

We decompose the above symmetric form as the sum of the following symmetric forms

\displaystyle v\mapsto\int_{\partial U}\mathcal{T}(v^{2})e^{2W_{M}^{\varepsilon}}\nabla(G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\bm{S},\qquad v\mapsto\int_{\partial U}\mathcal{T}(v^{2})e^{2W_{M}^{\varepsilon}}\nabla\widehat{Y}_{M}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{S}.

(19)

Now, by the convergence
\xi_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\xi
and the assumed convergences of the
X^{\varepsilon}
and
X^{\varepsilon}-\xi_{\varepsilon}
from the construction assumption
3.2

(I)
, we obtain by estimates on the Green’s function, which we have put in Corollary
A.10
, and estimates of derivatives in Besov spaces, see Lemma
A.6
,

\displaystyle W^{\varepsilon}_{M}\rightarrow W_{M}

\displaystyle\mbox{ in }\mathcal{C}^{\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(20)

\displaystyle G_{0}*\xi_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow G_{0}*\xi

\displaystyle\mbox{ in }\mathcal{C}^{\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(21)

\displaystyle G_{M}*(X^{\varepsilon}-\xi_{\varepsilon})\rightarrow G_{M}*(X-\xi)

\displaystyle\mbox{ in }\mathcal{C}^{2\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(22)

\displaystyle(G_{M}-G_{0})*\xi_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow(G_{M}-G_{0})*\xi

\displaystyle\mbox{ in }\mathcal{C}^{1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(23)

In particular, by combining (
22
) and (
23
), for
\delta\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
we have

\displaystyle\widehat{Y}_{M}^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\widehat{Y}_{M}\qquad\mbox{in }\mathcal{C}^{2\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(24)

In Section
4
we will show in more detail that (
20
) and (
24
) imply the convergence of the second symmetric form in (
19
).
This is basically because the function
e^{2W_{M}^{\varepsilon}}\nabla\widehat{Y}_{M}^{\varepsilon}
converges in a space of positive regularity.
Now for
\delta>1
the function
e^{2W_{M}^{\varepsilon}}\nabla(G_{0}*\xi_{\varepsilon})
converges in a space of positive regularity.
However for
\delta\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
the convergence in (
21
) does not seem to suffice, due to the integration over the
d-1
dimensional boundary
\partial U
.
Let us elaborate on this.
We can rewrite the first symmetric form in (
19
) as
v\mapsto\langle\tilde{Y}^{U}_{\varepsilon},e^{2W^{\varepsilon}_{M}}v^{2}\rangle
, where
\tilde{Y}^{U}_{\varepsilon}
is the distribution
as in
3.2

(II)
given by

\varphi\mapsto\int_{\partial U}\varphi\nabla(G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\bm{S}.

The distribution
\tilde{Y}^{U}_{\varepsilon}
could be formally interpreted as the product of
\nabla(G_{0}*\xi_{\varepsilon})
with the distribution
\delta_{\partial U}
, given by
\varphi\mapsto\int_{\partial U}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}S
.
The distribution
\delta_{\partial U}
is of regularity
-1
(e.g., for
I=[0,1]^{d-1}\times\{0\}
,
\delta_{I}
is the tensor product of
\mathbbm{1}_{[0,1]^{d-1}}
and
\delta_{0}
, which are in
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
and
\mathcal{C}^{-1}(\mathbb{R})
, respectively; hence the tensor product is in
\mathcal{C}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, see
[
64
]
).
Therefore the product of these two, and thus
\tilde{Y}^{U}_{\varepsilon}
, converges only in the space of regularity
-2+\delta
.
As the symmetric form is on
H^{1}
, by this reasoning, this convergence is sufficient only for
\delta>1
.

When additionally imposing conditions on the boundary, called the Neumann assumption
3.2

(II)
it actually suffices to assume
\delta>\frac{1}{2}
.
For the details we refer to Theorem
4.3
, though we do mention it is basically due to the identity

\displaystyle\int_{\partial U}\mathcal{T}(v^{2})e^{2W_{M}^{\varepsilon}}\nabla(G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\bm{S}=\langle\tilde{Y}^{U}_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{R}(e^{2W^{\varepsilon}_{M}}\mathcal{T}(v)^{2})\rangle,

where
\mathcal{T}
is the trace operator and
\mathcal{R}
is the composition of the right inverse trace operator and the extension operator (see
Lemma
2.8

(b)
).

Remark 3.6
.

To construct the natural Neumann Anderson Hamiltonian without the Neumann condition
3.2

(II)
,
we conjecture that “boundary renormalisation” is necessary.

For instance, if
\xi
is the 3D white noise, the recent work
[
28
]
suggests that the
operators associated to the symmetric forms

(u,v)\mapsto\int_{U}\nabla u\cdot\nabla v-(\xi_{\varepsilon}-c_{\varepsilon})uv+c^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\int_{\partial U}uv\,\mathrm{d}S

converge, where the constants
c^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
diverge logarithmically.

3.2
Construction assumption

Here we give examples of the noise
\xi
for which the construction assumption
3.2

(I)
is satisfied. We recall that the heuristic to find
X
is outlined in Remark
1.16
.

Example 3.7
.

Let
d=2
and
\xi
be the white noise on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Then
\xi\in L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

and by the proof of Theorem
A.3
,
\xi_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\xi
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
for all
p\in[1,\infty)
,

\sigma\in(0,\infty)
and
\delta\in(0,1)
.

We set

c_{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}[\lvert\nabla G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}(0)],\qquad\tau^{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=\lvert\nabla G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}-c_{\varepsilon}.

In the following, all convergences hold for all for all
p\in[1,\infty)
,

\sigma\in(0,\infty)
and
\delta\in(0,1)
, so we refrain from repeating this.
As shown in
[
34
, Proposition 1.3]
(recall (
10
)),

we have
\tau^{\varepsilon}\to\tau
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-2+2\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.
We then set

X^{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=\xi_{\varepsilon}+\tau^{\varepsilon}.

We define
W_{N}^{\varepsilon}
and
Y_{N}^{\varepsilon}
as in Assumption
3.2
.
To compute
Y_{N}^{\varepsilon}
, we observe that

\lvert\nabla W_{N}^{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}=\lvert\nabla G_{0}\ast X^{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}+2(\nabla G_{0}\ast X^{\varepsilon})\cdot(\nabla(G_{N}-G_{0})\ast X^{\varepsilon})+\lvert\nabla(G_{N}-G_{0})\ast X^{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}.

To simplify notation, we set
r_{N}^{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=(G_{N}-G_{0})\ast X^{\varepsilon}
,
and recall that the limit
r_{N}\vcentcolon=(G_{N}-G_{0})\ast X
is smooth by Remark
2.12
.
Recalling (
12
), we obtain

\displaystyle Z_{N}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\xi_{\varepsilon}-c_{\varepsilon}+\lvert\nabla W_{N}^{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}+\Delta W_{N}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\phantom{\vcentcolon}=2(\nabla G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})\cdot(\nabla G_{0}\ast\tau^{\varepsilon})+\lvert\nabla G_{0}\ast\tau^{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}+2(\nabla G_{0}\ast X^{\varepsilon})\cdot\nabla r_{N}^{\varepsilon}+\lvert\nabla r_{N}^{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{\vcentcolon=}+\Delta r_{N}^{\varepsilon}+[\Delta(G_{0}-G)]\ast X^{\varepsilon}.

By the properties of
G_{0}
(see Corollary
A.10
),

\displaystyle\nabla G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon}\to\nabla G_{0}\ast\xi\quad

\displaystyle\text{in }L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})),

\displaystyle\nabla G_{0}\ast\tau^{\varepsilon}\to\nabla G_{0}\ast\tau\quad

\displaystyle\text{in }L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-1+2\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})).

Since
\delta
can be arbitrarily close to
1
, the products in the formula of
Z_{N}^{\varepsilon}
have well-defined limits, and therefore

Z_{N}^{\varepsilon}
converges in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.
Because
W_{N}^{\varepsilon}=G_{N}*(\xi_{\varepsilon}+\tau^{\varepsilon})
, by Corollary
A.10

\displaystyle W_{N}^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow W_{N}:=G_{N}*(\xi+\tau)\quad\mbox{in }L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})),

and, as
F
is Lipschitz, also
F(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})
converges in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
(using Lemma
A.4
to “get rid of
\sigma
”).
Therefore, for
\delta\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
,
Y_{N}^{\varepsilon}=F(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})Z_{N}^{\varepsilon}
converges in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
by the Bony type estimates for weighted Besov spaces

[
31
, Lemma 2.14]
.

Finally, the estimate (
16
) on
Y_{N}
follows from the estimate

\lVert r_{N}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{\kappa,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim_{\kappa,\sigma}2^{(\kappa-\delta)N}\lVert X\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

which is a consequence of Corollary
A.10
. Therefore, Assumption
3.2

(I)
holds.

Example 3.8
.

Let
d=3
and
\xi
be the white noise on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Then
\xi\in L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))

and by the proof of Theorem
A.3
,
\xi_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\xi
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
for all
p\in[1,\infty)
,

\sigma\in(0,\infty)
and
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
.
In the following, all convergences hold for all for all
p\in[1,\infty)
,

\sigma\in(0,\infty)
and
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, so we refrain from repeating this.
From the convergences of the renormalized models associated with the 3D parabolic Anderson model
[
35
, Theorem 5.3]
or by considering the convergences in
[
30
, Theorem 2.38]
, we deduce the existence of
\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{3},\tau_{4}
and the following convergences as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
,

\displaystyle\tau_{1}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\lvert\nabla G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\lvert\nabla G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}(0)]\to\tau_{1}

\displaystyle\text{in }L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-2+2\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),

\displaystyle\tau_{2}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=(\nabla G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})\cdot(\nabla G_{0}\ast\tau_{1}^{\varepsilon})\to\tau_{2}

\displaystyle\text{in }L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-2+3\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),

\displaystyle\tau_{3}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\lvert\nabla G_{0}\ast\tau_{1}^{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\lvert\nabla G_{0}\ast\tau_{1}^{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}(0)]\to\tau_{3}

\displaystyle\text{in }L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-2+4\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),

\displaystyle\tau_{4}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=(\nabla G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})\cdot(\nabla G_{0}\ast\tau_{2}^{\varepsilon})\to\tau_{4}

\displaystyle\text{in }L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})).

We set

c_{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}[\lvert\nabla G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}(0)]+\mathbb{E}[\lvert\nabla G_{0}\ast\tau_{1}^{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}(0)],

X^{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=\xi_{\varepsilon}+\tau_{1}^{\varepsilon}+2\tau_{2}^{\varepsilon}+\tau_{3}^{\varepsilon}

and
r_{N}^{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=(G_{N}-G_{0})\ast X^{\varepsilon}
.
Similar to the 2D case, we obtain

\displaystyle Z_{N}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\xi_{\varepsilon}-c_{\varepsilon}+\lvert\nabla W_{N}^{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}+\Delta W_{N}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\phantom{\vcentcolon}=4\tau_{4}^{\varepsilon}+2(\nabla G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})\cdot(\nabla G_{0}\ast\tau_{3}^{\varepsilon})+2(\nabla G_{0}\ast\tau_{1}^{\varepsilon})\cdot(\nabla G_{0}\ast(2\tau_{2}^{\varepsilon}+\tau_{3}^{\varepsilon}))

\displaystyle\qquad+2(\nabla G_{0}\ast X^{\varepsilon})\cdot\nabla r_{N}^{\varepsilon}+\lvert\nabla r_{N}^{\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}+\Delta r_{N}^{\varepsilon}+[\Delta(G_{0}-G)]\ast X^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\qquad+\lvert\nabla G_{0}\ast(2\tau_{2}^{\varepsilon}+\tau_{3}^{\varepsilon})\rvert^{2},

from which we see that
Z_{N}^{\varepsilon}
converges in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
.
As for the 2D case,
F(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})
converges in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
.
However, now
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and so
-1+2\delta
is negative.
Hence we cannot guarantee the convergence of
Y_{N}^{\varepsilon}=F(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})Z_{N}^{\varepsilon}
in the same way as for the 2D case (recall the Bony type estimates for weighted Besov spaces

[
31
, Lemma 2.14]
).
However, with some techniques from paracontrolled distributions
[
32
]
, we can guarantee the convergence.
We describe the method without proving all the details. (The symbols “
\prec,\succ,\circ
” are here replaced by “
\varolessthan,\varogreaterthan,\varodot
” to align with the recent notation.)

From the above, and using Lemma
A.6

(a)
and Corollary
A.10
, observe that
\nabla(G_{0}*\tau_{3}^{\varepsilon}+r_{N}^{\varepsilon})
converges in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-1+4\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))

and
\nabla(G_{0}*\xi_{\varepsilon})
converges in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))

so that

\displaystyle 4\tau_{4}^{\varepsilon}+2(\nabla G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})\cdot\nabla(G_{0}\ast\tau_{3}^{\varepsilon}+r_{N}^{\varepsilon})

(25)

converges in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
and

\displaystyle\hat{Z}_{N}^{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=Z_{N}^{\varepsilon}-\Big(4\tau_{4}^{\varepsilon}+2(\nabla G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})\cdot\nabla(G_{0}\ast\tau_{3}^{\varepsilon}+r_{N}^{\varepsilon})\Big)

converges in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-1+2\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
.
Therefore the product
F(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})\hat{Z}_{N}^{\varepsilon}
converges in the space
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-1+2\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
for
\delta\in(\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{2})
.

Hence, we are left to consider the product
F(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})(Z_{N}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{Z}_{N}^{\varepsilon})
.
Now, we finish by showing

(a)

F(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})\tau_{4}^{\varepsilon}
converges in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-1+2\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
,

(b)

F(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})(\nabla G_{0}*\xi_{\varepsilon})
converges in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-1+2\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3}))
.

Indeed, if (b) holds, then
F(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})(\nabla G_{0}*\xi_{\varepsilon})\cdot\nabla(G_{0}\ast\tau_{3}^{\varepsilon}+r_{N}^{\varepsilon})
converges in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{-1+2\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
because
-2+6\delta>0
for
\delta\in(\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{2})
.

Let
Q^{\varepsilon}
be either
\tau_{4}^{\varepsilon}
or
\nabla G_{0}*\xi_{\varepsilon}
for the moment.
As the convergences of the paraproducts
\varolessthan
and
\varogreaterthan
are guaranteed, it suffices to show the convergence of the resonant product
F(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})\varodot Q^{\varepsilon}
.
By the paralinearization lemma
[
32
, Lemma 2.6]
,

F(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})=F^{\prime}(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})\varolessthan W_{0}^{\varepsilon}+F^{\prime}(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})\varolessthan(W_{N}^{\varepsilon}-W_{0}^{\varepsilon})+R_{N}^{\varepsilon},

where
\lVert R_{N}^{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2\delta,\sigma}}\lesssim_{\delta,\sigma}1+\lVert W_{N}^{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{\delta,\sigma}}
.
Since
W_{N}-W_{0}
is smooth, it suffices to consider the resonant product between
F^{\prime}(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})\varolessthan W_{0}^{\varepsilon}
and
Q^{\varepsilon}
.
By the so-called Commutator lemma
[
31
, Lemma 2.16]

\displaystyle\|(F^{\prime}(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})\varolessthan W_{0}^{\varepsilon})\varodot Q^{\varepsilon}-F^{\prime}(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})(W_{0}^{\varepsilon}\varodot Q^{\varepsilon})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1+3\delta,2\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|F^{\prime}(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\|W_{0}^{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\|Q^{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})},

from which can deduce that for the convergence of
F(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})\varodot Q^{\varepsilon}
it suffices to show convergence of
F^{\prime}(W_{N}^{\varepsilon})(W_{0}^{\varepsilon}\varodot Q^{\varepsilon})
.

Proof of (b)

For
Q^{\varepsilon}=\nabla(G_{0}*\xi_{\varepsilon})
we have
W_{0}^{\varepsilon}\varodot\nabla(G_{0}*\xi_{\varepsilon})=\frac{1}{2}\nabla((G_{0}*\xi_{\varepsilon})\varodot(G_{0}*\xi_{\varepsilon}))+G_{0}*(X^{\varepsilon}-\xi_{\varepsilon})\varodot\nabla(G_{0}*\xi_{\varepsilon})
and so the convergence follows because
(G_{0}*\xi_{\varepsilon})\varodot(G_{0}*\xi_{\varepsilon})
and
G_{0}*(X^{\varepsilon}-\xi_{\varepsilon})
converge in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{C}^{2\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
.

Proof of (a)

For
Q^{\varepsilon}=\tau_{4}^{\varepsilon}
, we have the following identity

\displaystyle(G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})\varodot\tau_{4}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=(\nabla G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})\varodot(\nabla G_{0}\ast\tau_{4}^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\qquad-\nabla\cdot[(G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})\varodot\nabla(G_{0}\ast\tau_{4}^{\varepsilon})]+(G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})\varodot[\Delta(G_{0}-G)\ast\tau_{4}^{\varepsilon}].

The convergence of the two terms in the second line follow rather straightforwardly (recall (
11
)), whereas the first term on the right-hand side (on the first line)
converges as is shown in the proof of
[
30
, Theorem 2.38]
.

As in the 2D case, the estimate (
16
) on
Y_{N}
follows from Corollary
A.10
.

The above examples are specific cases for which the construction condition
3.2

(I)
is valid.
This condition is however valid under very general assumptions in terms of regularity structures, as we show in the next theorem.
Namely, we consider the regularity structure associated with the generalized parabolic Anderson model

\partial_{0}u=\Delta u+\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}g_{i,j}(u)\partial_{i}u\partial_{j}u+\sum_{i=1}^{d}h_{i}(u)\partial_{i}u+k(u)+f(u)\xi,

(26)

or equivalently, the following elliptic equation, as it leads to the same regularity structure

\Delta u=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}g_{i,j}(u)\partial_{i}u\partial_{j}u+\sum_{i=1}^{d}h_{i}(u)\partial_{i}u+k(u)+f(u)\xi,

as described in
[
12
]
and we also consider the renormalization approach considered therein called the BPHZ renormalization, which is further developed in the subsequent works
[
17
,
38
,
7
]
