.

In the following theorem, the assumption we rely on, namely Assumption
B.42
, is formulated in the language of regularity structures, based on the language discussed preceding it in Appendix
B
.
This assumption basically means that the solution theory of the generalized parabolic Anderson model can be developed.
Hence, the following theorem states that if this is the case, then the construction condition
3.2

(I)
is valid as well.

Theorem 3.9
.

Impose Assumption
B.42
, which assumes that the BPHZ renormalization models converge and a few probabilistic estimates hold.
By setting
X^{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=X^{\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{BPHZ},\varepsilon}}
and
X\vcentcolon=X^{\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}}
as in Definition
C.9

and letting
c_{\varepsilon}
be defined by (
104
),
the construction condition
3.2

(I)
is valid.

Examples 3.10
.

The work
[
17
]
, see especially Theorem 2.31 and Theorem 2.34 therein, gives
conditions of the noise
\xi
under which Assumption
B.42
holds.
It is worth observing that Assumption
B.42
holds for the 2D and the 3D white noise, and
the Gaussian noise
\xi
whose covariance is formally given by

\mathbb{E}[\xi(x)\xi(y)]=\gamma(x-y),

where
\gamma:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty)
is smooth and bounded away from
0
and
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
we have

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d},\\
\lvert k\rvert\leq 6d\end{subarray}}\sup_{x\in B(0,1)\setminus\{0\}}\lvert\partial^{k}\gamma(x)\rvert\lvert x\rvert^{\min\{4,d\}-\delta+|k|}<\infty,

see
[
17
, Theorem 2.15]
. For example one could take
\gamma
to be given by

\displaystyle\gamma(x)=c|x|^{-\alpha}

for some
c\in(0,\infty)
and
\alpha\in(0,\min\{d,4\})
.

3.3
Neumann assumption

In this section we discuss sufficient conditions for the Neumann condition
3.2

(II)
(Proposition
3.11
and Lemma
3.13
) and examples which satisfy the Neumann condition (Example
3.14
and Example
3.15
).

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\delta\in(1/2,1)
.
Suppose that for any bounded Lipschitz domain
U
, there exists a
\xi_{U}
such that

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{U}\xi_{\varepsilon}-\xi^{U}\rVert_{B^{-2+\delta}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}0\quad\mbox{in probability for all $p\in(2,\infty)$.}

Then, the convergence (
17
) holds. Furthermore, for any
\sigma\in(\frac{d}{p},\infty)
we have the bound

\lVert\tilde{Y}^{U_{L}}\rVert_{B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{\delta,p,\sigma,U}L^{2\sigma}\lVert\xi\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\lVert\xi^{U_{L}}\rVert_{B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\quad\text{for all }L\geq 1.

Proof.

The proof is based on the duality
[
63
, Theorem 2.17]
.
By the integration by parts formula (Lemma
1.14
),

\langle\tilde{Y}^{U_{L}}_{\varepsilon},\varphi\rangle=\int_{\partial U_{L}}\varphi\nabla(G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\bm{S}=\int_{U_{L}}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla(G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})-\int_{U_{L}}\varphi\Delta(G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon}).

(27)

We first consider the first term on the right-hand side of (
27
).
Let
\phi
be a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\phi=1
on a neighborhood
U
.
The map

\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad\varphi\mapsto\langle\mathbbm{1}_{U_{L}}\nabla\varphi,\phi(L^{-1}\cdot)\nabla(G_{0}\ast\xi)\rangle

(28)

is well-defined, is independent of
\phi
and is an element of
B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Indeed, if
q\in(1,2)
is such that
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
, by the duality

[
63
, Theorem 2.17]
,

\lvert\langle\mathbbm{1}_{U_{L}}\nabla\varphi,\phi(L^{-1}\cdot)\nabla(G_{0}\ast\xi)\rangle\rvert\leq\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{U_{L}}\nabla\varphi\rVert_{B_{q,q}^{1-\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lVert\phi(L^{-1}\cdot)\nabla(G_{0}\ast\xi)\rVert_{B_{p,p}^{-1+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

By Lemma
A.6
and Lemma
A.18
(see Definition
A.14
for
C_{\operatorname{Mult}}
), as
1-\delta<\frac{1}{2}<\frac{1}{q}
,

\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{U_{L}}\nabla\varphi\rVert_{B_{q,q}^{1-\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{q,\delta}C_{\operatorname{Mult}}^{U_{L}}[W_{q}^{1-\delta}]\lVert\nabla\varphi\rVert_{B_{q,q}^{1-\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{q,\delta,U}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{B_{q,q}^{2-\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

By Lemma
A.1
(remember that we have
p\sigma>d
) and Lemma
A.4
,

\lVert\phi(L^{-1}\cdot)\nabla(G_{0}\ast\xi)\rVert_{B_{p,p}^{-1+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{p,\delta,\sigma}L^{2\sigma}\lVert\nabla(G_{0}\ast\xi)\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

By Lemma
A.6
and Corollary
A.10
,

\lVert\nabla(G_{0}\ast\xi)\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{\delta,\sigma}\lVert\xi\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Therefore, the distribution defined by (
28
) belongs to
the dual space of
B_{q,q}^{2-\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which is identified with
B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
and its norm in
B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is bounded by a multiple of

L^{2\sigma}\lVert\xi\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

(29)

Now it is easy to see that this distribution is the limit of the first term of the right-hand side of (
27
) (as
\lVert\xi_{\varepsilon}-\xi\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\rightarrow 0
).

Let us turn to the second term of the right-hand side of (
27
).
Note that

\int_{U_{L}}\varphi\Delta(G_{0}\ast\xi_{\varepsilon})=-\int_{U_{L}}\varphi\xi_{\varepsilon}+\int_{U_{L}}\varphi[\Delta(G_{0}-G)]\ast\xi_{\varepsilon}.

The first term is equal to
\langle\mathbbm{1}_{U_{L}}\xi_{\varepsilon},\varphi\rangle
, whose convergence is guaranteed by the assumption.
The second term converges to

\int_{U_{L}}\varphi[\Delta(G_{0}-G)]\ast\xi,

which equals

\displaystyle\langle\mathbbm{1}_{U_{L}}\varphi,\phi(L^{-1}\cdot)[\Delta(G_{0}-G)]*\xi\rangle.

(30)

Now, as
[\Delta(G_{0}-G)]\ast\xi
is the smooth function
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\check{\chi})*\xi=\Delta_{\leq-1}\xi
by (
11
) (for
\Delta_{\leq J}
see Definition
A.7
) we obtain that the distribution defined by (
30
) has its norm in
B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
also bounded by a multiple of (
29
) by using a similar argument as above and Lemma
A.9

to obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\|[\Delta(G_{0}-G)]*\xi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{\sigma}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

∎

Lemma 3.12
.

We assume
\delta\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
and that there exists a

\delta^{\prime}\in(0,1)
such that, for each
p\in(1,\infty)
,
there exist a constant
C^{\partial}_{p}\in(0,\infty)
and a map
\bm{\varepsilon}_{p}^{\partial}:(0,1)^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to(0,\infty)

with the following properties.

(i)

One has

\sup_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\lambda\in(0,1),x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bm{\varepsilon}_{p}^{\partial}(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2};\lambda,x)<\infty

(31)

and for each fixed
\lambda\in(0,1)
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, one has
\lim_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\downarrow 0}\bm{\varepsilon}_{p}^{\partial}(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2};\lambda,x)=0
(by which we mean
\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\sup_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in(0,\varepsilon)}\bm{\varepsilon}_{p}^{\partial}(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2};\lambda,x)=0
).

(ii)

For every
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\lambda\in(0,1)
,
q\in(1,\infty)
, bounded Lipschitz domain
U
,

x\in B(U,1)
and
\phi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with

\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 1
and
\operatorname{supp}(\phi)\subseteq B(0,1)
, one has, with
\phi^{\lambda}_{x}\vcentcolon=\lambda^{-d}\phi(\lambda^{-1}(\cdot-x))
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\lvert\langle\xi_{\varepsilon_{1}},\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle\rvert^{q}]

\displaystyle\leq C^{\partial}_{q}\lambda^{(-2+\delta+\delta^{\prime})q},

(32)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\lvert\langle\xi_{\varepsilon_{1}}-\xi_{\varepsilon_{2}},\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle\rvert^{q}]

\displaystyle\leq\bm{\varepsilon}_{q}^{\partial}(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2};\lambda,x)\lambda^{(-2+\delta+\delta^{\prime})q}.

(33)

Then there exists a
\xi^{U}
with values in
\mathcal{C}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
r\in(0,\infty)

such that for all
p\in(\frac{d}{\delta^{\prime}}+1,\infty)
and
q\in[p,\infty)
the random variables
\mathbbm{1}_{U}\xi_{\varepsilon}

converge to
\xi^{U}
in
L^{q}(\mathbb{P},B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
and

\mathbb{E}[\lVert\xi^{U}\rVert_{B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}]\lesssim_{p,q,\delta,\delta^{\prime}}\lvert B(U,r)\rvert^{\frac{q}{p}}C^{\partial}_{q}.

(34)

Consequently, by Proposition
3.11
, the Neumann condition
3.2

(II)
holds.

Proof.

To simplify notation, we set
\eta\vcentcolon=\mathbbm{1}_{U}(\xi_{\varepsilon_{1}}-\xi_{\varepsilon_{2}})
.
We use the wavelet characterization of Besov spaces given in Propositions
A.12
and
A.13
.
Using the notation therein, we have

\lVert\eta\rVert_{B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\lesssim\Big(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}2^{np(-2+\delta)-nd}\sum_{G\in\mathfrak{G}^{n},m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\lvert\langle\eta,2^{\frac{nd}{2}}\Psi^{n,G}_{m}\rangle\rvert^{p}\Big)^{\frac{q}{p}}.

Since
\psi_{\mathfrak{f}}
and
\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}
are compactly supported, by (
78
) there exists an
r\in(0,\infty)
such that
the sum with respect to
m
is over
\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap 2^{n}B(U,r)
.
Therefore, by Minkowski’s inequality (remember that
\frac{q}{p}\geq 1
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\lVert\eta\rVert_{B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}]^{\frac{p}{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big\lVert\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}2^{np(-2+\delta)-nd}\sum_{G\in\mathfrak{G}^{n},m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap 2^{n}B(U,r)}\lvert\langle\eta,2^{\frac{nd}{2}}\Psi^{n,G}_{m}\rangle\rvert^{p}\Big\rVert_{L^{\frac{q}{p}}(\mathbb{P})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}2^{np(-2+\delta)-nd}\sum_{G\in\mathfrak{G}^{n},m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap 2^{n}B(U,r)}\mathbb{E}[\lvert\langle\eta,2^{\frac{nd}{2}}\Psi^{n,G}_{m}\rangle\rvert^{q}]^{\frac{p}{q}}.

By (
33
), by (
78
) and
observing that
2^{\frac{nd}{2}}\Psi_{m}^{n,G}=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\phi^{\lambda}_{\lambda m}
for
\phi=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\psi_{G_{j}}
and
\lambda=2^{-(n-1)_{+}}
,

and
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\#\mathfrak{G}^{n}<\infty
, we obtain

\sum_{G\in\mathfrak{G}^{n}}\mathbb{E}[\lvert\langle\eta,2^{\frac{nd}{2}}\Psi^{n,G}_{m}\rangle\rvert^{q}]^{\frac{p}{q}}\lesssim 2^{-np(-2+\delta+\delta^{\prime})}\bm{\varepsilon}_{q}^{\partial}(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2};2^{-(n-1)_{+}},2^{-(n-1)_{+}}m)^{\frac{p}{q}}.

Therefore

\mathbb{E}[\lVert\eta\rVert_{B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}]^{\frac{p}{q}}\\
\lesssim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}2^{-n\delta^{\prime}-nd}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap 2^{n}B(U,r)}\bm{\varepsilon}_{q}^{\partial}(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2};2^{-(n-1)_{+}},2^{-(n-1)_{+}}m)^{\frac{p}{q}}.

(35)

As
\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap 2^{n}B(U,r)}1\lesssim 2^{nd}|B(U,r)|
,
we have

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}2^{-n\delta^{\prime}-nd}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap 2^{n}B(U,r)}1\lesssim|B(U,r)|\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}2^{-n\delta^{\prime}}.

In view of the condition
(i)
, the dominated convergence theorem yields that the right-hand side (and thus left-hand side) of (
35
) converges to
0
as
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\downarrow 0
.
This proves the claim on the convergence of
\mathbbm{1}_{U}\xi_{\varepsilon}
.
By using (
32
), one can similarly prove the estimate (
34
).

Regarding the last claim, to prove (
18
), let
\alpha>0
. We have the bound

\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\sup_{L\in\mathbb{N}}L^{-\alpha}\lVert\tilde{Y}^{U_{L}}\rVert_{B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}}\big)^{q}\Big]\leq\sum_{L\in\mathbb{N}}L^{-\alpha q}\mathbb{E}[\lVert\tilde{Y}^{U_{L}}\rVert_{B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}}^{q}].

By Proposition
3.11
and (
34
), for any
\sigma\in(d/p,\infty)
the right-hand side is up to constant bounded by

\sum_{L\in\mathbb{N}}L^{-\alpha q}(L^{2\sigma q}+L^{\frac{qd}{p}}).

Therefore, if
\alpha>\frac{2d}{p}
, by choosing
\sigma
sufficiently close to
\frac{d}{p}
, the above sum is finite.
In particular, if
p>8d
we may choose
\alpha=\frac{1}{4}
.
∎

Lemma 3.13
.

Suppose that
\gamma,f,g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are measurable functions such that

\lvert\gamma\rvert\leq f+g,

(36)

with
g\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

f(\lambda x)=\lambda^{-\alpha}f(x)
with
\alpha<3
for every
\lambda\in(0,\infty)
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Furthermore, suppose that
f
is locally integrable.
Let
\xi
be a centered Gaussian noise whose covariance is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\langle\xi,\varphi\rangle\langle\xi,\psi\rangle]=\langle\gamma*\varphi,\psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\quad\varphi,\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Then,
\xi
satisfies the Neumann condition
3.2

(II)
.

Proof.

In view of Lemma
3.12
, it suffices to show that the conditions of that lemma are satisfied.
As
\xi
is Gaussian, so is for example
\langle\xi_{\varepsilon_{1}},\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi_{x}^{\lambda}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
for
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\lambda\in(0,\infty)
, where
\phi^{\lambda}_{x}\vcentcolon=\lambda^{-d}\phi(\lambda^{-1}(\cdot-x))
.
As for Gaussian random variables
Z
one has
\mathbb{E}{[|Z|^{p}]}=\mathbb{E}{[|Z|^{2}]}^{\frac{p}{2}}\mathbb{E}{[|X|^{p}]}
, for
X
a standard normal random variable,
it is sufficient to consider
p=2
.

Let
U
be a bounded Lipschitz domain and
\phi
be as in Lemma
3.12

(II)
.
Observe that because
\rho
is symmetric

\displaystyle\langle\xi_{\varepsilon_{1}}-\xi_{\varepsilon_{2}},\psi\rangle=\langle\xi*(\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}}-\rho_{\varepsilon_{2}}),\psi\rangle=\langle\xi,(\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}}-\rho_{\varepsilon_{2}})*\psi\rangle.

Therefore, for any
\lambda\in(0,1)
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\langle\xi_{\varepsilon_{1}}-\xi_{\varepsilon_{2}},\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle^{2}]

\displaystyle=\langle\gamma*(\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}}-\rho_{\varepsilon_{2}})*(\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi_{x}^{\lambda}),(\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}}-\rho_{\varepsilon_{2}})*(\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi_{x}^{\lambda})\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

We set

\bm{\varepsilon}_{2}^{\partial}(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2};\lambda,x)\vcentcolon=\lambda^{\alpha}\left\lvert\langle\gamma*(\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}}-\rho_{\varepsilon_{2}})*(\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi_{x}^{\lambda}),(\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}}-\rho_{\varepsilon_{2}})*(\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi_{x}^{\lambda})\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right\rvert.

One has

\lim_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\downarrow 0}\langle\gamma*\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}}*(\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi_{x}^{\lambda}),\rho_{\varepsilon_{2}}*(\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi_{x}^{\lambda})\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\langle\gamma\ast(\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi_{x}^{\lambda}),(\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi_{x}^{\lambda})\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

and hence
\lim_{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\downarrow 0}\bm{\varepsilon}_{2}^{\partial}(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2};\lambda,x)=0
.
To prove the bounds (
31
) and (
32
), it suffices to show

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1),\lambda\in(0,1),x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lambda^{\alpha}\left\lvert\langle\gamma*\rho_{\varepsilon}*(\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi_{x}^{\lambda}),\rho_{\varepsilon}*(\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi_{x}^{\lambda})\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right\rvert<\infty.

Let us write

\displaystyle U_{x}^{\lambda}=\lambda^{-1}(U-x).

(37)

By (
36
) (remember that
\phi^{\lambda}_{x}\vcentcolon=\lambda^{-d}\phi(\lambda^{-1}(\cdot-x))
)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lvert\langle\gamma*\rho_{\varepsilon}*(\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi_{x}^{\lambda}),\rho_{\varepsilon}*(\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi_{x}^{\lambda})\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lvert\langle\gamma(\lambda\cdot)\ast(\mathbbm{1}_{U_{x}^{\lambda}}\phi)\ast\rho_{\varepsilon/\lambda},(\mathbbm{1}_{U_{x}^{\lambda}}\phi)\ast\rho_{\varepsilon/\lambda}\rangle\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{-\alpha}\langle f\ast\lvert\phi\rvert\ast\lvert\rho_{\varepsilon/\lambda}\rvert,\lvert\phi\rvert\ast\lvert\rho_{\varepsilon/\lambda}\rvert\rangle+\langle g(\lambda\cdot)\ast\lvert\phi\rvert\ast\lvert\rho_{\varepsilon/\lambda}\rvert,\lvert\phi\rvert\ast\lvert\rho_{\varepsilon/\lambda}\rvert\rangle.

Since, using Young’s inequality, one can bound the second term by

\lVert g\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\lVert\rho\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2},

it comes down to showing

\sup_{\mu\in(0,\infty)}\langle f\ast\lvert\phi\rvert\ast\lvert\rho_{\mu}\rvert,\lvert\phi\rvert\ast\lvert\rho_{\mu}\rvert\rangle<\infty.

\bullet
Suppose
\mu\leq 1
. Let
\sigma>d
. By the weighted Young’s inequality, Theorem
A.2
, one has

\langle f\ast\lvert\phi\rvert\ast\lvert\rho_{\mu}\rvert,\lvert\phi\rvert\ast\lvert\rho_{\mu}\rvert\rangle\lesssim_{\sigma}\lVert w_{\sigma}f\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lVert w_{\sigma}\phi\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\lVert w_{-\sigma}\rho_{\mu}\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}.

As
\phi
is a continuous function with compact support, we have
\lVert w_{\sigma}\phi\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty
.
Since
f
is locally integrable and satisfies the scaling property,

\lVert w_{\sigma}f\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\int_{\partial B(0,1)}\lvert f(x)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}S(x)\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{d-1-\alpha}(1+r^{2})^{-\frac{\sigma}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}r<\infty.

Then, we observe, as
\mu\leq 1
,

\lVert w_{-\sigma}\rho_{\mu}\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\mu^{2}\lvert x\rvert^{2})^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\lvert\rho\rvert(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2})^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\lvert\rho\rvert(x)\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

\bullet
Now suppose
\mu\geq 1
.
By change of variables,

\displaystyle\langle f\ast\lvert\phi\rvert\ast\lvert\rho_{\mu}\rvert,\lvert\phi\rvert\ast\lvert\rho_{\mu}\rvert\rangle

\displaystyle=\mu^{-\alpha}\langle f\ast\lvert\phi_{\mu^{-1}}\rvert\ast\lvert\rho\rvert,\lvert\phi_{\mu^{-1}}\rvert\ast\lvert\rho\rvert\rangle.

Therefore, it reduces to the case
\mu\leq 1
.
∎

Example 3.14
.

An example of a
\gamma
that satisfies the conditions of Lemma
3.13
is the following.
Let
n\in\{1,\dots,d\}
and
d_{1},\dots,d_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
d=d_{1}+\cdots+d_{n}
.
Let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\in(0,\infty)
are such that

\alpha_{j}<d_{j}
for all
j
and
\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}<3
. Then, for
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
,
we set

\gamma(x)\vcentcolon=\lvert x_{1}\rvert^{-\alpha_{1}}\cdots\lvert x_{n}\rvert^{-\alpha_{n}}.

For this example,
f=\gamma
and
g=0
. Observe that for the local integrability of
f
, the condition
\alpha_{j}<d_{j}
for all
j
is necessary.

Example 3.15
.

The 2D white noise
\xi^{2\textnormal{D}}
does not satisfy the conditions of Lemma
3.13
.
However, one has

\mathbb{E}[\langle\xi_{\varepsilon_{1}}^{2\textnormal{D}}-\xi_{\varepsilon_{2}}^{2\textnormal{D}},\mathbbm{1}_{U}\phi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle^{2}]=\lambda^{-2}\lVert(\mathbbm{1}_{U_{x}^{\lambda}}\phi)\ast(\rho_{\varepsilon_{1}/\lambda}-\rho_{\varepsilon_{2}/\lambda})\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

where
U_{x}^{\lambda}
is as in (
37
).
Therefore, the 2D white noise satisfies the condition of Lemma
3.12
and therefore the Neumann condition
3.2

(II)

as well.

4
Analysis of symmetric forms

It is common practice in the theory of rough paths
[
49
]
to first show the existence of sufficiently many stochastic objects and then apply deterministic analysis to derive results. In this section we consider the (deterministic) analysis of symmetric forms, which we use in Section
5
in combination with Assumptions
3.2
to construct the Anderson Hamiltonian and derive its spectral properties.

First we recall the definition of a symmetric form and some related definitions in Definition
4.1
, then, in Definition
4.2
, we describe the symmetric forms
\mathcal{E}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}^{U}
that we will study in Sections
4.2
and
4.3
.
In Section
4.1
we study
examples of bounded symmetric forms.
In Section
4.2
we study basic spectral properties of the symmetric forms and their associated self adjoint operators.
And in Section
4.3
we consider estimates of eigenvalues.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space over
\mathbb{R}
.
A bilinear map
\mathcal{Q}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})\times\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, with
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})
a dense subspace of
H
, is called a
symmetric form
on
H
if
\mathcal{Q}(u,v)=\mathcal{Q}(v,u)
for all
u,v\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})
.
Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a symmetric form on
H
. We write

\llbracket\mathcal{Q}\rrbracket_{H}\vcentcolon=\sup_{u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}),\lVert u\rVert_{H}=1}\lvert\mathcal{Q}(u,u)\rvert.

(38)

If
\llbracket\mathcal{Q}\rrbracket_{H}<\infty
, then we call
\mathcal{Q}
a
bounded symmetric form
. In that case, without loss of generality we assume
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})=H
.
The set of bounded symmetric forms a Banach space under the norm
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{H}
.
Then, a sequence
(\mathcal{Z}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of bounded symmetric forms converges to a bounded symmetric form
\mathcal{Z}
if

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathcal{Z}_{n}-\mathcal{Z}\rrbracket_{H}\rightarrow 0.

Let
M>0
. A symmetric form
\mathcal{Q}
is called
M
-bounded from below
if

\mathcal{Q}(u,u)+M\lVert u\rVert_{H}^{2}\geq 0
for all
u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})
.
It is called
bounded from below
if it is
M
-bounded from below for some
M>0
.
If
\mathcal{Q}
is
M
-bounded from below and

(\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}),\mathcal{Q}+M\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H})

is a Hilbert space for some
M>0
, then
\mathcal{Q}
is said to be
closed
.
If
\mathcal{Q}
is a closed symmetric form and
M
is as above, then a subset of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})
is called a
core
for
\mathcal{Q}
if it is dense in the Hilbert space
(\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}),\mathcal{Q}+M\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H})
.

Observe that a symmetric form is determined by its values on the diagonal of
H\times H
, i.e.,
\mathcal{Q}(u,v)=\frac{1}{2}[\mathcal{Q}(u+v,u+v)-\mathcal{Q}(u,u)-\mathcal{Q}(v,v)]
. For this reason we often only define symmetric forms on the diagonal.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
U
be a bounded domain,

W\in L^{\infty}(U)
and
\mathcal{Z}
be a bounded symmetric form on
H^{s}(U)
for some
s\in[0,1)
.
We define the symmetric form
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}^{U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}
on
e^{W}H^{1}(U)
as follows: for
u=e^{W}u^{\flat}
with

u^{\flat}\in H^{1}(U)
, we set

\mathcal{E}(u,u)\vcentcolon=\mathcal{E}^{U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}(u,u)\vcentcolon=\int_{U}e^{2W(x)}\lvert\nabla u^{\flat}(x)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{Z}(u^{\flat},u^{\flat}).

4.1
Main examples of bounded symmetric forms

Recall the notation
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
from (
38
) and the constants in Definition
A.14
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\sigma\in(0,\infty)

and
s\in(1-\delta,1)
.

(a)

Let
Y\in\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
For any bounded domain
U
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\phi=1
on a neighborhood of
U
, the formula

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{Y}^{U}(v,v)=\langle\phi Y,\mathbbm{1}_{U}v^{2}\rangle

defines a bounded symmetric form on
H^{s}_{0}(U)

and if
U
is moreover Lipschitz, it also defines a bounded symmetric form on
H^{s}(U)
.
The symmetric form
\mathcal{Z}_{Y}^{U}
is independent of the choice of
\phi
.
Moreover, for
L\geq 1

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathcal{Z}_{Y}^{U_{L}}\rrbracket_{H^{s}_{0}(U_{L})}\lesssim_{\delta,\varepsilon,U}L^{2\sigma}\lVert Y\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

(39)

and if
U
is a bounded Lipschitz domain, then

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathcal{Z}_{Y}^{U_{L}}\rrbracket_{H^{s}(U_{L})}\lesssim_{\delta,\varepsilon,U}L^{2\sigma}\lVert Y\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

(40)

(b)

Let
U
be a bounded Lipschitz domain. Suppose that
\delta\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
and
s\in(\frac{3}{2}-\delta,1)
.
Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\delta-\frac{1}{2})
,
p\in(2,\infty)
and
q\in(1,2)
be such that
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
and

\displaystyle\beta\vcentcolon=2-\delta+\varepsilon-\frac{1}{q}\leq\frac{1}{2},\qquad 2-\delta+\varepsilon-\frac{1}{2q}+\frac{d}{2p}\leq s.

(41)

Let
\tilde{Y}\in B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\operatorname{supp}(\tilde{Y})\subseteq\partial U
,
\lVert\tilde{Y}_{\varepsilon}-\tilde{Y}\rVert_{B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}0
for some
\tilde{Y}_{\varepsilon}
given by
\varphi\mapsto\int_{\partial U}\varphi f_{\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}S
for
f_{\varepsilon}\in L^{1}(\partial U)
,
and
V\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta}(U)
.
Then, with
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{W_{2q}^{\beta+\frac{1}{2q}}(U)}
,
\mathcal{R}:W_{q}^{\beta-\varepsilon}(\partial U)\rightarrow W_{q}^{2-\delta}(U)
a right inverse of
\mathcal{T}_{W_{q}^{2-\delta}(U)}

and
\iota
a universal extension operator from
U
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as in Lemma
2.8
,

\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(v,v)\vcentcolon=\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{\tilde{Y},V}^{U}(v,v)\vcentcolon=\langle\tilde{Y},\iota\circ\mathcal{R}[V(\mathcal{T}v)^{2}]\rangle

defines a bounded symmetric form on
H^{s}(U)
that is independent of the choice of
\mathcal{R}
and
\iota
.

If
\tilde{Y}_{L}\in B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\operatorname{supp}(\tilde{Y}_{L})\subseteq\partial U_{L}

and
V_{L}\in\mathcal{C}^{\delta}(U_{L})
for
L\geq 1
, then

\displaystyle\llbracket\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{U_{L}}_{\tilde{Y}_{L},V_{L}}\rrbracket_{H^{s}(U_{L})}\lesssim_{\delta,\varepsilon,p,U}L^{2\varepsilon}\lVert V_{L}\rVert_{C^{\delta}(U_{L})}\lVert\tilde{Y_{L}}\rVert_{B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

(42)

(c)

Let
U
be a bounded Lipschitz domain. Suppose
\delta\in(0,1)
and
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
.
Let
\widehat{Y}\in\mathcal{C}^{1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
V\in\mathcal{C}^{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then, with
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{H^{s}(U)}
as in Lemma
2.8
(for the notation see Definition
1.13
),

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{U}_{\widehat{Y},V}(v,v)\vcentcolon=\int_{\partial U}(\mathcal{T}v)^{2}V\nabla\widehat{Y}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\bm{S}

defines a
bounded symmetric form on
H^{s}(U)
for every
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
. Moreover, for
L\geq 1

\llbracket\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{U_{L}}_{\widehat{Y},V}\rrbracket_{H^{s}(U_{L})}\lesssim_{\delta,\sigma,U}L^{\sigma}\lVert V\rVert_{C^{\delta}(U_{L})}\lVert\widehat{Y}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

(43)

Proof.

(a)

Let us first consider
U
to be a bounded Lipschitz domain and
v\in H^{s}(U_{L})
. We comment on how to obtain (
39
) afterwards.
Let
\phi
be as in the statement.
It is rather straightforward to check that the definition of
\mathcal{Z}_{Y}^{U}
does not depend on
\phi
.
Observe that therefore
\mathcal{Z}_{Y}^{U_{L}}(v,v)=\langle\phi(L^{-1}\cdot)Y,\mathbbm{1}_{U_{L}}v^{2}\rangle
for all
L>0
.
Choose a
p\in[1,\infty]
and an
\varepsilon\in(0,\delta)
such that
1-\delta+\varepsilon+\frac{d}{2p}\leq s
and
p\sigma>d
.
By the duality of Besov spaces
[
63
, Theorem 2.17]
, for
q\in[1,\infty]
such that
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
, we have

\lvert\langle\phi(L^{-1}\cdot)Y,\mathbbm{1}_{U_{L}}v^{2}\rangle\rvert\lesssim_{\delta,p}\lVert\phi(L^{-1}\cdot)Y\rVert_{B^{-1+\delta}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{U_{L}}v^{2}\rVert_{B_{q,q}^{1-\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

By Lemma
A.4
and Lemma
A.1
(more specifically, (
75
) using that
p\sigma>d
) we have

\displaystyle\lVert\phi(L^{-1}\cdot)Y\rVert_{B^{-1+\delta}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{p,\delta,\sigma,\phi}L^{2\sigma}\lVert Y\rVert_{B^{-1+\delta,2\sigma}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{p,\delta,\sigma}L^{2\sigma}\lVert Y\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Then by Lemma
2.7
(see Definition
A.14
for
C_{\operatorname{Mult}}
and
C_{\operatorname{Prod}}
)

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{U_{L}}v^{2}\rVert_{B_{q,q}^{1-\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\delta,p}\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{U_{L}}v^{2}\rVert_{W_{q}^{1-\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C_{\operatorname{Mult}}^{U_{L}}[W_{q}^{1-\delta}]\lVert v^{2}\rVert_{W_{q}^{1-\delta}(U_{L})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\operatorname{Mult}}^{U_{L}}[W_{q}^{1-\delta}]C_{\operatorname{Prod}}^{U_{L}}[W^{1-\delta+\varepsilon}_{2q}\to W^{1-\delta}_{q}]\lVert v\rVert^{2}_{W^{1-\delta+\varepsilon}_{2q}(U_{L})}.

Now we apply the embedding estimate (see Definition
A.14
for
C_{\operatorname{Embed}}
) and the estimate
\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1-\delta+\varepsilon+\frac{d}{2p}}(U_{L})}\lesssim_{\delta,\varepsilon,p}\lVert u\rVert_{H^{s}(U_{L})}
(as
1-\delta+\varepsilon+\frac{d}{2p}\leq s
), we have

\lVert v\rVert_{W_{2q}^{1-\delta+\varepsilon}(U_{L})}\lesssim_{\delta,\varepsilon,p}C_{\operatorname{Embed}}^{U_{L}}[H^{1-\delta+\varepsilon+\frac{d}{2p}}\to W_{2q}^{1-\delta+\varepsilon}]\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}(U_{L})}.

Hence

\llbracket\mathcal{Z}\rrbracket_{H^{s}(U_{L})}\lesssim_{\delta,\varepsilon,p,\sigma}L^{2\sigma}C^{U_{L}}_{\operatorname{Mult}}[W_{q}^{1-\delta}]C_{\operatorname{Prod}}^{U_{L}}[H^{1-\delta+\varepsilon}\to W^{1-\delta}_{1}]\\
\times C_{\operatorname{Embed}}^{U_{L}}[H^{s}\to W_{2q}^{1-\delta+\varepsilon}]\lVert Y\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Therefore (
40
) follows by Lemma
A.18
.
If
U
is not necessarily Lipschitz but
v\in H^{s}_{0}(U)
, in the above estimates, we can replace the constant
C_{\operatorname{Mult}}^{U}[W_{q}^{1-\delta}]
by
1
and the estimate (
39
) follows similarly.

(b)

First, observe that the requirements for the existence of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{R}
as in Lemma
2.8
are satisfied. Indeed,
\beta+\frac{1}{2q}\in(\frac{1}{2q},1+\frac{1}{2q})
, or equivalently,
\beta\in(0,1)
, and
2-\delta\in(\frac{1}{q},1+\frac{1}{q})
: On the one hand we have
2-\delta\in(1,\frac{3}{2})\subseteq(\frac{1}{q},1+\frac{1}{q})
and
because

\frac{1}{q}\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
,
on the other hand we have

\beta=2-\delta+\varepsilon-\frac{1}{q}>2-\delta-1=1-\delta>0
and
\beta\leq\frac{1}{2}
by assumption.

Again by the duality, we have

\lvert\langle\tilde{Y},\iota\circ\mathcal{R}[V(\mathcal{T}v)^{2}]\rangle\rvert\lesssim_{\delta,p}\lVert\tilde{Y}\rVert_{B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lVert\iota\circ\mathcal{R}[V(\mathcal{T}v)^{2}]\rVert_{B_{q,q}^{2-\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

(44)

Let us use this estimate first to show that
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}
is independent of the choice of
\mathcal{R}
and
\iota
, as we will use this for our norm estimates. For
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\overline{U})\cap W_{p}^{r}(U)
and
\mathcal{V}\in C^{\infty}(\overline{U})
the function
\iota\circ\mathcal{R}[\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{T}\varphi)^{2}]
is equal to
\mathcal{V}\varphi^{2}
on
\partial U
and thus for
\varepsilon>0

\displaystyle\int_{\partial U}f_{\varepsilon}(\iota\circ\mathcal{R}[\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{T}\varphi)^{2}])\,\mathrm{d}S=\int_{\partial U}f_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{V}\varphi^{2}\,\mathrm{d}S.

By the above estimates we have already seen that
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{U}_{\tilde{Y},V}(v)
is continuous as a function of
\tilde{Y}
,
V
and
v
. As
C^{\infty}(\overline{U})\cap W_{p}^{r}(U)
is dense in
W_{p}^{r}(U)
,
V
is the limit of smooth functions in
C^{\infty}(\overline{U})
and
\tilde{Y}
is the limit of
\tilde{Y}_{\varepsilon}
, it therefore follows that
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}
is independent of the choice of
\mathcal{R}
and
\iota
.

We continue by estimating the right-hand side of (
44
).
Again, by using Lemma
2.7
(remember that
\mathcal{R}
is a bounded linear operator
W_{q}^{\beta-\varepsilon}(\partial U)\rightarrow W_{q}^{2-\delta}(U)
),

\lVert\iota\circ\mathcal{R}[V(\mathcal{T}v)^{2}]\rVert_{B_{q,q}^{2-\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{\delta,p}\lVert\iota\rVert_{W_{q}^{2-\delta}(U)\rightarrow W_{q}^{2-\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lVert\mathcal{R}\rVert\lVert V(\mathcal{T}v)^{2}\rVert_{W_{q}^{\beta-\varepsilon}(\partial U)}.

(45)

Now we estimate
\lVert V(\mathcal{T}v)^{2}\rVert_{W_{q}^{\beta-\varepsilon}(\partial U)}
.
Recall the notation
[\cdot]
from Definition
2.4

(b)
.
If we set
\psi\vcentcolon=(\mathcal{T}v)^{2}
, then

\displaystyle[V\psi]_{W^{\beta-\varepsilon}_{q}(\partial U)}\leq\Big(\int_{\partial U}\int_{\partial U}\frac{\lvert V(x)-V(y)\rvert^{q}\lvert\psi(x)\rvert^{q}}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d-1+q(\beta-\varepsilon)}}\,\mathrm{d}S(x)\,\mathrm{d}S(y)\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\Big(\int_{\partial U}\int_{\partial U}\frac{\lvert\psi(x)-\psi(y)\rvert^{q}\lvert V(x)\rvert^{q}}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d-1+q(\beta-\varepsilon)}}\,\mathrm{d}S(x)\,\mathrm{d}S(y)\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\beta}\lVert V\rVert_{W_{\infty}^{\beta}(U)}\Big(\int_{\partial U}\int_{\partial U}\frac{\lvert\psi(x)\rvert^{q}}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d-1-q\varepsilon}}\,\mathrm{d}S(x)\,\mathrm{d}S(y)\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}+\lVert V\rVert_{L^{\infty}(U)}[\psi]_{W^{\beta-\varepsilon}_{q}(\partial U)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\beta,\delta}\lVert V\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{\delta}(U)}\Big(1+\sup_{x\in\partial U}\int_{\partial U}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}S(y)}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d-1-q\varepsilon}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}\lVert\psi\rVert_{W^{\beta-\varepsilon}_{q}(\partial U)},

where we used
\lVert V\rVert_{W_{\infty}^{\beta}(U)}\vee\lVert V\rVert_{L^{\infty}(U)}\lesssim_{\beta,\delta}\lVert V\rVert_{W_{\infty}^{\delta}(U)}=\lVert V\rVert_{C^{\delta}(U)}
which holds because
\beta\leq\frac{1}{2}<\delta
.

Therefore, by observing (see Definition
A.14
for
C_{\operatorname{Prod}}
and
C_{\operatorname{Embed}}
)

\displaystyle\lVert\psi\rVert_{W^{\beta-\varepsilon}_{q}(\partial U)}=\lVert(\mathcal{T}v)^{2}\rVert_{W^{\beta-\varepsilon}_{q}(\partial U)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\operatorname{Prod}}^{\partial U}[W^{\beta}_{2q}\to W^{\beta-\varepsilon}_{q}]\lVert\mathcal{T}v\rVert_{W^{\beta}_{2q}(\partial U)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\operatorname{Prod}}^{\partial U}[W^{\beta}_{2q}\to W^{\beta-\varepsilon}_{q}]\lVert\mathcal{T}\rVert^{2}\lVert v\rVert_{W^{\beta+\frac{1}{2q}}_{2q}(U)}^{2}

and
\lVert v\rVert_{W^{\beta+\frac{1}{2q}}_{2q}(U)}\leq C_{\operatorname{Embed}}^{U}[H^{s}\to W_{2q}^{\beta+\frac{1}{2q}}]\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}(U)}
, we obtain (as we may take the infimum over
\iota
and
\mathcal{R}
)

\displaystyle\llbracket\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}\rrbracket_{H^{s}(U)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\delta,\varepsilon,p}C_{\operatorname{Prod}}^{\partial U}[W_{2q}^{\beta}\to W_{q}^{\beta-\varepsilon}]C_{\operatorname{Embed}}^{U}[H^{s}\to W^{\beta+\frac{1}{2q}}_{2q}]^{2}C_{\operatorname{R}}^{\partial U}[W_{q}^{2-\delta}]

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big(1+\sup_{x\in\partial U}\int_{\partial U}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}S(y)}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d-1-q\varepsilon}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}C^{U}_{\operatorname{Ext}}[W^{q}_{2-\delta}]\lVert\mathcal{T}\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\lVert V\rVert_{C^{\delta}(U)}\lVert\tilde{Y}\rVert_{B_{p,p}^{-2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

With this (
42
) follows from Lemma
A.18
(for the estimate on the
C_{\operatorname{Embed}}
we use the second inequality in (
41
)) and because

\displaystyle\int_{\partial U_{L}}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}S(y)}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d-1-q\varepsilon}}=\int_{\partial U}L^{d-1}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}S(y)}{\lvert Lx-Ly\rvert^{d-1-q\varepsilon}}=L^{q\varepsilon}\int_{\partial U}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}S(y)}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d-1-q\varepsilon}},

so that

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\partial U_{L}}\Big(1+\int_{\partial U_{L}}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}S(y)}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d-1-q\varepsilon}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq L^{\varepsilon}\sup_{x\in\partial U}\Big(1+\int_{\partial U}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}S(y)}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d-1-q\varepsilon}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}.

The latter supremum is finite:
\int_{\partial U}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}y}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d-1-q\varepsilon}}
is finite for all
x\in\partial U
due to the fact that
U
is a Lipschitz domain, so that by the compactness of
\partial U
and continuity of
x\mapsto\int_{\partial U}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}y}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d-1-q\varepsilon}}
it follows that the supremum is finite as well.
The other
L^{\varepsilon}
factor is due to the estimate
C^{\partial U_{L}}_{\operatorname{Prod}}[W_{2q}^{r}\to W_{q}^{r-\varepsilon}]\lesssim_{p,\varepsilon,U}L^{\varepsilon}
, see Lemma
A.18
.

(c)

First, observe that (for
\nu
being the outer normal on
\partial U
)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{U}_{\widehat{Y},V}(v,v)\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\int_{\partial U}(\mathcal{T}v)^{2}V\nabla_{\nu}\widehat{Y}\,\mathrm{d}S\right\rvert\leq\lVert V\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\partial U)}\lVert\nabla\widehat{Y}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\partial U)}\lVert\mathcal{T}v\rVert_{L^{2}(\partial U)}^{2}.

Secondly, use
\lVert\mathcal{T}v\rVert_{L^{2}(\partial U)}\leq\lVert\mathcal{T}v\rVert_{W_{2}^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial U)}\leq\lVert\mathcal{T}_{H^{s}(U)}\rVert\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}(U)}
,
\lVert V\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\partial U)}\leq\lVert V\rVert_{C^{\delta}(U)}
and

\lVert\nabla\widehat{Y}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\partial U_{L})}\leq\lVert\phi(L^{-1}\cdot)\nabla\widehat{Y}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\lVert\phi(L^{-1}\cdot)\nabla\widehat{Y}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{\delta,\sigma,\phi}L^{\sigma}\lVert\widehat{Y}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1+\delta,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, where the last inequality is due to Lemma
A.4
. (
43
) then follows by Lemma
A.18
(observe that we use that
s>\frac{1}{2}
in order to have
\sup_{L\geq 1}\lVert\mathcal{T}_{H^{s}(U_{L})}\rVert<\infty
).
∎

4.2
Basic spectral properties

In this section, we will work in the setting of Definition
4.2
. More precisely:

Assumptions 4.4

(for this section)
.

In this section, we fix a bounded domain
U
, a function
W\in L^{\infty}(U)
, and a symmetric form
\mathcal{Z}
on
H^{s}(U)
for some
s\in(0,1)
.

The goal is to introduce the Dirichlet and Neumann operators (see Definition
4.8
below) corresponding to
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}^{U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}
(see Definition
4.2
) and study their spectral properties.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a symmetric form on a Hilbert space
H
.
Let
D
be the set of
u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})
such that there exists a
\tilde{u}\in H
such that
\mathcal{Q}(u,v)=\langle\tilde{u},v\rangle_{H}
for all
v\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})
. For such
u
the element
\tilde{u}
is unique, and we will write
Au=\tilde{u}
.
Then
A
on
D
forms a linear operator on
H
, called the
operator associated with

\mathcal{Q}
.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
U
be a bounded Lipschitz domain and
s\in(0,1)
. We define

\displaystyle C^{U}_{\operatorname{IP}}[H^{s}]\vcentcolon=\sup_{f\in H^{1}(U)\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}(U)}}{\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}(U)}^{1-s}\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}(U)}^{s}}.

If
U
is a bounded domain that is not necessarily Lipschitz, we define
C^{U}_{\operatorname{IP}}[H^{s}]
similarly as above by replacing “
H^{a}(U)
” by “
H^{a}_{0}(U)
” for
a
being either
s
or
1
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
s\in(0,1)
.
Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a bounded symmetric form on
H^{s}_{0}(U)
.
Then, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
and
v\in H^{1}_{0}(U)
, we have

\lvert\mathcal{Z}(v,v)\rvert\leq\delta\int_{U}|\nabla v|^{2}+\Big(\delta+\delta^{-\frac{s}{1-s}}C^{U}_{\operatorname{IP}}[H^{s}_{0}]^{\frac{2}{1-s}}\llbracket\mathcal{Z}\rrbracket_{H_{0}^{s}(U)}^{\frac{1}{1-s}}\Big)\lVert v\rVert_{L^{2}(U)}^{2}.

If
\mathcal{Z}
is a bounded symmetric form on
H^{s}(U)
, then the above statement holds with
H^{s}_{0}
replaced by
H^{s}
.

Proof.

We only prove the claim for a bounded Lipschitz domain.
One has
\lvert\mathcal{Z}(v,v)\rvert\leq\llbracket\mathcal{Z}\rrbracket_{H^{s}(U)}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}(U)}^{2}
.
By interpolation and Young’s inequality (using that
a^{s}b^{1-s}\leq a+b
),

\lVert v\rVert^{2}_{H^{s}(U)}\leq C_{\operatorname{IP}}^{U}[H^{s}]^{2}\lVert v\rVert^{2s}_{H^{1}(U)}\lVert v\rVert^{2(1-s)}_{L^{2}(U)}\leq C_{\operatorname{IP}}^{U}[H^{s}]^{2}(\eta\lVert v\rVert^{2}_{H^{1}(U)}+\eta^{-\frac{s}{1-s}}\lVert v\rVert_{L^{2}(U)}^{2})

for any
\eta\in(0,\infty)
.
Therefore,

\lvert\mathcal{Z}(v,v)\rvert\leq\eta\llbracket\mathcal{Z}\rrbracket_{H^{s}(U)}C_{\operatorname{IP}}^{U}[H^{s}]^{2}\lVert v\rVert^{2}_{H^{1}(U)}+\eta^{-\frac{s}{1-s}}\llbracket\mathcal{Z}\rrbracket_{H^{s}(U)}C_{\operatorname{IP}}^{U}[H^{s}]^{2}\lVert v\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(U)}.

We can choose
\eta
so that
\delta=\eta\llbracket\mathcal{Z}\rrbracket_{H^{s}(U)}C_{\operatorname{IP}}^{U}[H^{s}]^{2}
and use that
\|v\|_{H^{1}(U)}^{2}=\|v\|_{L^{2}(U)}^{2}+\int_{U}|\nabla v|^{2}
.
∎

Definition 4.8
.

If
\mathcal{Z}
is a bounded symmetric form on
H_{0}^{1}(U)
, then we write
\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}
for
\mathcal{E}^{U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}
(recall Definition
4.2
) with
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}})=e^{W}H_{0}^{1}(U)
and let
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}=\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}=\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}
be the operator associated with
\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}
on
L^{2}(U)
.

If
\mathcal{Z}
is a bounded symmetric form on
H^{1}(U)
, then we write
\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}
for
\mathcal{E}^{U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}
with
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}})=e^{W}H^{1}(U)
and let
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}=\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}=\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}
be the operator associated with
\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}
on
L^{2}(U)
.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
W\in L^{\infty}(U)
and
\mathcal{Z}
be a bounded symmetric form on
H^{s}_{0}(U)
for some
s\in(0,1)
.
Then
\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}
is closed and
e^{W}C^{\infty}_{c}(U)
is a core. Consequently,
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}
is self-adjoint.

If
\mathcal{Z}
instead is a bounded symmetric form on
H^{s}(U)
, then
\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}
is closed and
e^{W}C^{\infty}(\overline{U})
is a core.
Consequently,
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}
is self-adjoint.

Proof.

In view of Lemma
4.7
and the symmetric form version of the Kato-Rellich theorem
[
42
, Theorem 1.33 in Chapter VI]
,
we can assume
\mathcal{Z}=0
. Observe that for
u=e^{W}u^{\flat}
,
u^{\flat}\in H^{1}(U)

e^{-2\lVert W\rVert_{L^{\infty}(U)}}\mathcal{E}_{0,0}(u^{\flat},u^{\flat})\leq\mathcal{E}_{W,0}(u,u)\leq e^{2\lVert W\rVert_{L^{\infty}(U)}}\mathcal{E}_{0,0}(u^{\flat},u^{\flat}).

Therefore the claim follows as
\mathcal{E}_{0,0}
is closed and
C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
is a core for
\mathcal{E}_{0,0}
.

The self-adjointness of the corresponding operators follows as they are closed densely defined and symmetric, cf.
[
19
, Section 4.4]
).
∎

By applying a standard result from the spectral theory, we can easily show that the spectrum of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}
on a bounded domain
and that of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}
on a bounded Lipschitz domain are discrete and that the min-max formula (also known as the Courant-Fischer formula) holds for the eigenvalues.

Proposition 4.10
.

The spectrum of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}
is given by a sequence of eigenvalues
(\lambda^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
counting multiplicities, such that
\lambda^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}_{1}\leq\lambda^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}_{2}\leq\cdots
. Moreover, by using the notation
\sqsubset
for “is a linear subspace of”,

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}\vcentcolon=\lambda_{k}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}(U;W,\mathcal{Z})\vcentcolon=

\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\sqsubset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}})\\
\dim L=k\end{subarray}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in L\\
\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}(U)}=1\end{subarray}}\langle\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(U)},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\sqsubset e^{W}H^{1}_{0}(U)\\
\dim L=k\end{subarray}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in L\\
\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}(U)}=1\end{subarray}}\mathcal{E}^{U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}(u,u),

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\sqsubset e^{W}C^{\infty}_{c}(U)\\
\dim L=k\end{subarray}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in L\\
\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}(U)}=1\end{subarray}}\mathcal{E}^{U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}(u,u)

and
\lim_{k\to\infty}\lambda_{k}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}=\infty
.
In particular,
(\lambda-\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}})^{-1}
is a compact operator for all
\lambda
that are not in the spectrum of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}
.
If
U
is a bounded Lipschitz domain,
an analogous statement for

\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}
holds if
H^{1}_{0}(U)
and
C^{\infty}_{c}(U)
are replaced by
H^{1}(U)
and
C^{\infty}(\overline{U})
.

Proof.

By well-known results of spectral theory (see e.g.,
[
19
, Corollary 4.2.3, Theorem 4.5.2, Theorem 4.5.3]
in combination with Proposition
4.9
), it suffices to show that the form domain is compactly embedded in
L^{2}(U)
, which follows from the compact embeddings of Sobolev spaces (see
[
10
, Theorem 8.11.2]
for the fact that
the embedding
H^{1}_{0}(U)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(U)
is compact for any bounded domain
U

[
10
, Theorem 8.11.4]
for the fact that
the embedding
H^{1}(U)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(U)
is compact for any bounded Lipschitz domain
U
).
∎

We show continuous dependence of the spectral structure with respect to
W
and
\mathcal{Z}
.
This follows from the result of
[
47
]
.

Definition 4.11
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space and
M>0
. Let
(\mathcal{Q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathcal{Q}
be closed symmetric forms that are

M
-bounded from below.
We use the following convention: if
u\notin\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})
, then we set
\mathcal{Q}(u,u)\vcentcolon=\infty
.

(a)

[
47
, Definition 2.8]
We say the sequence
(\mathcal{Q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

\Gamma
-converges
to
\mathcal{Q}
, if the following hold:

(a)

If the sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
u
in
H
, then

\mathcal{Q}(u,u)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{Q}_{n}(u_{n},u_{n}).

(46)

(b)

For any
u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q})
, there exists a sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
H
such that

u_{n}\to u\text{ in }H\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{Q}_{n}(u_{n},u_{n})=\mathcal{Q}(u,u).

(b)

[
47
, Definition 2.12]
The sequence
(\mathcal{Q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is said to be
compact
if
the condition

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{Q}_{n}(u_{n},u_{n})+(M+1)\lVert u_{n}\rVert_{H}^{2}<\infty

implies
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is precompact in
H
, that is the sequence has a converges subsequence in
H
.

(c)

[
47
, Definition 2.13]
We say the sequence
(\mathcal{Q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

converges compactly

to
\mathcal{Q}
if
(\mathcal{Q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

\Gamma
-converges to
\mathcal{Q}
and
if
(\mathcal{Q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is compact. In that case, we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\xrightarrow{\rm{compact}}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{Q}.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space and
M>0
.
Suppose that
(\mathcal{Q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of closed quadratic forms on
H
that are
M
-bounded from below and converges compactly to
\mathcal{Q}
.
Let
A_{n}
(resp.
A
) be the self-adjoint operator associated with
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
(resp.
\mathcal{Q}
).

(a)

[
47
, Theorem 2.4 and Theorem 2.5]
For any bounded continuous function
f
on
\mathbb{R}
, we have
\lVert f(A_{n})-f(A)\rVert_{H\to H}\to 0
. In particular,
A_{n}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{NR}}_{n\rightarrow\infty}A
(see Definition
1.6
).

(b)

[
47
, Corollary 2.5]
Let
\lambda_{k,n}
(resp.
\lambda_{k}
) be the
k
-th eigenvalue of
A_{n}
(resp.
A
), counting multiplicities.
Then, we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{k,n}=\lambda_{k}
for any
k
.
Moreover, for any
k
there exist (a choice of the)
k
-th eigenfunctions
\phi_{k,n}
(resp.
\phi_{k}
) of
A_{n}
(resp.
A
) such that

\phi_{k,n}
converges to
\phi_{k}
in
H
.

Remark 4.13
.

In the proof of the following theorem we use the following elementary fact.
If
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence in
\mathbb{R}
and
\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}a_{n}<\infty
, then there exists a strictly increasing function
\varphi:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that
\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}a_{\varphi(n)}=\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}a_{n}
and
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}a_{\varphi(n)}<\infty
.

In the following theorem we consider the compact convergence for the symmetric forms
\mathcal{E}_{W_{n},\mathcal{Z}_{n}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}
on
H_{0}^{s}(U)
and the symmetric forms
\mathcal{E}_{W_{n},\mathcal{Z}_{n}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}
on
H^{s}(U)
as defined in Definition
4.8
.

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
s\in[0,1)
.
Suppose that
W_{n}\rightarrow W
in
C(\overline{U})
.

•

If
\llbracket\mathcal{Z}_{n}-\mathcal{Z}\rrbracket_{H^{s}_{0}(U)}\xrightarrow{n\rightarrow\infty}0
,
then

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{W_{n},\mathcal{Z}_{n}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}\xrightarrow{\rm{compact}}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{E}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}.

•

If
U
is a bounded Lipschitz domain and
\llbracket\mathcal{Z}_{n}-\mathcal{Z}\rrbracket_{H^{s}(U)}\xrightarrow{n\rightarrow\infty}0
,
then

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{W_{n},\mathcal{Z}_{n}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}\xrightarrow{\rm{compact}}_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{E}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}.

Proof.

We only prove the second statement.
We first show that
(\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}_{W_{n},\mathcal{Z}_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is compact. Suppose
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}_{W_{n},\mathcal{Z}_{n}}(u_{n},u_{n})+(M+1)\lVert u_{n}\rVert_{L^{2}(U)}^{2}<\infty
. We set
u_{n}^{\flat}\vcentcolon=e^{-W_{n}}u_{n}
.
By Lemma
4.7
, we have
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert u^{\flat}_{n}\rVert_{H^{1}(U)}<\infty

because for any
\delta\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}_{W_{n},\mathcal{Z}_{n}}(u_{n},u_{n})=\int_{U}e^{2W_{n}}\lvert\nabla u^{\flat}_{n}\rvert^{2}+\mathcal{Z}_{n}(u_{n}^{\flat},u_{n}^{\flat})

\displaystyle\geq e^{-2\|W_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}}(1-\delta)\int_{U}\lvert\nabla u^{\flat}_{n}\rvert^{2}-\Big(\delta+C\delta^{-\frac{s}{1-s}}\llbracket\mathcal{Z}_{n}\rrbracket_{H^{s}(U)}^{\frac{1}{1-s}}\Big)\|u_{n}^{\flat}\|_{L^{2}(U)}^{2},

and thus
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert u_{n}^{\flat}\rVert_{H^{1}(U)}<\infty
as
W_{n}\rightarrow W
in
C(\overline{U})
.
Since the embedding
H^{1}(U)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(U)
is compact, the sequence
(u_{n}^{\flat})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is also precompact in
L^{2}(U)
.
As
u_{n}=e^{W_{n}}u_{n}^{\flat}
and
\|W_{n}-W\|_{L^{\infty}(\overline{U})}\rightarrow 0
, also
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is precompact in
L^{2}(U)
.

Next we show that
(\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}_{W_{n},\mathcal{Z}_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

\Gamma
-converges to
\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}
.
Since (ii) of Definition
4.11

(a)
is trivial, we focus on showing (i) Definition
4.11

(a)
.

Suppose that
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
u
in
L^{2}(U)
.
As we want to show (
46
), we may assume
\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{E}_{W_{n},\mathcal{Z}_{n}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}(u_{n},u_{n})<\infty
and by Remark
4.13
we may as well assume
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{E}_{W_{n},\mathcal{Z}_{n}}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}(u_{n},u_{n})<\infty
(by possibly considering a subsequence), so that by the above
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert u_{n}^{\flat}\rVert_{H^{1}(U)}<\infty
.
It suffices to show

\displaystyle\int_{U}e^{2W}\lvert\nabla u^{\flat}\rvert^{2}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{U}e^{2W_{n}}\lvert\nabla u^{\flat}_{n}\rvert^{2},

(47)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{n}(u^{\flat}_{n},u^{\flat}_{n})=\mathcal{Z}(u^{\flat},u^{\flat}).

(48)

Since the sequence
(u_{n}^{\flat})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in
H^{1}(U)
and converges to
u^{\flat}
in
L^{2}(U)
, by interpolation it converges to
u^{\flat}
in
H^{s}(U)
from which (
48
) follows.

For
v\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
,

\langle\nabla u^{\flat}_{n},v\rangle_{L^{2}(U)}=-\langle u^{\flat}_{n},\nabla v\rangle_{L^{2}(U)}\to-\langle u^{\flat},\nabla v\rangle_{L^{2}(U)}=\langle\nabla u^{\flat},v\rangle_{L^{2}(U)},

which implies that
(\nabla u_{n}^{\flat})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly to
\nabla u^{\flat}
in
L^{2}(U)
.
Therefore,
(e^{W_{n}}\nabla u_{n}^{\flat})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly to
e^{W}\nabla u^{\flat}
in
L^{2}(U)
and
this implies (
47
) (this follows for example by the dual representation of the norm on
L^{2}(U)
).
∎

4.3
Estimates of eigenvalues

This section serves as preparation for Section
5.3
on the integrated density of states and its results are not used for the construction of Anderson Hamiltonians (Theorem
1.7
and Thereom
1.8
).

Assumptions 4.15

(for this section)
.

We assume the following throughout this section:

•

W
is a continuous function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

•

s\in[0,1)
,
\mathcal{U}
is the collection of bounded Lipschitz domains.

•

\mathcal{Z}^{U}
is a bounded symmetric form on
H^{s}(U)
for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
.

For each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we let

\mathcal{E}^{U}=\mathcal{E}^{U}_{W,\mathcal{Z}^{U}}
be the symmetric form defined in Definition
4.2
.
Recall the notations
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}},U}
from
Definition
4.8
and
\lambda^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}_{k}(U)
,
\lambda^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}_{k}(U)
from Proposition
4.10
.

Remark 4.16
.

For Dirichlet boundary conditions we do not necessarily need to consider Lipschitz domains.
Indeed, if
\mathcal{U}
would instead be the collection of all bounded domains and
\mathcal{Z}^{U}
a bounded symmetric form on
H^{s}_{0}(U)
for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
, then the statements of Lemma
4.18
, Lemma
4.24

(a)
and Lemma
4.28

(a)
remain valid.

Definition 4.17
.

For
\#\in\{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}\}
and
U\in\mathcal{U}
, we define the
eigenvalue counting functions

\bm{N}^{\#}(U,\lambda)
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
by

\bm{N}^{\#}(U,\lambda)\vcentcolon=\bm{N}^{\#}_{W,\mathcal{Z}}(U,\lambda)\vcentcolon=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\lambda_{k}^{\#}(U;W,\mathcal{Z})\leq\lambda\}}.

We set
\bm{N}^{\#}_{0}(U,\lambda)\vcentcolon=\bm{N}^{\#}_{0,0}(U,\lambda)
, which is the eigenvalue counting function of the Neumann or the Dirichlet Laplacian on
U
.

For
L>R>0
we set

\displaystyle U_{L}^{R}\vcentcolon=U_{L}\cap B(\partial U_{L},R),\quad C(\partial U_{L},R)\vcentcolon=\{x\in U_{L}\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}d(x,\partial U_{L})=R\}.

(49)

(Observe
C(\partial U_{L},R)=\partial U_{L}^{R}\setminus\partial U_{L}
.)
We denote by
H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m},R}(U_{L}^{R})

the closure in
H^{1}(U_{L}^{R})
with respect to
H^{1}
-norm of the set

\{\phi\in C^{\infty}(\overline{U_{L}^{R}})\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\phi=0\mbox{ on a neighborhood of }C(\partial U_{L},R)\}.

Let
\bm{N}^{\mathfrak{m}}_{0}(U_{L}^{R},\lambda)
be the eigenvalue counting function of the operator associated with the symmetric form

(u,u)\mapsto\int_{U_{L}^{R}}\lvert\nabla u\rvert^{2}
with the domain
H^{1}_{\mathfrak{m},R}(U^{R}_{L})
.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
U,U_{1},U_{2}\in\mathcal{U}
,
U_{1}\subseteq U_{2}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

\displaystyle\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}(U,\lambda)

\displaystyle\leq\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}(U,\lambda),

\displaystyle\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}(U_{1},\lambda)

\displaystyle\leq\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}(U_{2},\lambda).

Proof.

Since
H^{1}_{0}(U)\subseteq H^{1}(U)
, the min-max formula (Lemma
4.10
) implies
\lambda_{k}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}(U)\geq\lambda_{k}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}(U)
for all
k
, and thus the first inequality. The second also follows by the min-max formula, as
H^{1}_{0}(U_{1})\subseteq H^{1}_{0}(U_{2})
.
∎

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
U\in\mathcal{U}
,
s\in(0,1)
,
\theta\in(0,\infty)
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.
We set

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\pm}_{\lambda,\theta}(W,\mathcal{Z})\vcentcolon=(1\pm\theta)e^{\pm 4\lVert W\rVert_{L^{\infty}(U)}}(\lambda\pm A_{\pm}),

where

A_{\pm}\vcentcolon=A_{\pm,\theta}^{W,\mathcal{Z}}\vcentcolon=\theta+\Big(\frac{\theta}{1\pm\theta}\Big)^{-\frac{s}{1-s}}C_{\operatorname{IP}}^{U}[H^{s}]^{\frac{2}{1-s}}e^{(2\pm\frac{2s}{1-s})\lVert W\rVert_{L^{\infty}(U)}}\llbracket\mathcal{Z}\rrbracket_{H^{s}(U)}^{\frac{1}{1-s}}.

Then, one has

\displaystyle\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}_{0}(U,\Lambda^{-}_{\lambda,\theta}(W,\mathcal{Z}))\leq\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}(U,\lambda)\leq\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}(U,\lambda)\leq\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}_{0}(U,\Lambda^{+}_{\lambda,\theta}(W,\mathcal{Z})).

Proof.

We only prove
\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}(U,\lambda)\leq\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}_{0}(U,\Lambda^{+}_{\lambda,\theta}(U,W,\mathcal{Z}))
; the other inequality follows similarly.
By setting
\delta\vcentcolon=\frac{\theta}{1+\theta}e^{-2\lVert W\rVert_{L^{\infty}(U)}}
, Lemma
4.7
yields

\lvert\mathcal{Z}(u^{\flat},u^{\flat})\rvert\leq\delta\int_{U}\lvert\nabla u^{\flat}\rvert^{2}+A_{+}e^{-2\lVert W\rVert_{L^{\infty}(U)}}\int_{U}(u^{\flat})^{2}.

One has
\int_{U}e^{2W}\lvert\nabla u^{\flat}\rvert^{2}\geq e^{-2\lVert W\rVert_{L^{\infty}(U)}}\int_{U}\lvert\nabla u^{\flat}\rvert^{2}
.
Therefore, by Proposition
4.10
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}(U)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\sqsubset H^{1}(U),\\
\dim L=k\end{subarray}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}u^{\flat}\in L,\\
\int e^{2W}(u^{\flat})^{2}=1\end{subarray}}\int_{U}e^{2W}\lvert\nabla u^{\flat}\rvert^{2}+\mathcal{Z}(u^{\flat},u^{\flat})

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\sqsubset H^{1}(U),\\
\dim L=k\end{subarray}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}u^{\flat}\in L,\\
\int e^{2W}(u^{\flat})^{2}=1\end{subarray}}\frac{e^{-2\lVert W\rVert_{L^{\infty}(U)}}}{1+\theta}\int_{U}\lvert\nabla u^{\flat}\rvert^{2}-A_{+}e^{-2\lVert W\rVert_{L^{\infty}(U)}}\int_{U}(u^{\flat})^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{e^{-2\lVert W\rVert_{L^{\infty}(U)}}}{1+\theta}\Big\{\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\sqsubset H^{1}(U),\\
\dim L=k\end{subarray}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}u^{\flat}\in L,\\
\int e^{2W}(u^{\flat})^{2}=1\end{subarray}}\int_{U}\lvert\nabla u^{\flat}\rvert^{2}\Big\}-A_{+}.

We compute

\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\sqsubset H^{1}(U),\\
\dim L=k\end{subarray}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}u^{\flat}\in L,\\
\int e^{2W}(u^{\flat})^{2}=1\end{subarray}}\int_{U}\lvert\nabla u^{\flat}\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\sqsubset H^{1}(U),\\
\dim L=k\end{subarray}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}u^{\flat}\in L,\\
\int e^{2W}(u^{\flat})^{2}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\int_{U}\lvert\nabla u^{\flat}\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\sqsubset H^{1}(U),\\
\dim L=k\end{subarray}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}u^{\flat}\in L,\\
\int(u^{\flat})^{2}\leq e^{-2\lVert W\rVert_{L^{\infty}(U)}}\end{subarray}}\int_{U}\lvert\nabla u^{\flat}\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=e^{-2\lVert W\rVert_{L^{\infty}(U)}}\lambda_{k}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}(U;0,0).

(50)

Therefore,

\lambda^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}_{k}(U)\geq\frac{e^{-4\lVert W\rVert_{L^{\infty}(U)}}}{1+\theta}\lambda^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}_{k}(U;0,0)-A_{+}

and the claimed inequality follows.
∎

As Lemma
4.19
suggests, we need estimates of
\bm{N}^{\#}_{0}(U,\lambda)
.
The following lemma is sufficient for our purpose.

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
U
be a bounded Lipschitz domain.

(a)

Then, there exist
C_{U},R_{U}>0
such that

\bm{N}^{\mathfrak{m}}_{0}(U_{L}^{R},\lambda)\leq C_{U}R^{d}(1+\lambda)^{\frac{d}{2}}L^{d-1}

for every
L\geq 1
,
\lambda\geq 0
and
R\geq R_{U}
.

(b)

[
62
, Theorem 3.1 and Theorem 3.2]

There exists a
C^{\prime}_{U}>0
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\lvert B(0,1)\rvert}{(2\pi)^{d}}\lvert U\rvert\lambda^{\frac{d}{2}}-C^{\prime}_{U}\lambda^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\log\lambda

\displaystyle\leq\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}_{0}(U,\lambda)

\displaystyle\leq\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}_{0}(U,\lambda)\leq\frac{\lvert B(0,1)\rvert}{(2\pi)^{d}}\lvert U\rvert\lambda^{\frac{d}{2}}+C^{\prime}_{U}\lambda^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\log\lambda

for every
\lambda\geq 2
.

Proof.

The claim (a) follows from the proof of
[
21
, Theorem 6.2]
.
Indeed, we can combine the estimates (6.20), (6.23), (6.24) and (6.25) therein.
∎

Definition 4.21
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
be closed symmetric forms on Hilbert spaces
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
that are bounded from below.
We write
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\prec\mathcal{Q}_{2}
if there exists an isometry
\Phi:H_{2}\to H_{1}
such that
\Phi(\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}_{2}))\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}_{1})
and

\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\Phi(f),\Phi(f))\leq\mathcal{Q}_{2}(f,f)
for every
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Q}_{2})
.

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{2}
be as in Definition
4.21
and let
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
be the associated self-adjoint operators.
Suppose that the spectrum of
A_{1}
and that of
A_{2}
are discrete. We denote them by

(\mu_{k}(A_{1}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\mu_{k}(A_{2}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, respectively. Then,
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\prec\mathcal{Q}_{2}
implies
\mu_{k}(A_{1})\leq\mu_{k}(A_{2})
for every
k
.

Proof.

This follows from the min-max formula.

∎

In order to compare the eigenvalue counting functions on different domains, it will be convenient to introduce the following symmetric forms.

Definition 4.23
.

Let
J\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\mathcal{E}_{j}
be a symmetric form on a Hilbert space
H_{j}
for
j\in\{1,\dots,J\}
.
We define the symmetric form
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{J}\mathcal{E}_{j}
on the Hilbert space
\bigoplus H_{j}
by
\mathcal{D}(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{J}\mathcal{E}_{j})=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{J}\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{j})
and for
v=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{J}v_{j}
with
v_{j}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{j})
,
(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{J}\mathcal{E}_{j})(v,v):=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathcal{E}_{j}(v_{j},v_{j})
.

Observe that if
A_{j}
is the operator associated with
\mathcal{E}_{j}
for all
j
, then the operator
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{J}A_{j}
defined by
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{J}A_{j}v=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{J}A_{j}v_{j}
for
v=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{J}v_{j}\in\bigoplus_{j=1}^{J}\mathcal{D}(A_{j})=:\mathcal{D}(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{J}A_{j})
is the operator associated with
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{J}\mathcal{E}_{j}
.
In particular, the principal eigenvalue of
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{J}A_{j}
is given by
\min_{j\in\{1,\dots,J\}}\lambda_{1}(A_{j})
, where
\lambda_{1}(A_{j})
is the principal eigenvalue of
A_{j}
for all
j
.

Moreover, if
A_{j}
has a countable spectrum for all
j
, then one has
\bm{N}_{\bigoplus_{j=1}^{J}\mathcal{E}_{j}}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\bm{N}_{\mathcal{E}_{j}}
, where
\bm{N}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is the eigenvalue counting function corresponding to the operator associated with
\mathcal{Q}
.

Observe that using this notation, one also has
\bm{N}_{a\mathcal{Q}+b\mathfrak{I}}(\lambda)=\bm{N}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\frac{\lambda-b}{a})
, where
\mathfrak{I}
is the symmetric form
\mathfrak{I}(v,v)=\lVert v\rVert^{2}
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\prec\mathcal{Q}_{2}
implies
\bm{N}_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}\geq\bm{N}_{\mathcal{Q}_{2}}
.

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
U,U_{1},\dots,U_{J}\in\mathcal{U}
,

U=\cup_{j=1}^{J}U_{j}
with
\overline{U}_{j}\cap\overline{U}_{k}=\partial U_{j}\cap\partial U_{k}
for
j\neq k
.

(a)

If

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{U_{j}}(v,v)=\mathcal{Z}^{U}(v,v),\qquad v\in H_{0}^{1}(U_{j}),j\in\{1,\dots,J\},

(51)

then

\lambda^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}_{1}(U)\leq\min_{j\in\{1,\dots,J\}}\lambda_{1}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}(U_{j})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}(U,\lambda)\geq\sum_{j=1}^{J}\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}(U_{j},\lambda).

(52)

(b)

If

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{U}(v,v)=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathcal{Z}^{U_{j}}(v|_{U_{j}},v|_{U_{j}}),\quad v\in H^{1}(U),

(53)

then

\lambda^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}_{1}(U)\geq\min_{j\in\{1,\dots,J\}}\lambda_{1}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}(U_{j})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}(U,\lambda)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{J}\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}}(U_{j},\lambda).

Proof.

(a)
follows from the fact that
\oplus_{j=1}^{J}H^{1}_{0}(U_{j})\subseteq H^{1}_{0}(U)
.

(b)

As
L^{2}(U)
and
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{J}L^{2}(U_{j})
are isometric,
H^{1}(U)\subseteq\oplus_{j=1}^{J}H^{1}(U_{j})
and

\mathcal{E}^{U}(u,u)=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathcal{E}^{U_{j}}(u|_{U_{j}},u|_{U_{j}})
for
u\in e^{W}H^{1}(U)
, we have
\oplus_{j=1}^{J}\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U_{j}}\prec\mathcal{E}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{N}$}},U}
.
Now both inequalities follow from Lemma
4.22
(see also the comments in Definition
4.23
).
∎

Remark 4.25
.

Observe that (
51
) and (
53
) hold for
U,U_{1},\dots,U_{J}
as in Lemma
4.28

(a)
if
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\sigma\in[0,\infty)
,

Y\in\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{Z}^{U}
is given by
\mathcal{Z}^{U}_{Y}
for
U\in\mathcal{U}
as in Theorem
4.3

(a)
; or if
Y\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\mathcal{Z}^{U}
is given for
U\in\mathcal{U}
by

\mathcal{Z}^{U}(v,v)\vcentcolon=\int_{\partial U}v^{2}\nabla Y\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\bm{S},

or if it is a linear combination of the above examples.

We can give a “reversed” inequality of (
52
).
First we present an auxiliary lemma which is based on the IMS formula, see
[
66
]
.

Lemma 4.26
.

Let
J\in\mathbb{N}
and
U,U_{1},\dots,U_{J}\in\mathcal{U}
.
Let
\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{J}
be smooth functions
\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow[0,1]
such that there exists an
A>0
such that

\displaystyle\Big\|\sum_{j=1}^{J}|\nabla\eta_{j}|^{2}\Big\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq A,\quad j\in\{1,\dots,J\},\qquad\sum_{j=1}^{J}\eta_{j}^{2}=1\mbox{ on }U.

Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{U}_{W,0}(u,u)\geq\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathcal{E}_{W,0}^{U}(\eta_{j}u,\eta_{j}u)-A\|\eta_{j}u\|_{L^{2}}^{2},\quad u\in e^{W}H^{1}(U).

Proof.

Observe that
\sum_{j=1}^{J}\nabla(\eta_{j}^{2})=0
.
Let
u=e^{W}u^{\flat}
with
u^{\flat}\in H^{1}(U)
.
Then

\displaystyle\eta_{j}^{2}|\nabla u^{\flat}|^{2}=\lvert\nabla(\eta_{j}u^{\flat})\rvert^{2}-\lvert\nabla\eta_{j}\rvert^{2}(u^{\flat})^{2}-\nabla(\eta_{j}^{2})\cdot u^{\flat}\nabla u^{\flat},

and therefore

\displaystyle\int_{U}e^{2W}\lvert\nabla u^{\flat}\rvert^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int_{U}e^{2W}\eta_{j}^{2}\lvert\nabla u^{\flat}\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j=1}^{J}\Big\{\int_{U}e^{2W}\lvert\nabla(\eta_{j}u^{\flat})\rvert^{2}-A\lVert\eta_{j}u^{\flat}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big\}.

∎

Remark 4.27
.

So far we have only considered the Anderson Hamiltonians on bounded domains, which means bounded open subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
However, whether one considers
U
or
\overline{U}
, does not intrinsically make a difference. In the following lemma and further on we will consider the Anderson Hamiltonian on closed boxes of the form
[0,L]^{d}
for example. One may read
(0,L)^{d}
instead in order to align with the rest of the text, though we write
[0,L]^{d}
as this is more common in the literature.

Lemma 4.28
.

Let
Z\in\mathcal{C}^{-1+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\delta\in(0,1)
and
suppose
\mathcal{Z}^{U}=\mathcal{Z}^{U}_{Z}
as in Theorem
4.3

(a)
for every Lipschitz domain
U
.

(a)

There exists a
K>0
(which depends only on
d
) such that for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
, all
l,L>0
with
L>2l
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}([0,nL]^{d})

\displaystyle\geq\min_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap[-1,n+1]^{d}}\lambda_{1}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}(kL+[-l,L+l]^{d})-\frac{K}{l^{2}},

\displaystyle\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}([0,nL]^{d},\lambda)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap[-1,n+1]^{d}}\bm{N}^{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{D}$}}}\Big(kL+[-l,L+l]^{d},\lambda+\frac{K}{l^{2}}\Big).

(b)

There exists a
K>0
(depending only on
d
) such that for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
and
s\in(1-\delta,1)
there exist
C_{s,U},R_{U}>0
such that for all
L\geq 1
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
R\geq R_{U}
,
