\displaystyle\lesssim_{\sigma,m}\sum_{l=0}^{m}\binom{m}{l}L^{-\lvert m-l\rvert}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2})^{\frac{\sigma-\lvert l\rvert}{2}}\lvert\partial^{m-l}\phi(L^{-1}x)\rvert

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{m}\binom{m}{l}L^{-\lvert m-l\rvert}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\lvert Lx\rvert^{2})^{\frac{\sigma-\lvert l\rvert}{2}}\lvert\partial^{m-l}\phi(x)\rvert

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m,\sigma,\phi}L^{\sigma}.\qed

Lemma A.5
.

Let
U
be a bounded domain,
r\in\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma\in(0,\infty)
. Then for for
Z\in\mathcal{C}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\lVert Z\rVert_{C^{r}(U_{L})}\lesssim_{U,\sigma}L^{\sigma}\lVert Z\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\qquad L\geq 1.

Proof.

Let
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be
1
on a neighborhood of
U
. Then
\lVert Z\rVert_{C^{r}(U_{L})}=\lVert\phi(L\cdot)Z\rVert_{C^{r}(U)}\leq\lVert\phi(L\cdot)Z\rVert_{C^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.
By Lemma
2.7
,

\displaystyle\lVert\phi(L\cdot)Z\rVert_{C^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{r}\lVert\phi(L\cdot)Z\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Therefore, we obtain the desired estimate by an application of Lemma
A.4
.
∎

Lemma A.6
.

Let
p,q\in[1,\infty]
,
r\in\mathbb{R}
,
\sigma\in[0,\infty)
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
and
a\in\mathbb{R}
.

(a)

One has
\lVert\partial^{m}f\rVert_{B^{r-\lvert m\rvert,\sigma}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{p,q,r,\sigma,m}\lVert f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.

(b)

Let
\tilde{\chi}
be a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\tilde{\chi}=0
in a neighborhood of
0
and all the derivatives are bounded.
Then, one has
\lVert\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\lvert 2\pi\cdot\rvert^{2a}\tilde{\chi}\mathcal{F}f]\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{r-2a,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{p,q,r,\sigma,a,\tilde{\chi}}\lVert f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.

Proof.

We only prove (a), as the proof of (b) is similar.
We use the notations from Definition
2.2
.
Let
\psi
be a compactly supported smooth function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\psi=1
on a neighborhood of

\operatorname{supp}(\chi)
and set
\psi_{j}\vcentcolon=\psi(2^{-j}\cdot)
.
Because
\Delta_{j}\partial^{m}f=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\chi(2^{-j}\cdot)\psi_{j}]*\Delta_{j}f
for
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, by Theorem
A.2
we have

\lVert w_{\sigma}(\Delta_{j}\partial^{m}f)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\lVert w_{-\sigma}\mathcal{F}^{-1}[(-2\pi i\cdot)^{m}\psi_{j}]\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lVert w_{\sigma}\Delta_{j}f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

(77)

It remains to observe that for all
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

\displaystyle 2^{-j\lvert m\rvert}\lVert w_{-\sigma}\mathcal{F}^{-1}[(-2\pi i\cdot)^{m}\psi_{j}]\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\lVert w_{-\sigma}\mathcal{F}^{-1}[(-2^{-j+1}\pi i\cdot)^{m}\psi(2^{-j}\cdot)]\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\lVert 2^{jd}\Big[w_{-\sigma}(2^{-j}\cdot)[\mathcal{F}^{-1}[(-2\pi i\cdot)^{m}\psi\Big](2^{j}\cdot)\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert w_{-\sigma}[\mathcal{F}^{-1}[(-2\pi i\cdot)^{m}\psi]\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

as
w_{-\sigma}(2^{-j}\cdot)\leq w_{-\sigma}
.
For
j=-1
a similar estimate as (
77
) holds for a
\tilde{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\tilde{\psi}=1
on
\operatorname{supp}(\tilde{\chi})
.
∎

Definition A.7
.

For
J\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
or
J=-1
we write

\displaystyle\Delta_{\leq J}f=\sum_{j=-1}^{J}\Delta_{j}f,\qquad\Delta_{\geq J}f=\sum_{j=J}^{\infty}\Delta_{j}f.

Remark A.8
.

Observe that by definition of
\check{\chi}
and
\chi
(Definition
2.2
), for
N\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle(1-\check{\chi})(2^{-N}x)=\sum_{j=N}^{\infty}\chi(2^{-j}x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

and therefore

\displaystyle\Delta_{\geq N}f

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\Big((1-\check{\chi})(2^{-N}\cdot)\mathcal{F}f\Big),\quad\Delta_{\leq N}f=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\Big(\check{\chi}(2^{-N}\cdot)\mathcal{F}f\Big).

Lemma A.9
.

Let
p,q\in[1,\infty]
,
r,s\in\mathbb{R}
with
r\leq s
,
\sigma\in[0,\infty)
and
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Then, one has (observe the difference of the positions of
r
and
s
)

\displaystyle\lVert\Delta_{\geq N}f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s-r,\sigma}2^{-(s-r)N}\lVert f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{s,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

\displaystyle\lVert\Delta_{\leq N}f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{s,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s-r,\sigma}2^{(s-r)N}\lVert f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Proof.

We first observe by Remark
A.8
that
\Delta_{\geq N}\Delta_{j}f=[2^{Nd}\mathcal{F}^{-1}(1-\check{\chi})(2^{N}\cdot)*f
.
Thus, by
[
56
, Theorem 2.4 and Lemma 2.6]
, one has

\lVert w_{\sigma}(\Delta_{j}\Delta_{\geq N}f)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\lVert w_{\sigma}(\Delta_{j}f)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

Therefore,

\displaystyle\lVert\Delta_{\geq N}f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum_{j=N-1}^{\infty}2^{jqr}\lVert w_{\sigma}(\Delta_{j}\Delta_{\geq N}f)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-(N-1)(s-r)}\Big(\sum_{j=N-1}^{\infty}2^{jqs}\lVert w_{\sigma}(\Delta_{j}\Delta_{\geq N}f)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s-r,\sigma}2^{-N(s-r)}\Big(\sum_{j=N-1}^{\infty}2^{jqs}\lVert w_{\sigma}(\Delta_{j}f)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-N(s-r)}\lVert f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{s,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

The second inequality can be proven similarly.
∎

Recall the definition of an admissible kernel and of
G_{N}
, see Definition
B.35
and Definition
2.10
.

Corollary A.10
.

Let
p,q\in[1,\infty]
,
r,s\in\mathbb{R}
with
r\leq s
,
\sigma\in[0,\infty)
and
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Let
K
be an admissible kernel. Set
H_{N}\vcentcolon=G_{N}-K
.
Then, one has

\displaystyle\lVert G_{N}\ast f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{r+2,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{p,q,r,s,\sigma}2^{-(s-r)N}\lVert f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{s,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

\displaystyle\lVert H_{N}\ast f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{s+2,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{p,q,r,s,\sigma}2^{(s-r)N}\lVert f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

\displaystyle\lVert(G_{N}-G_{0})\ast f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{s+2,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{p,q,r,s,\sigma}2^{(s-r)N}\lVert f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Proof.

Suppose
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is
0
in a neighborhood of
0
and is
equal to
1
on
\operatorname{supp}(1-\check{\chi})
.
If we set
g\vcentcolon=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\lvert 2\pi\cdot\rvert^{-2}\psi\mathcal{F}f]
, then

G_{N}\ast f=\Delta_{\geq N}g
. Therefore, the first claimed inequality
follows from Lemma
A.6

(b)
and Lemma
A.9
.

To prove the second claimed inequality, recall that one has
H_{N}=(G_{N}-G_{0})+(G_{0}-K)
.
By Lemma
C.1
below,
G_{0}-K
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Therefore,

\lVert(G_{0}-K)\ast f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{s+2,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{p,q,r,s,\sigma}\lVert f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

On the other hand, one has
(G_{N}-G_{0})\ast f=(\Delta_{\geq N}-\Delta_{\geq 0})g=(\Delta_{\leq N-1}-\Delta_{-1})g
.
Hence, by Lemma
A.6

(b)
and by Lemma
A.9
, the third inequality and thus the second follow.
∎

Finally, we recall a wavelet characterization of weighted Besov spaces.

Theorem A.11

(
[
54
]
,
[
71
, Theorem 1.61]
)
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist
\psi_{\mathfrak{f}},\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}\in C_{c}^{k}(\mathbb{R})
with the following properties.

•

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, if we denote by
V_{n}
the subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
spanned by

\{\psi_{\mathfrak{f}}(2^{n}\cdot-m)\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}m\in\mathbb{Z}\},

then the inclusions
V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq V_{n}\subseteq V_{n+1}\subseteq\cdots
hold and

L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is the closure of
\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}V_{n}
.

•

The set

\{\psi_{\mathfrak{f}}(\cdot-m)\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}m\in\mathbb{Z}\}\cup\{\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(\cdot-m)\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}m\in\mathbb{Z}\}

forms an orthonormal basis of
V_{1}
.
Therefore, the set

\{\psi_{\mathfrak{f}}(\cdot-m)\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}m\in\mathbb{Z}\}\cup\{2^{\frac{n}{2}}\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(2^{n}\cdot-m)\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},m\in\mathbb{Z}\}

forms an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

•

One has
\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{l}\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=0
for every
l\in\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
.

One can build an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as follows.

Proposition A.12

(
[
71
, Proposition 1.53]
)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\psi_{\mathfrak{f}},\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}\in C_{c}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be as in Theorem
A.11
.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we define the sets of
d
-tuples by

\mathfrak{G}^{n}\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\{(\mathfrak{f},\ldots,\mathfrak{f})\}&\text{if }n=0,\\
\{(G_{1},\ldots,G_{d})\in\{\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{m}\}^{d}\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\exists j\text{ s.t. }G_{j}=\mathfrak{m}\}&\text{if }n\geq 1.\end{cases}

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
G\in\mathfrak{G}^{n}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we set
(n-1)_{+}=\max\{n-1,0\}
and

\Psi_{m}^{n,G}(x)\vcentcolon=2^{\frac{d(n-1)_{+}}{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{d}\psi_{G_{j}}(2^{(n-1)_{+}}x_{j}-m_{j}).

(78)

The set
\{\Psi_{m}^{n,G}\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},G\in\mathfrak{G}^{n},m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\}
forms an orthonormal
basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

With the expansion by the basis
\{\Psi_{m}^{n,G}\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},G\in\mathfrak{G}^{n},m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\}
,
one can give a wavelet characterization of weighted Besov spaces.

Proposition A.13

(
[
71
, Theorem 6.15]
)
.

Let
p,q\in[1,\infty]
,
r\in\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma\in(0,\infty)
.
Suppose

k>\max\Big\{r,\frac{2d}{p}+\frac{d}{2}-r\Big\}

and let
\{\Psi_{m}^{n,G}\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},G\in\mathfrak{G}^{n},m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\}
be as in Proposition
A.12
.
Then, there exists a constant
C\in(0,\infty)
such that for every
f\in B_{p,q}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
one has

C^{-1}\lVert f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\\
\leq\Big\lVert\Big(2^{n(r-d/p)}\Big(\sum_{G\in\mathfrak{G}^{n},m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}w_{\sigma}(2^{-n}m)^{p}\lvert 2^{nd/2}\langle f,\Psi^{n,G}_{m}\rangle\rvert^{p}\Big)^{1/p}\Big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\Big\rVert_{l^{q}(\mathbb{N}_{0})}\\
\leq C\lVert f\rVert_{B_{p,q}^{r,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

A.2
Estimates of constants of functional inequalities on bounded domains

In Definition
4.6
we have introduced the smallest constant that appears in interpolation inequalities. In this section we introduce also other constants that appear in functional inequalities and study their behaviour (also under scaling of the underlying domain).

Definition A.14
.

Let
U
be a bounded domain and
p,p_{1},p_{2}\in[1,\infty]
,
r_{1},r_{2},s\in[0,\infty)
,
r\in(0,\infty)
and
\delta\in(0,r)
.
We set

\displaystyle C^{U}_{\operatorname{Embed}}[W^{r_{1}}_{p_{1}}\to W^{r_{2}}_{p_{2}}]\vcentcolon=\sup_{f\in W^{r_{1}}_{p_{1}}(U)\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\lVert f\rVert_{W^{r_{2}}_{p_{2}}(U)}}{\lVert f\rVert_{W^{r_{1}}_{p_{1}}(U)}},

\displaystyle C^{U}_{\operatorname{Prod}}[W_{2p}^{r}\to W_{p}^{r-\delta}]\vcentcolon=\sup_{f\in W^{r}_{2p}(U)\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\lVert f^{2}\rVert_{W^{r-\delta}_{p}(U)}}{\lVert f\rVert_{W^{r}_{2p}(U)}^{2}}.

Similarly, we set
C^{U}_{\operatorname{Embed}}[W^{r_{1}}_{p_{1},0}\to W^{r_{2}}_{p_{2},0}],\ldots
by
replacing the function spaces “
W_{p}^{r}
” to those with zero boundary conditions “
W_{p,0}^{r}
”.
If
U
is a bounded Lipschitz domain, for a universal extension operator
\iota
from
U
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as in Lemma
2.8
, we set

\displaystyle C^{U}_{\operatorname{Ext}}[W^{r_{1}}_{p_{1}},W^{r_{2}}_{p_{2}}]\vcentcolon=\inf\{\lVert\iota\rVert_{W^{r_{1}}_{p_{1}}(U)\rightarrow W^{r_{1}}_{p_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\lVert\iota\rVert_{W^{r_{2}}_{p_{2}}(U)\rightarrow W^{r_{2}}_{p_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt|\iota\mbox{ is an universal extension operator}\},

\displaystyle C^{\partial U}_{\operatorname{R}}(W^{r}_{p})\vcentcolon=\inf\{\lVert\mathcal{R}\rVert_{W^{r}_{p}(\partial U)\to W^{r+\frac{1}{p}}_{p}(U)}\ |\ \mathcal{R}\mbox{ is a right inverse of }\mathcal{T}_{W_{p}^{r+\frac{1}{p}}(U)}\},

\displaystyle C^{\partial U}_{\operatorname{Prod}}[W_{2p}^{r}\to W_{p}^{r-\delta}]\vcentcolon=\sup_{f\in W^{r}_{2p}(\partial U)\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\lVert f^{2}\rVert_{W^{r-\delta}_{p}(\partial U)}}{\lVert f\rVert_{W^{r}_{2p}(\partial U)}^{2}},

\displaystyle C^{U}_{\operatorname{Mult}}[W_{p}^{r}]\vcentcolon=\sup_{f\in W_{p}^{r}(U)\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{U}f\rVert_{W_{p}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}}{\lVert f\rVert_{W^{r}_{p}(U)}},

and
C^{U}_{\operatorname{Ext}}[W^{r}_{p}]\vcentcolon=C^{U}_{\operatorname{Ext}}[W^{r}_{p},W^{r}_{p}]
.

Lemma A.15
.

Let
U
be a domain.

(a)

Let
p_{1},p_{2}\in(1,\infty)
with
p_{1}\leq p_{2}
and

r_{1},r_{2}\in[0,\infty)
with
r_{2}=r_{1}-d(\frac{1}{p_{1}}-\frac{1}{p_{2}})
. Then, one has

C^{U}_{\operatorname{Embed}}[W^{r_{1}}_{p_{1},0}\to W^{r_{2}}_{p_{2},0}]\lesssim_{p_{1},p_{2},r_{1}}1
.
If
U
is a bounded Lipschitz domain, one has

C^{U}_{\operatorname{Embed}}[W^{r_{1}}_{p_{1}}\to W^{r_{2}}_{p_{2}}]\lesssim_{p_{1},p_{2},r_{1}}C^{U}_{\operatorname{Ext}}[W^{r_{1}}_{p_{1}}]
.

(b)

Let
s\in(0,1)
. Then, one has

C^{U}_{\operatorname{IP}}[H^{s}_{0}]\lesssim_{s}1
.
If
U
is a bounded Lipschitz domain, one has

C^{U}_{\operatorname{IP}}[H^{s}]\lesssim_{s}C^{U}_{\operatorname{Ext}}[L^{2},H^{1}]
.

Proof.

We only prove the claim for a bounded Lipschitz domain
U
.

(a) Let
\iota
be a universal extension operator from
U
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, by using
the Sobolev embedding in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

[
10
, Theorem 8.12.6]
for the second inequality,

\lVert f\rVert_{W^{r_{2}}_{p_{2}}(U)}\leq\lVert\iota(f)\rVert_{W^{r_{2}}_{p_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{p_{1},p_{2},r_{1}}\lVert\iota(f)\rVert_{W^{r_{1}}_{p_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\lVert\iota\rVert_{W_{p_{1}}^{r_{1}}(U)\rightarrow W_{p_{1}}^{r_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lVert f\rVert_{W^{r_{1}}_{p_{1}}(U)},

and thus
\lVert f\rVert_{W^{r_{2}}_{p_{2}}(U)}\lesssim_{p_{1},p_{2},r_{1}}C^{U}_{\operatorname{Extend}}[W^{r_{1}}_{p_{1}}]\lVert f\rVert_{W^{r_{1}}_{p_{1}}(U)}
.

(b) We can prove the claim similarly by using the inequality

[
4
, Proposition 2.22]

\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{s}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{1-s}\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{s}.\qed

Lemma A.16
.

Let
U
be a bounded domain,
p\in[1,\infty)
,
r\in(0,1)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,r)
.
Then we have

C^{U}_{\operatorname{Prod}}[W_{2p,0}^{r}\to W_{p,0}^{r-\varepsilon}]\lesssim_{p,\varepsilon}1,

and if
U
is a bounded Lipschitz domain

C^{U}_{\operatorname{Prod}}[W_{2p}^{r}\to W_{p}^{r-\varepsilon}]\lesssim_{p,\varepsilon}1,

C^{\partial U}_{\operatorname{Prod}}[W_{2p}^{r}\to W_{p}^{r-\varepsilon}]\lesssim_{p,\varepsilon}1+\sup_{x\in\partial U}\Big(\int_{\partial U}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}y}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d-1-2p\varepsilon}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2p}}.

Proof.

We only prove the first inequality.
Since

\int_{\lvert x\rvert\geq 1}\frac{1}{\lvert x\rvert^{d+p(r-\varepsilon)}}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty,

one has

\lVert f^{2}\rVert_{W_{p}^{r-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim_{p,r}\lVert f^{2}\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\int_{\lvert x-y\rvert\leq 1}\frac{\lvert f(x)^{2}-f(y)^{2}\rvert^{p}}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d+p(r-\varepsilon)}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y.

Observe that
\lVert f^{2}\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\lVert f\rVert^{2}_{L^{2p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.
Furthermore, observe that

\frac{\lvert f(x)^{2}-f(y)^{2}\rvert^{p}}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d+p(r-\varepsilon)}}=\Big(\frac{\lvert f(x)+f(y)\rvert}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{\frac{d}{2p}-\varepsilon}}\Big)^{p}\Big(\frac{\lvert f(x)-f(y)\rvert}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{\frac{d}{2p}+r}}\Big)^{p},

so that, by Hölder’s inequality

\int_{\lvert x-y\rvert\leq 1}\frac{\lvert f(x)^{2}-f(y)^{2}\rvert^{p}}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d+p(r-\varepsilon)}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\lVert f\rVert_{W^{r}_{2p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big(\int_{\lvert x-y\rvert\leq 1}\frac{\lvert f(x)+f(y)\rvert^{2p}}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{d-2p\varepsilon}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{\frac{1}{2p}}.

Now the latter integral can be estimated by
\|f\|_{L^{2p}}
times the following integral over the unit ball that can be estimated as follows

\int_{\lvert x\rvert\leq 1}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x}{\lvert x\rvert^{d-2p\varepsilon}}\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dr}{r^{1-2p\varepsilon}}=\frac{1}{2p\varepsilon}.\qed

Lemma A.17

(
[
68
, Proposition 5.3]
)
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
,
r\in(0,\frac{1}{p})
and let
U
be a bounded Lipschitz domain.
Then, the map

B_{p,p}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow B_{p,p}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad f\mapsto f\mathbbm{1}_{U}

is a bounded linear operator.

Lemma A.18
.

Let
U
be a bounded Lipschitz domain.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{L\geq 1}C^{U_{L}}_{\operatorname{Ext}}[W^{r_{1}}_{p_{1}},W^{r_{2}}_{p_{2}}]<\infty,

\displaystyle\quad p_{1},p_{2}\in[1,\infty],r_{1},r_{2}\in[0,\infty),

(79)

\displaystyle\sup_{L\geq 1}\lVert\mathcal{T}_{W^{r}_{p}(U_{L})}\rVert<\infty,

\displaystyle\quad p\in(1,\infty),r\in(\tfrac{1}{p},1+\tfrac{1}{p}),

(80)

\displaystyle\sup_{L\geq 1}C^{\partial U_{L}}_{\operatorname{R}}[W^{r}_{p}]<\infty,

\displaystyle\quad p\in(1,\infty),r\in(0,1),

(81)

\displaystyle\sup_{L\geq 1}C^{U_{L}}_{\operatorname{Embed}}[W^{r_{1}}_{p_{1}}\to W^{r_{2}}_{p_{2}}]<\infty,

\displaystyle\quad\begin{cases}p_{1},p_{2}\in(1,\infty),r_{1},r_{2}\in[0,\infty),&\\
p_{1}\leq p_{2},r_{2}=r_{1}-d(\frac{1}{p_{1}}-\frac{1}{p_{2}}),\end{cases}

(82)

\displaystyle\sup_{L\geq 1}C^{U_{L}}_{\operatorname{IP}}[H^{s}]<\infty

\displaystyle\quad s\in(0,1),

(83)

\displaystyle\sup_{L\geq 1}C^{U_{L}}_{\operatorname{Mult}}[W_{p}^{r}]<\infty,

\displaystyle\quad p\in(1,\infty),r\in(0,\tfrac{1}{p}),

(84)

\displaystyle\sup_{L\geq 1}C^{U_{L}}_{\operatorname{Prod}}[W_{2p}^{r}\to W_{p}^{r-\varepsilon}]<\infty

\displaystyle\quad p\in[1,\infty),r\in(0,1),\varepsilon\in(0,r),

(85)

\displaystyle\sup_{L\geq 1}L^{-\varepsilon}C^{\partial U_{L}}_{\operatorname{Prod}}[W_{2p}^{r}\to W_{p}^{r-\varepsilon}]<\infty,

\displaystyle\quad p\in[1,\infty),r\in(0,1),\varepsilon\in(0,r).

(86)

If
U
is a bounded domain (that is not necessarily Lipschitz), then (
82
) and (
85
) hold by replacing the occurrences of the form “
W^{a}_{b}
” by “
W^{a}_{b,0}
”.

Proof.

Let
\iota
be a universal extension operator from
U
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(Definition
2.9
).
For
L\geq 1
, we define a universal extension operator
\iota_{L}
from
U_{L}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\iota_{L}(f)\vcentcolon=\iota(f(L\cdot))(L^{-1}\cdot)
.
By change of variables, and using that
\partial^{\alpha}\iota(f)=\iota(\partial^{\alpha}f)
it is straightforward to check that
\lVert\iota_{L}\rVert_{W^{r}_{p}(U_{L})\rightarrow W^{r}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\lVert\iota\rVert_{W^{r}_{p}(U)\rightarrow W^{r}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.
This implies (
79
).
The two (
80
) and (
81
) estimates can be proven similarly.

(
82
) and (
83
) follow by Lemma
A.15
and by (
79
).

(
84
)
First observe
r\in(0,1)
. Set
F\vcentcolon=\iota_{L}(f)
for
f\in W_{p}^{r}(U_{L})
. Then,
\mathbbm{1}_{U_{L}}f=\mathbbm{1}_{U_{L}}F
and thus
\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{U_{L}}f\rVert_{W_{p}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}}+[\mathbbm{1}_{U_{L}}F]_{W^{r}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.
By change of variables, one has
[g(L^{-1}\cdot)]_{W_{p}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=L^{\frac{d}{p}-s}[g]_{W_{p}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and thus

[\mathbbm{1}_{U_{L}}F]_{W^{r}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=L^{\frac{d}{p}-r}[\mathbbm{1}_{U}F(L\cdot)]_{W^{r}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

By Lemma
A.17

and Lemma
2.7
, one has

\displaystyle[\mathbbm{1}_{U}F(L\cdot)]_{W^{r}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{U}F(L\cdot)\rVert_{W^{r}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{U,p,r}\lVert F(L\cdot)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+[F(L\cdot)]_{W^{r}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=L^{-\frac{d}{p}}\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+L^{-\frac{d}{p}-r}[F]_{W^{r}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

The claim follows because
\lVert F\rVert_{W^{p}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\lVert\iota_{L}\rVert_{W_{p}^{r}(U_{L})}\lVert f\rVert_{W^{p}_{r}(U_{L})}\leq\lVert\iota\rVert_{W_{p}^{r}(U)}\lVert f\rVert_{W^{p}_{r}(U_{L})}
.

(
85
) and (
86
) follow from Lemma
A.16
.
∎

Appendix B
A regularity structure for the gPAM

In this appendix, we list the necessary definitions and results from the paper of Bruned, Hairer and Zambotti
[
12
]
regarding the regularity structure for the generalized Parabolic Anderson model

\partial_{0}u=\Delta u+\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}g_{i,j}(u)\partial_{i}u\partial_{j}u+\sum_{i=1}^{d}h_{i}(u)\partial_{i}u+k(u)+f(u)\xi,

(87)

that we use in Appendix
C
.

B.1
Terminologies

Here we review some terminologies from
[
12
]
.

Definition B.1
.

We fix a
\emph{typeset}

{{}}{}\vcentcolon=\{\Xi,\mathscr{I}\}
.
The symbol represents the noise
\xi
and the symbol
represents an abstract integration operator.

Definition B.2
.

We define the following notions regarding graphs.

(a)

A
rooted tree
is a finite connected simple graph without cycles, with a distinguished vertex called
the
root
. We do not allow for an empty tree but we allow for a
trivial tree

\bullet
which consists of only
one vertex. Vertices will be called
nodes
.
Given a rooted tree
T
, the set of nodes and that of edges are denoted by
N=N_{T}
and by
E=E_{T}
respectively.
We denote by the root of
T
.
Nodes of a rooted tree are endowed with a partial order
\leq
by their distances
from the root. We orient edges
(x,y)\in E

so that
x\leq y
.

(b)

A
forest
is a finite simple graph without cycles. We say a forest is
rooted
if every
component of the forest is a rooted tree.
We allow for an empty rooted forest.
Given a rooted forest
F
, the set of nodes and that of edges are denoted by
N={{}}{}
and by
E={{}}{}
respectively.

(c)

A tree or a forest is called
\emph{typed}
if it is endowed with a map
{{}}{}:E\to\mathfrak{L}
where

E
is the set of edges.

(d)

We say
A
is a
subforest
of a forest
F
, and write
A\subseteq F
, if

N_{A}\subseteq N_{F}
and
E_{A}\subseteq E_{F}
and if
(x,y)\in E_{A}
implies
\{x,y\}\subseteq N_{A}
.
We note that a
subtree
of a rooted tree is again a rooted tree whose root is the unique vertex which is
closest to the root of the original rooted tree. Therefore, a subforest of a rooted forest is again
a rooted forest. If a forest is typed, a subforest inherits types by restriction.
If
A
and
B
are (rooted, typed) forests, we denote by
A\sqcup B
the disjoint union of
A
and
B
with
types naturally inherited.

Definition B.3
.

In this paper, a typed forest
F
is often equipped with a
colouring

\hat{F}
and
decorations

\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o},\mathfrak{e}
as follows.

(a)

A pair is called a
colourful forest
if the following hold:

•

F=(E_{F},N_{F},\mathfrak{t})
is a typed rooted forest.

•

One has
\hat{F}:E_{F}\sqcup N_{F}\to\{0,1,2\}
such that if
\hat{F}((x,y))=i>0
for
(x,y)\in E_{F}

then
\hat{F}(x)=\hat{F}(y)=i
.

(b)

If
(F,\hat{F})
is a colourful forest and

•

\mathfrak{n}:N_{F}\to\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
,

•

\mathfrak{o}:N_{F}\to\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{L}]
with
\operatorname{supp}(\mathfrak{o})\subseteq\cup_{i>0}\hat{F}^{-1}(i)
,

•

\mathfrak{e}:E_{F}\to\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
and
\operatorname{supp}(\mathfrak{e})\subseteq E_{F}\setminus\operatorname{supp}(\hat{F})
,

then the
5
-tuple , also written
(F,\hat{F})^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
, is called a
decorated forest
.
We denote by
\mathfrak{F}
the set of decorated forests.

(c)

For
x,y\in N_{F}
, we write
x\sim y
if they are connected in
\cup_{i>0}\hat{F}^{-1}(i)
.

(d)

Given a decorated forest
(F,\hat{F},\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o},\mathfrak{e})
, we view a subforest
A\subseteq F
as
a decorated forest by restricting the associated maps
(\hat{F},\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o},\mathfrak{e})
.

(e)

We write for the decorated tree

(\bullet,2,m,0,0)
.

Many examples of colourful forests can be found in
[
12
]
.

Definition B.4
.

Two notions of product for forests are defined as follows.

(a)

For decorated forests
\tau_{i}=(F_{i},\hat{F}_{i},\mathfrak{n}_{i},\mathfrak{o}_{i},\mathfrak{e}_{i})
(
i=1,2
),
we define the
forest product
by

{{}}{}\vcentcolon=(F_{1}\sqcup F_{2},\hat{F}_{1}+\hat{F}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{1}+\mathfrak{n}_{2},\mathfrak{o}_{1}+\mathfrak{o}_{2},\mathfrak{e}_{1}+\mathfrak{e}_{2})

where, for
i\neq j
,
(\hat{F}_{i},\mathfrak{n}_{i},\mathfrak{o}_{i},\mathfrak{e}_{i})
are set to
0
on
F_{j}
.

(b)

For a a decorated forest
\tau=(F,\hat{F},\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o},\mathfrak{e})
, we denote by
{{}}{}(\tau)
the decorated tree

(\mathscr{J}(F),[\hat{F}],[\mathfrak{n}],[\mathfrak{o}],\mathfrak{e}),

where
\mathscr{J}(F)
is the tree obtained by gluing all the roots of
F
,

[\hat{F}](\rho_{\mathscr{J}(F)})\vcentcolon=\max_{y\text{ is a root of $F$}}\hat{F}(y),\quad[\hat{F}](x)=\hat{F}(x)\,\,\mbox{for }x\neq\rho_{\mathscr{J}(F)}

and
[\mathfrak{n}]
and
[\mathfrak{o}]

are defined at the new root by summing the values at the roots of
F
, and are equal to
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{o}
elsewhere, respectively.
The
tree product
is defined by

{{}}{}\vcentcolon=\mathscr{J}(\tau_{1}\cdot\tau_{2}).

Definition B.5

(
[
12
, Section 4.3]
)
.

For a decorated tree
\tau=(T,\hat{T})^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
,
we write

\mathscr{I}_{k}(\tau)
for a decorated tree
\sigma=(S,\hat{S})^{\tilde{\mathfrak{n}},\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{e}}}

obtained by connecting the old root
\rho_{\tau}
to a new root
\rho_{\sigma}
with a new edge
e=(\rho_{0},\rho_{\tau})

and by defining
\hat{S}
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{n}}
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}
and

\tilde{\mathfrak{e}}
as an extension of
\hat{T}
,
\mathfrak{n}
,
\mathfrak{o}
and
\mathfrak{e}
such that

\hat{S}(\rho_{\sigma})=\tilde{\mathfrak{n}}(\rho_{\sigma})=\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}(\rho_{\sigma})=0,\quad\mathfrak{t}(e)=\mathscr{I},\quad\hat{S}(e)=0,\quad\tilde{\mathfrak{e}}(e)=k,

For
i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, we write
\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau)\vcentcolon=\mathscr{I}_{\bm{e_{i}}}(\tau)
,
where
\bm{e}_{i}
is the
i
th standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Definition B.6

(
[
12
, Definition 5.3]
)
.

We assign the
degree
to the types by

\lvert\Xi\rvert\vcentcolon=-2+\delta,\quad\lvert\mathscr{I}\rvert\vcentcolon=2.

(88)

We extend the degree to
(k,v)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{L}]
by

\lvert(k,v)\rvert\vcentcolon=\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}+a\lvert\Xi\rvert+b\lvert\mathscr{I}\rvert

where
v=a\Xi+b\mathscr{I}
.
For a decorated tree
\tau=(F,\hat{F},\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o},\mathfrak{e})
,
we set

\hat{E}_{i}\vcentcolon=\hat{F}^{-1}(i)\cap E_{F},\quad\hat{E}\vcentcolon=\hat{E}_{1}\cup\hat{E}_{2},\quad\hat{N}_{i}\vcentcolon=\hat{F}^{-1}(i)\cap N_{F}

and we define two notions of degrees
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{-}
and

\lvert\cdot\rvert_{+}
by

\displaystyle\lvert\tau\rvert_{-}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\sum_{e\in E_{F}\setminus\hat{E}}(\lvert\mathfrak{t}(e)\rvert-\mathfrak{e}(e))+\sum_{x\in N_{F}}\mathfrak{n}(x)

\displaystyle\lvert\tau\rvert_{+}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\sum_{e\in E_{F}\setminus\hat{E}_{2}}(\lvert\mathfrak{t}(e)\rvert-\mathfrak{e}(e))+\sum_{x\in N_{F}}\mathfrak{n}(x)+\sum_{x\in N_{F}\setminus\hat{N}_{2}}\lvert\mathfrak{o}(x)\rvert.

B.2
Hopf algebras on forests and trees

In this section, we introduce Hopf algebra structures on some spaces of forests and those of trees.
For this purpose, we begin with introducing contraction operators.

Definition B.7

(
[
12
, Definition 3.18]
)
.

We set

{{}}{}(F,\hat{F})^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}_{\mathfrak{e}}\vcentcolon=(\mathscr{K}_{\hat{F}}F,\hat{F})^{[\mathfrak{n}],[\mathfrak{o}]}_{[\mathfrak{e}]},

where

•

\mathscr{K}_{\hat{F}}F
is the quotient forest
F/\sim
, where the equivalent relation
\sim
is
in the sense of Definition
B.3
-(c);

•

\hat{F}
and
[\mathfrak{e}]
are natural “restrictions”;

•

one has
[\mathfrak{n}](x)\vcentcolon=\sum_{y\sim x}\mathfrak{n}(y)
;

•

one has

[\mathfrak{o}](x)\vcentcolon=\sum_{y\sim x}\mathfrak{o}(y)+\sum_{e\in E(x)}\mathfrak{t}(e),\quad E(x)\vcentcolon=\{(y,z)\in E\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}y\sim z\sim x\}.

For a decorated forest
\tau
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
, one has a unique decomposition
\tau=\mu\cdot\nu
such that
on
\nu
the map
\hat{F}
is equal to
i
and on each component of
\mu
the map
\hat{F}
is not equal to
i
everywhere.

Definition B.8
.

Then, we set

k_{i}(\nu)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}(\bullet,i,\sum_{x\in N_{\nu}}\mathfrak{n}(x),0,0)&\text{if }\sum_{x\in N_{\nu}}\mathfrak{n}(x)>0\\
\varnothing&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and

\mathscr{K}_{i}(\tau)\vcentcolon=\mathscr{K}(\mu)\cdot k_{i}(\nu).

In addition, we denote by
\hat{\mathscr{K}}_{i}(\tau)
the decorated forest that is obtained from
\mathscr{K}_{i}(\tau)
by setting

\mathfrak{o}
to
0
on
\hat{F}^{-1}(i)
.

Remark B.9
.

\nu
is allowed to be an empty forest
\varnothing
and
k_{i}(\varnothing)=\varnothing
.

With these operators, one can write Hopf algebras associated to regularity structures and renormalization structures.

Definition B.10
.

We define vector spaces
H_{1},H_{\circ}
as follows.

(a)

We denote by the free vector space generated by

{{}}{}(H_{1})\vcentcolon=\{(F,\hat{F})^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}_{\mathfrak{e}}\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\hat{F}\leq 1,\,\,\mathscr{K}_{1}(F)=F\}.

(b)

We denote by the free vector space generated by
\mathfrak{B}(H_{\circ})
, where
\tau\in\mathfrak{B}(H_{\circ})

if and only if

•

\tau
is a tree and
\hat{F}\leq 1
;

•

\mathscr{K}(\tau)=\tau
.

Definition B.11

(
[
12
, Definition 3.3]
)
.

Given a decorated forest
\tau=(F,\hat{F})^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
, we denote
by
\mathfrak{U}_{1}(\tau)
the set of all subforests of
F
which contains
\hat{F}^{-1}(1)
and subforests of
F
that are disjoint from

\hat{F}^{-1}(2)
.
We set

\begin{multlined}\Delta_{1}\tau\vcentcolon=\sum_{A\in\mathfrak{U}_{1}(\tau)}\sum_{\mathfrak{n}_{A}:\mathfrak{n}_{A}\leq\mathfrak{n}}\sum_{\varepsilon^{F}_{A}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{F}_{A}!}\binom{\mathfrak{n}}{\mathfrak{n}_{A}}(A,F|_{A},\mathfrak{n}_{A}+\pi\varepsilon^{F}_{A},\mathfrak{o}|_{N_{A}},\mathfrak{e}|_{E_{A}})\\
\otimes(F,\hat{F}\cup_{1}A,\mathfrak{n}-\mathfrak{n}_{A},\mathfrak{o}+\mathfrak{n}_{A}+\pi(\varepsilon^{F}_{A}-\mathfrak{e}\mathbbm{1}_{A}),\mathfrak{e}\mathbbm{1}_{E_{F}\setminus E_{A}}+\varepsilon^{F}_{A}),\end{multlined}

(89)

where

•

\varepsilon^{F}_{A}
runs over all maps
E_{F}\to\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
supported on the (outgoing) boundary

\partial(A,F)\vcentcolon=\{(e_{+},e_{-})\in E_{F}\setminus E_{A}\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}e_{+}\in N_{A}\};

•

for
\varepsilon:E_{F}\to\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
one defines
\pi\varepsilon:N_{F}\to\mathbb{Z}^{d}
by

\pi\varepsilon(x)\vcentcolon=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in E_{F}\\
e=(x,y)\text{ for some }y\end{subarray}}\varepsilon(x);

•

\hat{F}\cup_{1}A
is the map defined by

\hat{F}\cup_{1}A(x)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }x\in A\\
\hat{F}(x)&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Some of the main results from
[
12
]
are the following.

Proposition B.12

(
[
12
, Proposition 4.11]
)
.

The vector space
H_{1}
is a Hopf algebra with multiplication

\mathcal{M}(\tau_{1}\otimes\tau_{2})\vcentcolon=\mathscr{K}_{1}(\tau_{1}\cdot\tau_{2}),

with unit
\varnothing
, with coproduct
(\mathscr{K}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{K}_{1})\Delta_{1}
and with
counit

\mathbbm{1}^{\prime}_{H_{1}}((F,\hat{F})^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}_{\mathfrak{e}})\vcentcolon=\mathbbm{1}_{\{\varnothing\}}((F,\hat{F})^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}_{\mathfrak{e}}),\quad

The Hopf algebra
H_{1}
is graded with respect to
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{-}
.

Proposition B.13

(
[
12
, Proposition 4.14]
)
.

The vector space
H_{\circ}
is a left comodule over the Hopf algebra
H_{1}
with coaction

(\mathscr{K}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{K})\Delta_{1}:H_{\circ}\to H_{1}\otimes H_{\circ}.

B.3
Rule

We set

{{}}{}\vcentcolon=\{(F,\hat{F},\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o},\mathfrak{e})\in\mathfrak{F}\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}F\mbox{ is a tree, }\hat{F}\equiv 0,\,\,\mathfrak{o}\equiv 0\}.

The set
\mathfrak{T}
is a monoid with the tree product and with the trivial tree as unit.
We simply write
T^{\mathfrak{n}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
for
(T,0,\mathfrak{n},0,\mathfrak{e})\in\mathfrak{T}
.

Definition B.14
.

Given a decorated tree
T^{\mathfrak{n}}_{\mathfrak{e}}\in\mathfrak{T}
, we associate to each
x\in N_{T}
a node type

{{}}{}\vcentcolon=\mathscr{N}(x)\vcentcolon=\big((\mathfrak{t}(e_{1}),\mathfrak{e}(e_{1})),\ldots,(\mathfrak{t}(e_{n}),\mathfrak{e}(e_{n}))\big),

where
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
are the edges leaving the node
x
, namely, for each
j
one can find a
y_{j}\in N_{T}
such that

e_{j}=(x,y_{j})
.

Definition B.15
.

Let
(\mathfrak{L}\times\mathbb{N}_{0})^{n}/\sim_{n}
be the set of unordered
n
-tuples valued in
\mathfrak{L}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
let
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{N}
be the power set of
\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}(\mathfrak{L}\times\mathbb{N}_{0})^{n}/\sim_{n}
.
We define the
rule

{{}}{}:\mathfrak{L}\to\mathcal{P}\mathcal{N}
by

\displaystyle R(\Xi)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{()\},

\displaystyle R(\mathscr{I})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{([\mathscr{I}]_{n}),([\mathscr{I}]_{n},\mathscr{I}_{i}),([\mathscr{I}]_{n},\mathscr{I}_{i},\mathscr{I}_{j}),([\mathscr{I}]_{n},\Xi);n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},i,j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\},

where we write
[\mathscr{I}]_{n}
for the
n
-tuple of
(\mathscr{I},0)
and write
\mathscr{I}_{i}
for
(\mathscr{I},\bm{e}_{i})
,
where
\bm{e}_{i}
is the
i
th unit vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

It is not difficult to show that the rule
R
is subcritical in the sense of
[
12
, Definition 5.14]

and complete in the sense of
[
12
, Definition 5.20]
.

Definition B.16

(
[
12
, Definition 5.8]
)
.

Let
\tau=T^{\mathfrak{n}}_{\mathfrak{e}}\in\mathfrak{T}
.

(a)

We say
\tau

conforms to the rule
R
at the node
x
if the following hold:

•

if
x
is the root, then
\mathscr{N}(x)\in R(\Xi)
or
\mathscr{N}(x)\in R(\mathscr{I})
;

•

otherwise, one has
\mathscr{N}(x)\in R(\mathfrak{t}(e))
, where
e
is the edge such that

e=(y,x)
for some node
y
.

(b)

We say
\tau

conforms to the rule
R
if
\tau
comforms to
R
at every node, except possibly the root.

(c)

We say
\tau

strongly conforms to the rule
R
if
\tau
comforms to
R
at every node.

Definition B.17

(
[
12
, Definition 5.13]
)
.

We define sets
\mathfrak{T}_{\diamond}
(
\diamond\in\{\circ,1,-\}
) as follows.

(a)

We denote by
{{}}{}\subseteq\mathfrak{T}
the set of trees which strongly conform to
R
.

(b)

We denote by
{{}}{}\subseteq\mathfrak{F}
the smallest submonoid under the forest product which contains
\mathfrak{T}_{\circ}
.

(c)

We denote by
{{}}{}\subseteq\mathfrak{T}_{\circ}
the set of trees
T^{\mathfrak{n}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
with the following properties:

•

one has
\lvert\tau\rvert_{-}<0
and
\mathfrak{n}(\rho_{T})=0
;

•

if there exists only one edge containing
\rho_{T}
, then

T^{\mathfrak{n}}_{\mathfrak{e}}=\hbox to20.99pt{\vbox to48.83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 9.0679pt\lower-16.94461pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(90)

B.4
Definition of the regularity structure

The content of this section is parallel to
[
12
, Section 5.5]
.
Our goal here is to construct subspaces of
H_{\diamond}
(
\diamond\in\{1,\circ\}
) which provide a correct
framework for the theory of regularity structures.
Since we desire that elements of those spaces conform to the rule
R
, one might want to consider a subspace spanned by

\mathfrak{T}_{\diamond}
. However,
\mathfrak{T}_{\diamond}
is not closed under the coproduct of
H_{\diamond}
.
Therefore, we introduce the following definition.

Definition B.18
.

Recall the notation introduced in Definition
B.11
.
For
\diamond\in\{1,\circ\}
,
we denote by
\mathfrak{B}(H^{C}_{\diamond})\subseteq\mathfrak{B}(H_{\diamond})
the set consisting of

\mathscr{K}_{1}(F,\hat{F}\cup_{1}A,\mathfrak{n}-\mathfrak{n}_{A},\mathfrak{n}_{A}+\pi(\varepsilon^{F}_{A}-\mathfrak{e}\mathbbm{1}_{A}),\mathfrak{e}\mathbbm{1}_{F\setminus A}+\varepsilon^{F}_{A})

(91)

for
\tau=(F,\hat{F})^{\mathfrak{n},0}_{\mathfrak{e}}\in\mathfrak{T}_{\diamond}
,
A\in\mathfrak{U}_{1}(\tau)
,
\mathfrak{n}_{A}\leq\mathfrak{n}
with
\operatorname{supp}(\mathfrak{n}_{A})\subseteq N_{A}
and

\varepsilon^{F}_{A}:E_{F}\to\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
\operatorname{supp}(\varepsilon^{F}_{A})\subseteq\partial(A,F)
.
We denote by the free vector space generated by
\mathfrak{B}(H^{C}_{\diamond})
.

Remark B.19
.

By choosing
A=\varnothing

one observes
\mathfrak{T}_{\diamond}\subseteq\mathfrak{B}(H^{C}_{\diamond})
for
\diamond\in\{1,\circ\}
.
In fact, as Lemma
B.20
below shows,

H^{C}_{1}
is
the smallest subbialgebra of
H_{\diamond}
both containing
\mathfrak{T}_{\diamond}
and closed under the coactions.

Lemma B.20
.

The subspace
H_{1}^{C}
is a subbialgebra of
H_{1}
.
Furthermore, the statements of Proposition
B.13

remain valid
if one replaces
(H_{1},H_{\circ})
by
(H^{C}_{1},H^{C}_{\circ})
.

Proof.

This is essentially proven in
[
12
, Lemma 5.25 and Lemma 5.28]
.

∎

Definition B.21
.

For
\diamond\in\{1,\circ\}
we denote by the free vector space generated by

\mathfrak{B}(H^{R}_{\diamond})\vcentcolon=\{(F,\hat{F})^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}_{\mathfrak{e}}\in\mathfrak{B}(H^{C}_{\diamond})\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}(F,\hat{F}\mathbbm{1}_{\hat{F}\neq 1})^{\mathfrak{n},0}_{\mathfrak{e}}\in\mathfrak{T}_{\diamond}\}.

Definition B.22
.

We denote by
\mathscr{T}_{1}
the free vector space generated by

\mathfrak{B}(\mathscr{T}_{1})\vcentcolon=\{\tau\in\mathfrak{B}(H^{C}_{1})\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}(F,0)^{\mathfrak{n},0}_{\mathfrak{e}}\in\mathfrak{T}_{-}\text{ for every connected component }(F,\hat{F})^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}_{\mathfrak{e}}\text{ of }\tau\}

and by
\mathscr{T}_{2}
the free vector space generated by
\mathfrak{B}(\mathscr{T}_{2})
, where
\tau\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathscr{T}_{2})

if and only if

\hat{\mathscr{K}}_{2}\big[\mathscr{I}_{k_{1}}(\tau_{1})\cdots\mathscr{I}_{k_{n}}(\tau_{n}){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}^{m}\big]

for
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
and
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n}\in H^{R}_{\circ}
such that

\lvert\mathscr{I}_{k_{j}}(\tau_{j})\rvert_{+}>0
for every
j=1,\ldots,n
.
We denote by
\mathfrak{p}_{1}^{C}:H_{1}^{C}\to\mathscr{T}_{1}
the natural projection.
We note that
\mathscr{T}_{1}
is an algebra under the forest product and
\mathscr{T}_{2}
is an algebra under the tree product.

Proposition B.23

(
[
12
, Proposition 5.35]
)
.

The linear map

\Delta_{1}\vcentcolon=(\mathfrak{p}_{1}^{C}\otimes\mathfrak{p}_{1}^{C})(\mathscr{K}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{K}_{1})\Delta_{1}:\mathscr{T}_{1}\to\mathscr{T}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{T}_{1}

defines a coproduct over the algebra
\mathscr{T}_{1}
(with the forest product as multiplication).
With this coproduct and the counit as in Proposition
B.12
,
\mathscr{T}_{1}
is a Hopf algebra.
Furthermore, the vector space
H^{R}_{\circ}
is a right comodule over
\mathscr{T}_{1}
with coaction

{{}}{}\vcentcolon=(\mathfrak{p}_{1}^{C}\otimes\operatorname{Id})(\mathscr{K}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{K})\Delta_{1}:H^{R}_{\circ}\to\mathscr{T}_{1}\otimes H^{R}_{\circ}.

Remark B.24
.

As shown in
[
12
, Proposition 5.34]
, one can view
\mathscr{T}_{2}
as a Hopf algebra with grade
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{+}

and one can define a coaction
\Delta^{\circ}_{+}:H^{R}_{\circ}\to H^{R}_{\circ}\otimes\mathscr{T}_{2}
, of which we do not need the precise definition here but will only use the recursive formula
[
12
, Proposition 4.17]
.

Definition B.25
.

We set

\mathscr{T}\vcentcolon=H^{R}_{\circ},\quad\mathscr{T}_{+}\vcentcolon=\mathscr{T}_{2},\quad\mathscr{T}_{-}\vcentcolon=\mathscr{T}_{1},

Then, in the language of
[
6
]
,
the pair is a concrete regularity structure and the
pair is a renormalization structure for the generalized PAM.
For
\diamond\in\{-,+\}
, we denote

•

by the coproduct of
\mathscr{T}_{\diamond}
,

•

by the unit of
\mathscr{T}_{\diamond}
,

•

by the counit of
\mathscr{T}_{\diamond}
and

•

by the antipode of
\mathscr{T}_{\diamond}
.

Recall that the product
\mathscr{M}_{-}
of
\mathscr{T}_{-}
is the forest product
while the product
\mathscr{M}_{+}
of
\mathscr{T}_{+}
is the tree product.
We write
\bm{1}\in\mathscr{T}
for
\bullet^{0}
.
For
\diamond\in\{-,+\}
, the Hopf algebra
\mathscr{T}_{\diamond}
is graded with
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{\diamond}
.
The vector space
\mathscr{T}
is graded both with
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{-}
and with
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{+}
.

Definition B.26
.

As shown in
[
12
, Proposition 5.39]
, if

A\vcentcolon=\{\lvert\tau\rvert_{+}\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\tau\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathscr{T})\},

and we denote by
G
the character group of
\mathscr{T}_{+}
,
the triplet
(A,\mathscr{T},G)

is a regularity structure in the sense of
[
33
, Definition 2.1]
.
We have the graded decomposition

\mathscr{T}=\oplus_{\gamma\in A}\mathscr{T}_{\gamma},\quad\mathscr{T}_{\gamma}\vcentcolon=\operatorname{span}\{\tau\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathscr{T})\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\lvert\tau\rvert_{+}=\gamma\}.

We write for the natural projection from
\mathscr{T}
to
\mathscr{T}_{<\beta}\vcentcolon=\oplus_{\gamma<\beta}\mathscr{T}_{\gamma}
.

Definition B.27
.

If
\tau,\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathscr{T})
are such that
\tau\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathscr{T})
, we write

\tau\star\sigma\vcentcolon=\tau\sigma
. We extend the product
\star
bilineary.
Note that the product
\star
is not defined for all pairs
(\tau,\sigma)
.

The following lemma essentially states that the product
\star
is regular in the sense of

[
33
, Definition 4.6]
.

Lemma B.28

(
[
12
, Proposition 3.11]
)
.

If
\tau,\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathscr{T})
are such that
\tau\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathscr{T})
, then

\Delta^{\circ}_{+}(\tau\star\sigma)=\Delta^{\circ}_{+}(\tau)\Delta^{\circ}_{+}(\sigma)
.

Definition B.29
.

Let
V
be the subspace of
\mathscr{T}
generated by

\mathscr{I}(\tau_{1})\cdots\mathscr{I}(\tau_{n}),\qquad\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n}\in\mathscr{T}.

For
i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, we define the linear map
{{}}{}:V\to\mathscr{T}
, called a
derivative
, by

\mathscr{D}_{i}[\mathscr{I}(\tau_{1})\cdots\mathscr{I}(\tau_{n})]=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau_{j})\prod_{k\neq j}\mathscr{I}(\tau_{k}).

Proposition B.30

(
[
12
, Section 6.1]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{i}_{-}:\mathscr{T}_{-}\to H^{R}_{1}
be the natural projection.
Then, there exists a unique algebra morphism
\hat{\mathscr{A}}_{-}:\mathscr{T}_{-}\to H^{R}_{1}
such that

\mathscr{M}_{H_{1}}(\hat{\mathscr{A}}_{-}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\hat{\mathscr{T}}_{-}})(\mathscr{K}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{K}_{1})\Delta_{1}\mathfrak{i}_{-}=\bm{1}^{\prime}_{-}(\cdot)\bm{1}_{H_{1}}\quad\text{on }\mathscr{T}_{-},

where
\mathscr{M}_{H_{1}}
is the product in
H_{1}
.

Definition B.31
.

We call a
negative twisted antipode
.

As for
\hat{\mathscr{A}}_{-}
, we only use the following property.
As shown in
[
12
, Proposition 6.6]
, one has the following recursive formula:

\hat{\mathscr{A}}_{-}\tau=-\mathscr{M}_{H_{1}}(\hat{\mathscr{A}}_{-}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{H^{R}_{1}})(\hat{\Delta}_{-}\tau-\tau\otimes\bm{1}_{-}),

(92)

where
\hat{\Delta}_{-}\vcentcolon=(\mathfrak{p}_{1}^{C}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{H^{R}_{1}})(\mathscr{K}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{K}_{1})\Delta_{1}
.

B.5
Models and modelled distributions

Recall the notion of
models

{{}}{}=(\Pi,\Gamma)
from
[
33
, Definition 2.17]
. In our situation (generalized parabolic Anderson model), the scaling
\mathfrak{s}
is uniform:

\mathfrak{s}=(1,1,\ldots,1)
.
We also need the functional
\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\cdot\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}
and the psuedometric
\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\cdot;\cdot\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}

from
[
33
, (2.16) and (2.17)]
.

An important concept in the theory of regularity structures is the
modelled distribution
(
[
33
, Definition 3.1]
).
We denote by
{{}}{}=\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(\mathscr{Z})

the space of modelled distributions with respect to the model
\mathscr{Z}
whose
images are in
\mathscr{T}_{<\gamma}=\oplus_{\beta<\gamma}\mathscr{T}_{\beta}
.
We set

\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{Z})\vcentcolon=\{f\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{Z})\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}f\mbox{ is }\oplus_{\alpha\leq\beta<\gamma}\mathscr{T}_{\beta}\mbox{-valued}\}.

We will use the norm
\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\cdot\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}
given by
[
33
, (3.1)]
.

Definition B.32
.

Let
\mathscr{Z}
be a model over
\mathscr{T}
and let
\gamma,l>0
. By
[
33
, Theorem 3.10]
, there exists
a unique continuous linear operator
{{}}{}=\mathcal{R}^{\mathscr{Z}}:\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{Z})\to\mathcal{C}^{\min A}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with the following property: there exists a
C=C(\gamma,l,\mathscr{T})>0
such that
for every compact set
\mathfrak{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}

\lvert(\mathcal{R}f-\Pi_{x}f(x))(\phi_{x}^{\lambda})\rvert\leq C\lambda^{\gamma}\lVert\mathscr{Z}\rVert_{\gamma;B(\mathfrak{K},l)}\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert f\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;B(\mathfrak{K},l)},\quad\text{where }\phi^{\lambda}_{x}\vcentcolon=\lambda^{-d}\phi(\lambda^{-1}(\cdot-x)),

(93)

uniformly over
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(B(0,l))
with
\lVert\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 1
,

\lambda\in(0,1)
,
f\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{Z})
and
x\in\mathfrak{K}
.
The operator
\mathcal{R}
is called the
reconstruction operator
.

Proposition B.33

(
[
33
, Theorem 4.7]
)
.

Let
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
be subspaces of
\mathscr{T}
closed under the action of the structure group.
Suppose the product
\tau_{1}\star\tau_{2}
is well-defined for every
\tau_{1}\in V_{1}
and
\tau_{2}\in V_{2}
.
Let
\mathscr{Z}
be a model for
\mathscr{T}
and
let
f_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{\alpha_{i}}^{\gamma_{i}}(V_{i},\mathscr{Z})
for
i=1,2
.
Then, if we set
\gamma\vcentcolon=\min\{\gamma_{1}+\alpha_{2},\gamma_{2}+\alpha_{1}\}
, one has

\mathfrak{p}_{<\gamma}(f_{1}\star f_{2})\in\mathcal{D}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}^{\gamma}(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{Z})
.
Moreover, there exists a constant
C\in(0,\infty)
which depends only on
\mathscr{T}
such that

\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\mathfrak{p}_{<\gamma}(f_{1}\star f_{2})\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}\leq C(1+\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\mathscr{Z}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2};\mathfrak{K}})^{2}\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert f_{1}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma_{1};\mathfrak{K}}\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert f_{2}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma_{2};\mathfrak{K}}\quad\text{for every compact set }\mathfrak{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Definition B.34
.

Let
F\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and let
V
be a subspace of
\{\tau\in\mathscr{T}\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\mathfrak{p}_{<0}\tau=0\}

that is closed under the product
\star
and under the action of the structure group.
We define the map
F^{\star}:V\to V
by

F^{\star}(\tau)\vcentcolon=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{D^{k}F(\bar{\tau})}{k!}(\tau-\bar{\tau})^{\star k},\quad\bar{\tau}\vcentcolon=\mathfrak{p}_{0}\tau.

According to
[
33
, Theorem 4.16]
, if
\gamma>0
and
f\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(V,\mathscr{Z})
, then
one has

F^{\star}_{\gamma}(f)(x)\vcentcolon=\mathfrak{p}_{<\gamma}F^{\star}(f(x))\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(V,\mathscr{Z}).

Furthermore, there exist a constant
C\in(0,\infty)
and an integer
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
which depend only on
\mathscr{T}
,
F
and
\gamma
, such that

\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\mathfrak{p}_{<\gamma}F(f)\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}\leq C(1+\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\mathscr{Z}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}+\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert f\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma:\mathfrak{K}})^{k}\quad\text{for every compact set }\mathfrak{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(94)

B.6
Operations with kernels

Definition B.35
.

A smooth map
K:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\operatorname{supp}K\subseteq B(0,1)
is called an
admissible kernel
if it satisfies

[
33
, Assumption 5.1]
with
K(x,y)\vcentcolon=K(x-y)

and if the following are satisfied:

•

with the notation of
[
33
, Assumption 5.1]
one has the scaling

\mathfrak{s}=(1,1,\ldots,1)
and the regularization
\beta=2
;

•

one has
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}x^{k}K(x)dx=0
for
\lvert k\rvert\leq 1
;

•

the difference
K-G
is smooth on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
G
is the Green’s function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(see Section
2.3
).

The existence of such a kernel is guaranteed by
[
33
, Lemma 5.5]
.

Definition B.36

(
[
33
, Definition 5.9]
)
.

Given an admissible kernel
K
, a model
(\Pi,\Gamma)
for
\mathscr{T}
is said to
realize
K
if
one has

\Pi_{x}\mathscr{I}_{k}\tau=\partial^{k}K\ast\Pi_{x}\tau-\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}:\lvert\tau\rvert_{+}+2-\lvert j\rvert-\lvert k\rvert>0}\frac{(\cdot-x)^{j}}{j!}[\partial^{k+j}K\ast\Pi_{x}\tau](x)

for every
\tau\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathscr{T})
,
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
\lvert k\rvert\leq 1
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The space
\bar{\mathscr{M}}(\mathscr{T},K)
of all
K
-admissible models is endowed with the topology induced by
the collection of pseudometrics
(\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\cdot\,;\cdot\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma,\mathfrak{K}})_{\gamma,\mathfrak{K}}
.
In fact,
the space
\bar{\mathscr{M}}(\mathscr{T},K)
is a complete metric space.

We recall operations of kernels on modelled distributions from
[
33
, Section 5]
.

Definition B.37
.

Let
\mathscr{Z}=(\Pi,\Gamma)
be a model realizing
K
in the sense of
[
33
, Definition 5.9]
.

(a)

We set

\mathcal{J}(x)\tau\vcentcolon=\mathcal{J}^{\mathscr{Z}}(x)\tau\vcentcolon=\sum_{\lvert k\rvert<\lvert\tau\rvert_{+}+2}\frac{X^{k}}{k!}\big[D^{k}K\ast\Pi_{x}\tau(x)\big],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

for
\tau\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathscr{T})
and extend it linearly for
\tau\in\mathscr{T}
.

(b)

Let
\gamma\in(0,\infty)\setminus\mathbb{N}
and
f\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{Z})
. We set

\mathcal{N}f(x)\vcentcolon=\mathcal{N}^{\mathscr{Z}}_{\gamma}f(x)\vcentcolon=\sum_{\lvert k\rvert<\gamma+2}\frac{X^{k}}{k!}D^{k}K\ast(\mathcal{R}^{\mathscr{Z}}f-\Pi_{x}f(x))(x)

(95)

and

\mathcal{K}f(x)\vcentcolon=\mathcal{K}^{\mathscr{Z}}_{\gamma}f(x)\vcentcolon=(\mathscr{I}+\mathcal{J}^{\mathscr{Z}}(x))f(x)+\mathcal{N}^{\mathscr{Z}}_{\gamma}f(x).

(96)

By
[
33
, Theorem 5.12]
,
\mathcal{K}
maps
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{Z})

to
\mathcal{D}^{\gamma+2}(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{Z})
and
one has
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{K}f=K\ast\mathcal{R}f
.
More precisely, one has

\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\mathcal{K}f\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma+2;\mathfrak{K}}\lesssim_{\mathscr{T},\gamma}(1+\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\mathscr{Z}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma+2;B(\mathfrak{K},1)})^{2}\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert f\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;B(\mathfrak{K},1)}.

(97)

uniformly over
\mathscr{Z}\in\mathscr{M}(\mathscr{T},K)
,
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{Z})
and compact sets

\mathfrak{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. See
[
36
, Theorem 5.1]
.

(c)

For a smooth function
F
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\beta\in(0,\infty)
, we set

R_{\beta}F(x)\vcentcolon=\sum_{\lvert k\rvert<\beta}\frac{X^{k}}{k!}D^{k}F(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Then
[
33
, Lemma 2.12]
implies
R_{\beta}F\in\mathcal{D}^{\beta}(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{Z})
.

B.7
BPHZ renormalization

Interesting models can be derived from the realization defined below.

Definition B.38

(
[
12
, Definition 6.9]
)
.

We call a linear map
{{}}{}:\mathscr{T}\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
a (
\zeta
-)
realization
if

\mathbf{\Pi}\bm{1}=1,\quad\mathbf{\Pi}\Xi=\zeta,\quad\mathbf{\Pi}(X^{k}\tau)=x^{k}\mathbf{\Pi}\tau\quad\text{for every }\tau\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathscr{T}).

A realization is called
smooth
if its image is a subset of
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Given an admissible kernel
K
, a realization
\mathbf{\Pi}
is called
K
-admissible
if it additionally satisfies

\mathbf{\Pi}\mathscr{I}_{k}(\tau)=\partial^{k}K\ast\tau\quad\text{for every }\tau\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathscr{T})\text{ and }k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\text{ with }\lvert k\rvert\leq 1.

Definition B.39

(
[
12
, Definition 6.8]
)
.

Let
K
be an admissible kernel.
To a smooth
K
-admissible realization
\mathbf{\Pi}
, one can associate a model

{{}}{}\vcentcolon=(\Pi,\Gamma)

realizing
K
as in
[
12
, Definition 6.8]
.

We denote by the closure in
\bar{\mathscr{M}}(\mathscr{T},K)
of

\{\mathscr{Z}(\mathbf{\Pi})\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\mathbf{\Pi}\text{ is a smooth $K$-admissible realization}\}.

Definition B.40

(
[
12
, Proposition 6.12]
)
.

A
(
K
-)canonical realization

\mathbf{\Pi}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}
for
\xi_{\varepsilon}
is the smooth
K
-admissible

\xi_{\varepsilon}
-realization characterized by the identities

\mathbf{\Pi}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}(\tau\sigma)=\mathbf{\Pi}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}(\tau)\mathbf{\Pi}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}(\sigma),\quad\mathbf{\Pi}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}(\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\tau)=\mathbf{\Pi}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}\tau,

where
\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\tau
is obtained from
\tau=(F,\hat{F})^{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{o}}_{\mathfrak{e}}
by
resetting
\hat{F}(\rho_{\mathscr{R}\tau})=1
and
\mathfrak{o}(\tau)=\alpha
.
We set

\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=(\Pi^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon},\Gamma^{\mathrm{can}})\vcentcolon=\mathscr{Z}(\mathbf{\Pi}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}).

In the situation of our interest, the model
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}
does not converge as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.
To obtain a limit, one has to “twist” the realization
\mathbf{\Pi}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}
.
This operation of twisting is called
renormalization
.
The most natural renormalization is called
the
BPHZ renormalization
, as introduced in
[
12
]
.

Definition B.41

(
[
12
, Theorem 6.16]
)
.

The
BPHZ realization

\mathbf{\Pi}^{\mathrm{BPHZ},\varepsilon}

is a unique
\xi_{\varepsilon}
-realization
characterized by the following properties:

•

\mathbf{\Pi}^{\mathrm{BPHZ},\varepsilon}=(g\otimes\mathbf{\Pi}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon})\Delta^{\circ}_{-}
for some algebraic map
g:\mathscr{T}_{-}\to\mathbb{R}
;

•

For every
\tau\in\mathscr{T}
with
\lvert\tau\rvert_{+}<0
, one has
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{\Pi}^{\mathrm{BPHZ},\varepsilon}\tau(0)]=0
.

We set

\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{BPHZ},\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=(\Pi^{\mathrm{BPHZ},\varepsilon},\Gamma^{\mathrm{BPHZ},\varepsilon})\vcentcolon=\mathscr{Z}(\mathbf{\Pi}^{\mathrm{BPHZ},\varepsilon}).

To solve the generalized parabolic Anderson model (
87
), we need the convergence of
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{BPHZ},\varepsilon}
. More precisely, we assume the following; for a condition under which this assumption is satisfied, we refer to
[
17
, Theorem 2.31]
.

Assumption B.42
.

As
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, the family of models
(\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{BPHZ},\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}
converges to
some model
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}=(\Pi^{\mathrm{BPHZ}},\Gamma^{\mathrm{BPHZ}})
, independent of the mollifier
\rho
, in

\mathscr{M}(\mathscr{T},K)
in probability.
Furthermore, there exists a
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,1)
with the following property.
For every
p\in 2\mathbb{N}
, there exist constants
C_{p}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}\in(0,\infty)
and a map

\bm{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}_{p}:(0,1)\to(0,\infty)
such that
\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\bm{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}_{p}(\varepsilon)=0

and the estimates

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\lvert\langle\Pi_{x}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}\tau,\phi_{x}^{\lambda}\rangle\rvert^{p}]

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}\lambda^{p(\lvert\tau\rvert_{+}+\delta^{\prime})},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\lvert\langle\Pi_{x}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}\tau-\Pi_{x}^{\mathrm{BPHZ},\varepsilon}\tau,\phi_{x}^{\lambda}\rangle\rvert^{p}]

\displaystyle\leq\bm{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{BPHZ}}_{p}(\varepsilon)\lambda^{p(\lvert\tau\rvert_{+}+\delta^{\prime})}

hold for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\lambda\in(0,1)
,
\phi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\lVert\phi\rVert_{C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 1

and with
\operatorname{supp}(\phi)\subseteq B(0,1)
and
\tau=(T,0)^{\mathfrak{n},0}_{\mathfrak{e}}\in\mathcal{T}
with
\lvert\tau\rvert_{+}<0
.
Here we write
\phi^{\lambda}_{x}\vcentcolon=\lambda^{-d}\phi(\lambda^{-1}(\cdot-x))
.

List of symbols from Appendix

Appendix C
Proof of Theorem
3.9

Based on the framework discussed in Appendix
B
, here we provide the details to prove Theorem
3.9
.
First, we prepare some results on the kernel
G_{N}
from Definition
2.10
.
In the rest of this section, we fix an admissible kernel
K
as in Definition
B.35

and we set

H_{N}\vcentcolon=G_{N}-K.

(98)

Lemma C.1
.

For every
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the function
H_{N}
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.

Since the Fourier transform of
G_{N}-G
has a compact support, we observe that

H_{N}=(G_{N}-G)+(G-K)
is smooth. Thus, it comes down to showing that

H_{N}
decays rapidly or equivalently, as
K
is supported on
B(0,1)
, to showing that
G_{N}
decays rapidly.

For
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
, one has

\mathcal{F}[x^{n}\partial^{m}G_{N}](\xi)=(-2\pi\mathrm{i})^{m-n}\partial_{\xi}^{n}[\xi^{m}\lvert\xi\rvert^{-2}(1-\check{\chi})(2^{-N}\xi)].

If
\lvert k\rvert>0
, the function
\partial^{k}\check{\chi}
is smooth and supported on
B(0,2^{N+2}/3)\setminus B(0,3/4)
.
Therefore, with some compactly supported smooth function
R
one has

\partial_{\xi}^{n}[\xi^{m}\lvert\xi\rvert^{-2}(1-\check{\chi})(2^{-N}\xi)]=\partial_{\xi}^{n}[\xi^{m}\lvert\xi\rvert^{-2}]\times(1-\check{\chi})(2^{-N}\xi)+R(\xi).

If
n_{1},\ldots,n_{d}
are sufficiently large, then
\partial^{n}[\xi^{m}\lvert\xi\rvert^{-2}]
is bounded for
\lvert\xi\rvert\geq 3/4
.
This means that
\mathcal{F}[x^{n}\partial^{m}G_{N}](\xi)
is integrable, or equivalently,
x^{n}\partial^{m}G_{N}
is bounded.

∎

Remark C.2
.

Thanks to Lemma
C.1
, the convolution
H_{N}\ast f
is well-defined for
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}

and the distribution
H_{N}\ast f
represents a smooth function.

Definition C.3
.

Suppose that the model
\mathscr{Z}
realizes
K
.
For
\gamma\in(0,\infty)\setminus\mathbb{N}
,

f\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma}(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{Z})
and
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we set

\mathcal{G}_{N}f(x)\vcentcolon=\mathcal{G}_{N,\gamma}^{\mathscr{Z}}f(x)\vcentcolon=\mathcal{K}^{\mathscr{Z}}_{\gamma}f(x)+R_{\gamma+2}[H_{N}\ast\mathcal{R}^{\mathscr{Z}}f](x).

Recalling that
G_{N}=K+H_{N}
from (
98
), one has
\mathcal{R}^{\mathscr{Z}}\mathcal{G}^{\mathscr{Z}}_{N}f=G_{N}\ast\mathcal{R}^{\mathscr{Z}}f
.
For the motivation behind the parameter
N
, see Remark
3.1
.

C.1
Definition of modelled distributions

Here we rigorously implement the strategy outlined in Remark
1.16
, in the framework of Appendix
B
.

Definition C.4
.

We define
\mathcal{T},\mathcal{T}_{-}\subseteq\mathscr{T}
and
\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{T}),\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{T}_{-})\subseteq\mathfrak{B}(\mathscr{T})

as follows.

(a)

For
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathscr{T}
we write “
\nabla\mathscr{I}(\tau_{1})\cdot\nabla\mathscr{I}(\tau_{2})
” for “
\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathscr{I}_{j}(\tau_{1})\mathscr{I}_{j}(\tau_{2})
”.

(b)

We denote by
\mathcal{T}
the smallest subset of
\mathscr{T}
with the following properties:

•

\Xi\in\mathcal{T}
and

•

if
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
, then
\nabla\mathscr{I}(\tau_{1})\cdot\nabla\mathscr{I}(\tau_{2})\in\mathcal{T}
.

Furthermore, we associate
c(\tau)\in\mathbb{N}
to each
\tau\in\mathcal{T}
by setting
c(\Xi)\vcentcolon=1
and by inductively setting for
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathcal{T}

c(\nabla\mathscr{I}(\tau_{1})\cdot\nabla\mathscr{I}(\tau_{2}))\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}2c(\tau_{1})c(\tau_{2})&\text{if }\tau_{1}\neq\tau_{2},\\
c(\tau_{1})c(\tau_{2})&\text{if }\tau_{1}=\tau_{2}.\end{cases}

(c)

One defines
\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{T})\subseteq\mathfrak{B}(\mathscr{T})
as the minimal subset with the following properties:

•

\Xi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{T})
and

•

if
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{T})
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, then
\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau_{1})\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau_{2})\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{T})
.

(d)

We set
\mathcal{T}_{-}\vcentcolon=\{\tau\in\mathcal{T}\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\lvert\tau\rvert_{+}<0\}
and

\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{T}_{-})\vcentcolon=\{\tau\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{T})\nonscript\>:\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\lvert\tau\rvert_{+}<0\}
.

Definition C.5
.

Given a model
\mathscr{Z}
realizing
K
, we associate
\tau^{\mathcal{K}}=\tau^{\mathcal{K},\mathscr{Z}}\in\mathcal{D}^{\gamma_{\tau}}(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{Z})
to each

\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{-}
by setting
\Xi^{\mathcal{K}}\vcentcolon=\Xi
and by inductively setting

\gamma_{\tau}\vcentcolon=\min\{\gamma_{\tau_{1}}+1+\lvert\tau_{2}\rvert_{+},\gamma_{\tau_{2}}+1+\lvert\tau_{1}\rvert_{+}\},\quad\tau^{\mathcal{K}}\vcentcolon=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathscr{D}_{i}[\mathcal{K}\tau_{1}^{\mathcal{K}}]\star\mathscr{D}_{i}[\mathcal{K}\tau_{2}^{\mathcal{K}}]

for
\tau=\nabla\mathscr{I}(\tau_{1})\cdot\nabla\mathscr{I}(\tau_{2})
.
The exponent
\gamma_{\Xi}
is chosen so that
\gamma_{\tau}>2
for every
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{-}
.

Proposition C.8
.

Suppose that a model
\mathscr{Z}
realizes
K
. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, one has
\bm{W}_{N}^{\mathscr{Z}}\in\mathcal{D}_{0}^{2}(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{Z})
and
\bm{Y}_{N}^{\mathscr{Z}}\in\mathcal{D}^{\delta}_{-1+\delta}(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{Z})
.
More precisely, there exist constants
\gamma,C\in(0,\infty)
and integers

such that
the following estimates hold uniformly over

N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathscr{Z},\overline{\mathscr{Z}}\in\mathscr{M}(\mathscr{T},K)
and convex compact sets
\mathfrak{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\displaystyle\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\bm{X}^{\mathscr{Z}}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{2;\mathfrak{K}}

\displaystyle\leq C(1+\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\mathscr{Z}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;B(\mathfrak{K},l)})^{k},

\displaystyle\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\bm{W}^{\mathscr{Z}}_{N}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{2;\mathfrak{K}}

\displaystyle\leq C\{(1+\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\mathscr{Z}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;B(\mathfrak{K},l)})^{k}+\lVert H_{N}\ast(\mathcal{R}^{\mathscr{Z}}X^{\mathscr{Z}})\rVert_{C^{2}(\mathfrak{K})}\},

\displaystyle\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\bm{Y}_{N}^{\mathscr{Z}}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\delta;\mathfrak{K}}

\displaystyle\leq C(1+\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\mathscr{Z}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;B(\mathfrak{K},l)}+\lVert H_{N}\ast(\mathcal{R}^{\mathscr{Z}}X^{\mathscr{Z}})\rVert_{C^{2}(\mathfrak{K})})^{k},

and furthermore

\displaystyle\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\bm{X}^{\mathscr{Z}};\bm{X}^{\overline{\mathscr{Z}}}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{2;\mathfrak{K}}

\displaystyle\leq C(1+\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\mathscr{Z}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;B(\mathfrak{K},l)}\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\overline{\mathscr{Z}}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;B(\mathfrak{K},l)})^{k}\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\mathscr{Z};\overline{\mathscr{Z}}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;B(\mathfrak{K},l)},

\displaystyle\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\bm{W}^{\mathscr{Z}}_{N};\bm{W}^{\overline{\mathscr{Z}}}_{N}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{2;\mathfrak{K}}

\displaystyle\leq C(1+\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\mathscr{Z}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;B(\mathfrak{K},l)}+\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\overline{\mathscr{Z}}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;B(\mathfrak{K},l)})^{k}\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\mathscr{Z};\overline{\mathscr{Z}}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;B(\mathfrak{K},l)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\lVert H_{N}\ast(\mathcal{R}^{\mathscr{Z}}X^{\mathscr{Z}}-\mathcal{R}^{\overline{\mathscr{Z}}}X^{\overline{\mathscr{Z}}})\rVert_{C^{2}(\mathfrak{K})}

\displaystyle\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\bm{Y}_{N}^{\mathscr{Z}};\bm{Y}_{N}^{\overline{\mathscr{Z}}}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\delta;\mathfrak{K}}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(1+\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\mathscr{Z}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;B(\mathfrak{K},l)}+\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\overline{\mathscr{Z}}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;B(\mathfrak{K},l)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\lVert H_{N}\ast(\mathcal{R}^{\mathscr{Z}}X^{\mathscr{Z}})\rVert_{C^{2}(\mathfrak{K})}+\lVert H_{N}\ast(\mathcal{R}^{\overline{\mathscr{Z}}}X^{\overline{\mathscr{Z}}})\rVert_{C^{2}(\mathfrak{K})}\Big)^{k}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times(\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\mathscr{Z};\overline{\mathscr{Z}}\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{\gamma;B(\mathfrak{K},l)}+\lVert H_{N}\ast(\mathcal{R}^{\mathscr{Z}}X^{\mathscr{Z}}-\mathcal{R}^{\overline{\mathscr{Z}}}X^{\overline{\mathscr{Z}}})\rVert_{C^{2}(\mathfrak{K})}).

Proof.

The estimate for
\bm{X}^{\mathscr{Z}}
follows from (
99
).
As for the estimate of
\bm{W}_{N}^{\mathscr{Z}}
, the Schauder estimate (
97
)
gives the estimate for
\mathcal{K}\bm{X}^{\mathscr{Z}}
. The estimate for
R_{2}[H_{N}\ast(\mathcal{R}^{\mathscr{Z}}\bm{X}^{\mathscr{Z}})]
follows from
the estimate

\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert\kern-1.07639pt\lvert R_{2}[H_{N}\ast(\mathcal{R}^{\mathscr{Z}}\bm{X}^{\mathscr{Z}})]\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert\kern-1.07639pt\rvert_{2;\mathfrak{K}}\leq\sum_{m:\lvert m\rvert\leq 2}\lVert\partial^{m}[H_{N}\ast(\mathcal{R}^{\mathscr{Z}}\bm{X}^{\mathscr{Z}})]\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{K})},

where the convexity of
\mathfrak{K}
is used.
The estimate for
\bm{Y}_{N}^{\mathscr{Z}}
follows from Proposition
B.33
,
the estimate (
94
) and the Schauder estimate (
97
).

For the estimates of the differences,
the proof is similar. Indeed, we apply difference-analogue of Proposition
B.33
,
the estimate (
94
) and (
97
),
which can be found in
[
33
, Proposition 4.10]
,

[
37
, Proposition 3.11]
and
[
33
, Theorem 5.12]
respectively.
∎

Definition C.9
.

Given a model
\mathscr{Z}

Lemma C.10
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. To simplify notation, we write

X^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=X^{\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}}
here for instance.
Then, one has the following identity:

\lvert\nabla W_{N}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}\rvert^{2}+\Delta W_{N}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}=-\xi_{\varepsilon}\\
+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{-},\\
\lvert\tau_{1}\rvert_{+}+\lvert\tau_{2}\rvert_{+}>-2\end{subarray}}c(\tau_{1})c(\tau_{2})\nabla(K\ast\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}\tau_{1}^{\mathcal{K},\mathrm{can},\varepsilon})\cdot\nabla(K\ast\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}\tau_{2}^{\mathcal{K},\mathrm{can},\varepsilon})\\
+2\nabla[K\ast X^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}]\cdot\nabla[H_{N}\ast(X^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon})]+\lvert\nabla[H_{N}\ast(X^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon})]\rvert^{2}+[\Delta(G_{N}-G)]\ast X^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}.

Proof.

One has

W_{N}^{\mathrm{can}}=K\ast X^{\mathrm{can}}+H_{N}\ast X^{\mathrm{can}}

and

\lvert\nabla W_{N}^{\mathrm{can}}\rvert^{2}=\sum_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{-}}c(\tau_{1})c(\tau_{2})\nabla[K\ast\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{can}}\tau_{1}^{\mathcal{K},\mathrm{can}}]\cdot\nabla[K\ast\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{can}}\tau_{2}^{\mathcal{K},\mathrm{can}}]\\
+2\nabla[K\ast X^{\mathrm{can}}]\cdot\nabla[H_{N}\ast X^{\mathrm{can}}]+\lvert\nabla H_{N}\ast X^{\mathrm{can}}\rvert^{2}.

Furthermore,

\Delta W_{N}^{\mathrm{can}}=-\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{-}}c(\tau)\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{can}}\tau^{\mathcal{K},\mathrm{can}}+[\Delta(G_{N}-G)]\ast X^{\mathrm{can}}.

Now it remains to observe

\sum_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{-}}c(\tau_{1})c(\tau_{2})\nabla[K\ast\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{can}}\tau_{1}^{\mathcal{K},\mathrm{can}}]\cdot\nabla[K\ast\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{can}}\tau_{2}^{\mathcal{K},\mathrm{can}}]-\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{-}}c(\tau)\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{can}}\tau^{\mathcal{K},\mathrm{can}}\\
=-\xi_{\varepsilon}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{-},\\
\lvert\tau_{1}\rvert_{+}+\lvert\tau_{2}\rvert_{+}>-2\end{subarray}}c(\tau_{1})c(\tau_{2})\nabla(K\ast\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{can}}\tau_{1}^{\mathcal{K},\mathrm{can}})\cdot\nabla(K\ast\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{can}}\tau_{2}^{\mathcal{K},\mathrm{can}}).\qed

C.2
BPHZ renormalization for
\bm{X}

The goal of this section is to show
X^{\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{BPHZ},\varepsilon}}=X^{\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{can},\varepsilon}}-c_{\varepsilon}

(Proposition
C.18
). To this end, our first goal is to obtain
the basis expansion for modelled distributions

\tau^{\mathcal{K},\mathscr{Z}}\in\mathcal{T}_{-}
, which will be given in Lemma
C.13
.

Lemma C.11
.

For every
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{-}
with
\lvert\tau_{1}\rvert_{+},\lvert\tau_{2}\rvert_{+}<-1
and
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, one has

\Delta^{\circ}_{+}[\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau_{1})]=\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau_{1})\otimes\bm{1}_{+},\quad\Delta^{\circ}_{+}[\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau_{1})\mathscr{I}_{j}(\tau_{2})]=[\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau_{1})\mathscr{I}_{j}(\tau_{2})]\otimes\bm{1}_{+}.

In particular, the constant map
x\mapsto\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau_{1})\mathscr{I}_{j}(\tau_{2})

belongs to
\mathcal{D}^{\infty}_{\lvert\tau_{1}\rvert+\lvert\tau_{2}\rvert+2}(\mathscr{T},\mathscr{Z})
for any model
\mathscr{Z}=(\Pi,\Gamma)

and

\mathcal{R}[\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau_{1})\mathscr{I}_{j}(\tau_{2})]=\Pi_{x}[\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau_{1})\mathscr{I}_{j}(\tau_{2})],

where the right-hand side is independent of
x
.

Proof.

In view of the recursive formula

[
12
, Proposition 4.17]
, one can prove the claim by induction on
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{+}
.
Indeed, suppose one is going to prove
\Delta^{\circ}_{+}\tau=\tau\otimes\bm{1}_{+}
,
where
\tau=\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau_{1})\mathscr{I}_{j}(\tau_{2})
and
\Delta^{\circ}_{+}\tau_{k}=\tau_{k}\otimes\bm{1}_{+}
.
By Lemma
B.28
,

\Delta^{\circ}_{+}\tau=\Delta^{\circ}_{+}[\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau_{1})]\Delta^{\circ}_{+}[\mathscr{I}_{j}(\tau_{2})]
.
Therefore, it suffices to show

\Delta^{\circ}_{+}[\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau_{1})]=[\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau_{1})]\otimes\bm{1}_{+}
.
By
[
12
, Proposition 4.17]
, one has

\Delta^{\circ}_{+}\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau_{1})=(\mathscr{I}_{i}\otimes\operatorname{Id})\Delta\tau_{1}+\sum_{k:\lvert\tau\rvert_{+}+1-\lvert k\rvert>0}\frac{X^{k}}{k!}\otimes\hat{\mathscr{I}}_{e_{i}+k}(\tau_{1}).

It remains to observe that
(\mathscr{I}_{i}\otimes\operatorname{Id})\Delta\tau_{1}=[\mathscr{I}_{i}(\tau_{1})]\otimes\bm{1}_{+}
by hypothesis of the induction
and that the set over which
k
ranges is empty.
∎

Definition C.12
.

We use some notations from Section
B.1
.
Let
\tau\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{T})
and let
e
be an edge of
\tau
with
\mathfrak{t}(e)=\mathscr{I}
.
By removing the edge
e
, we
obtain a decorated forest with two connected components. We denote by

\operatorname{Remove}(\tau;e)

the component containing the root of
\tau
, with decoration inherited from
\tau
